\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
We train a single Lorentz-equivariant transformer to regress tree- and loop-level
scattering amplitudes across many processes ($ee\!\to\!WW$, $ee\!\to\!t\bar t$,
$ee\!\to\!u\bar u(g)$, $ee\!\to\!\gamma\gamma(\gamma)$, \dots) jointly, as a step
toward a reusable ``foundation'' model that transfers to new processes and
perturbative orders. This note fixes precise definitions for the regression target
and its preprocessing, the equivariant architecture and the physical particle
encoding, the joint loss, and the multi-fidelity (\textsc{DyHPO}) hyperparameter
search, and then reports the empirical results with their governing quantities. The
settled results are: (i) amplitude preprocessing must be resolved \emph{per process},
which lowers the median relative error by $\sim\!6\times$ and de-sensitizes the whole
sweep; (ii) under maximal-update parameterization ($\mu$P) the optimal learning rate
\emph{transfers across model width} but obeys a strong \emph{inverted-$U$ law in
training length}, peaking at an absolute optimizer time scale $\tstar\!\approx\!3\times10^3$
steps independent of dataset size, which rewrites how we set and search learning
rates; (iii) a family of physics-motivated input features (coupling order, internal
propagator mass, off-shellness, \dots) helps exactly when the added quantity is
\emph{not recoverable from the external momenta}, and is neutral otherwise; (iv) among
the three maintained $\mu$P architectures LLoCa is both the cheapest ($\sim\!20\times$ fewer
FLOPs/step than L-GATr) and the most accurate at matched compute \emph{and} matched wall-time,
and fine-tuning a foundation model onto one-loop targets withheld from pretraining transfers
with the same
scaling exponent but a strictly lower floor and no plateau, holding for both a foundation that
contains the targets' tree-level counterparts and one that excludes the targets entirely, with
full per-layer-decay retraining
the best method; and (v) a per-step optimization campaign took the training step from
$0.356$ to $0.128$\,s (data-bound fix) down onto a compute floor that vectorization and
a fused optimizer lowered by a further $\sim\!3\times$, every numerical fast path proven
equivalent to the original. We close with the methodological principles these results
impose on how sweeps and HPO are run.
\end{abstract}

\tableofcontents

% ==========================================================================
\section{The system, target, and notation}\label{sec:system}
% ==========================================================================

\paragraph{Scattering amplitudes.} For a fixed partonic process the squared matrix
element $\amp$ is a function of the external four-momenta $\{p_i\}_{i=1}^{n}$ (with
$p_i\in\R^{4}$, metric $(+,-,-,-)$) that determines the differential cross section. It
is the object a Monte-Carlo event generator evaluates once per phase-space point; our
aim is to \emph{learn} it, so that a trained network replaces the per-point matrix-element
call. We treat the problem as regression of a single scalar, $\amp$, from the event's
particle set.

\paragraph{Data.} Each dataset is one process, stored as rows of flat four-momenta
($4n$ numbers), the $n$ PDG identifiers, and the scalar $\amp$ in the last column.
Events are generated over a range of centre-of-mass energy $\sqrt s$. The maintained
joint set spans eight base processes ($ee\!\to\!WWZ,\,WW,\,t\bar t,\,u\bar ug,\,
u\bar ugg,\,\gamma\gamma,\,\gamma\gamma\gamma,\,u\bar u$) and, in the ``big-run'' recipes,
of order $10^{2}$ datasets once the coupling-, mass-, and order-scans are included. A
\emph{process} (or \emph{dataset}) index $p\in\{1,\dots,P\}$ labels which physical
process an event belongs to; the model is trained on all of them at once.

\paragraph{Notation.} We write $n$ for the number of particles in an event, $\nh$ for the
number of attention heads (the $\mu$P \emph{width} axis, with transformer hidden dimension
$d=16\,\nh$), $D\equiv n_{\mathrm{train}}$ for the number of training events, and
$t\equiv t_{\mathrm{steps}}$ for the number of optimizer steps (the training horizon /
\emph{fidelity}). $\eta$ denotes the learning rate \code{training.lr}. A hat marks a
model prediction. All logarithms in scaling exponents are base-10 unless noted.

% ==========================================================================
\section{The regression target and its preprocessing}\label{sec:target}
% ==========================================================================

Raw $\amp$ spans many orders of magnitude (down to $\sim\!10^{-13}$) and can be
negative at one loop (interference); it must be compressed and standardized before it
is a sane regression target.

\begin{definition}[Amplitude transform]\label{def:trafo}
The target is the preprocessed amplitude $y = (s\circ f)(\amp)$, a \emph{log} (or
\emph{signed-log}) compression followed by an affine \emph{standardization}:
\begin{equation}
  f(a) =
  \begin{cases}
    \ln a, & \text{$a>0$ throughout (positive-definite $\amp$),}\\[2pt]
    \operatorname{sgn}(a)\,\ln\!\bigl(1+\abs a\bigr), & \text{$a$ genuinely signed (loop interference),}
  \end{cases}
  \qquad
  s(x) = \frac{x-\mu}{\sigma},
  \label{eq:trafo}
\end{equation}
with $(\mu,\sigma)$ estimated on the training split only. The choice log vs.\ signed-log
is made by \code{resolve\_amp\_trafos} from the sign content of the data.
\end{definition}

\begin{principle}[Per-process preprocessing]\label{prin:perproc}
The transform and its $(\mu,\sigma)$ are resolved \textbf{per process}, not once
globally. This is the validated default (\code{preprocess\_per\_dataset: true}).
\end{principle}

\noindent The reason is quantitative and was a genuine bug fix:
signed-log only compresses $\abs a\!\gg\!1$, but every $\amp\!\ll\!1$, so a single
\emph{global} signed-log, forced on all processes by one loop-level dataset with
negative entries, leaves the targets in \emph{linear} scale (target $\sigma$ ranging
$8$--$30$, up to $1.2\times10^{4}$ for one process), whereupon a shared standardization
cannot reach unit variance and most processes simply fail to train. Resolving the
transform per process ($416$ log / $32$ signed-log across the $448$-dataset big run)
collapses the global preprocessed $\sigma$ from $282$ to $1.06$.

% ==========================================================================
\paragraph{Beam labelling of the stored events (found and fixed, September 2026).}
Every stored event lists the two beams first, with the beam-$-$ (the $e^-$, or the first
quark) along $+z$ in row 0. MadGraph orders the legs as the \code{generate} string does,
i.e.\ \code{e+ e- > ...} puts the $e^+$ in slot 0. The two labelling backends disagreed on
this: matrix2py/MadLoop swapped the beams (correct for $e^+e^-$), while the default C++
driver fed the rows unpermuted, so \emph{every LO dataset built on the C++ path carried
the $\theta\to\pi-\theta$ image of its own beam labels}. Measured on the compiled
$e^+e^-\to u\bar u$ standalone at $\sqrt s=200$\,GeV, $|\cos\theta|=0.6$: the two beam
orders give $4.7\times10^{-2}$ and $5.4\times10^{-3}$, and the physical one (up quark
forward along the $e^-$, $A_{FB}>0$ above the $Z$) is the one the old driver did
\emph{not} store. Consequences: every LO-only result in these notes is unaffected, since
a consistent relabelling is a bijection of the input space and the models learned the
mirrored function equally well; forward--backward statements read off LO data would be
sign-flipped; and any training set mixing LO with NLO-virt data (\code{pretrain25\_nlo},
the LO$\to$NLO map) paired the two conventions on forward--backward-asymmetric
processes, which caps what such a set could have taught. The fix
(\code{mg5\_pipeline\_final.row\_to\_slot\_perm}) derives the row$\to$slot permutation
from \code{pdg\_ids} against the generate string and is used by both backends and the NLO
pipeline; the C++ wrapper now also sets $\alpha_s$ per event inside the matrix element
(exact for mixed QCD$+$EW amplitudes, identical to the previous analytic rescale for
pure $\alpha_s^k$ ones); recipe identities carry \code{label\_convention=mg5\_slot\_v2} so
no pre-fix cache is ever reused; \code{tests/test\_beam\_order.py} guards the catalog and
the sign of $A_{FB}$ on the real driver. All data generated on CC-IN2P3 is post-fix.

\paragraph{Heavy-quark masses: one convention (September 2026).} The \code{sm} and
\code{loop\_sm} models share their default parameter card ($m_t=173.0$, $m_b=4.7$\,GeV, same
electroweak inputs); the NLO-virt pipeline overrides only $m_t\to172.5$ (and \code{ymt}) to
match the collaborators' $t\bar t$ reference (\code{tools/nlo\_conventions.py}), and the LO
catalog never received that override. Every catalog entry with a top now carries the same
patch and samples at \code{LOCKED\_MT}$=172.5$; $m_b$ stays the model default $4.7$ for LO
and virt alike. Two pre-existing inconsistencies of the same kind surfaced while locking
this down and are fixed: the virt table sampled $e^+e^-\to b\bar b$ with $m_b=4.18$ against
a card with $4.7$ (no $b$ reference dataset justified $4.18$), and the LO \code{ee\_wwbb}
entry sampled its $b$ quarks massless against the same card, so both put external $b$
momenta slightly off the mass the matrix element used. \code{tests/test\_beam\_order.py}
now checks every LO entry's sampling masses against the masses its card resolves to.

\section{The process catalog}\label{sec:catalog}
The joint pretraining sets so far were hand-picked: the $8$- and $25$-process sets, and the
$448$-set of Sec.~\ref{sec:bigrun}, which is $25$ bases times parameter scans. For the re-run
on CC-IN2P3 the catalog is enumerated by rule and validated by MadGraph
(\code{recipes/gen\_catalog\_v2.py}; generated entries in
\code{recipes/catalog\_v2\_processes.yaml}, loaded into the pipeline at import; recipes
\code{catalog\_v2\_train.yaml} and \code{catalog\_v2\_holdout.yaml}). Beams are $e^+e^-$,
$u\bar u$, $u\bar d$, $uu$, $ud$, $us$ at parton level, no PDFs; the coupling orders of every
entry (Def.~\ref{def:coup}) are read off MadGraph's own \code{WEIGHTED} line: its default
order is minimal QED, so the line fixes the minimal EW power and hence the maximal $\alpha_s$
power; an explicit \code{QED<=N} raises only the EW maximum, and one that does not enlarge the
diagram set is dropped as a duplicate of the default-order process.

\paragraph{Layers and rules.} The training recipe holds $400$ processes: $302$ trees ($35$ at
$2\to2$, $82$ at $2\to3$, $185$ at $2\to4$), $95$ one-loop QCD interferences ($29$ at $2\to2$,
$66$ at $2\to3$) and $3$ loop-induced amplitudes.
\begin{itemize}
  \item $2\to2$: enumerated exhaustively and reasoned one by one. Out: loop-induced,
    Yukawa-suppressed (massless $e$), $2\to1$, and charge- or colour-violating combinations.
    Masslessness relabels ($s\bar s$, $c\bar c$, $\nu_\tau$) and pure crossings
    ($u\bar u\to\mu\mu$, $u\bar u\to\gamma\gamma$) are hold-outs, not training entries.
  \item $2\to3$: every $2\to2$ base plus one leg wherever it attaches ($+g$ on coloured,
    $+\gamma$ on all, $+Z$/$+H$ on bosonic and heavy-quark finals), plus the $W$-emission and
    fusion processes ($e^-\bar\nu W^+$, $\nu\bar\nu H$, $e^+e^-H$, $ZHH$, $t\bar tH$, $WWH$).
  \item $2\to4$: every $2\to3$ plus $g$ or $\gamma$; all four-fermion finals (pair $\times$
    pair, identical flavours included); the multi-boson finals carrying the quartic vertices
    ($WWZZ$, $WWWW$, $WW\gamma\gamma$, $WWZH$, $ZZHH$, \dots); $\nu\bar\nu + XY$ fusion.
  \item One-loop QCD interference ($\alpha_s$-stripped, Def.~\ref{def:coup}): every coloured
    tree at $2\to2$ and $2\to3$; at $2\to4$ only the hand-written handful.
  \item Loop-induced: the candidates reachable from these beams, admitted one by one on
    certification (below).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Validation.} Of $267$ tree candidates the rules produce, $265$ exist in MadGraph
$3.7.0$; $e^+e^-\to\gamma\gamma H$ (and its $+\gamma$) has no tree. A one-loop backend enters
the catalog only with an explicit PASS from the pole certifier (a crashed checker used to
count as a pass, which had certified modules the environment could not even import; the
verdict now requires the PASS line). The $20$ hand-written one-loop entries certify: the
$e^+e^-\to q\bar q$ interferences on both poles, the quark-initiated ones on the double pole
(coloured beams enter $-\sum_i C_i$; no closed-form single pole), the $2\to3$ ones on the
double pole with $n$-body phase space ($-(2C_F+C_A)$ for $q\bar q g$, $-C_A$ for $Q\bar Q g$).
Of the loop-induced candidates, $e^+e^-\to HH$, $u\bar u\to Hg$ and $u\bar u\to HH$ certify
(poles exactly $0$; $u\bar u\to Hg$ scales as exactly $2^3$ when $\alpha_s$ doubles, the
$HH$ ones are pure-EW top loops with no $\alpha_s$ dependence). $e^+e^-\to gg$ vanishes
(Furry / Landau--Yang): MadLoop returns $|\mathcal M|^2\sim10^{-37}$ with an inconsistent
$\alpha_s$ scaling, i.e.\ noise. $e^+e^-\to\gamma H$ and $u\bar u\to H\gamma$ return a
single pole of $15$--$20\%$ of the finite part at every point although no tree exists, while
the $HH$ finals in the same model give exactly zero: an $H\gamma$-final pathology of the tool,
and both stay out.

The one-loop entry of $u\bar u\to t\bar t$ now loops the same mixed (\code{QED<=2}) born as its tree; it
used to loop the pure-QCD born, so its per-process loss is not comparable with any earlier run.

\paragraph{Certifying the rule-generated one-loop set.} The whole one-loop table ($104$ MadLoop
modules: the $20$ hand-written, the $82$ by rule, the two loop-induced candidates) was built and
certified in one CPU job ($16$ cores, $16$ minutes). The first pass reported $15$ failures, and
$11$ of them were the checker's, not the backend's: the single-pole closed forms assumed the
coloured pair carried all of $s$, so every $e^+e^-\to q\bar q+\gamma/Z/H$ entry was compared with
the wrong number. Both forms are now evaluated on the pair: the massless one carries
$-\sum_i C_i\ln(\mu^2/s_{q\bar q})$ next to $-\sum_i\gamma_i$, the massive one takes $\beta$ from
$s_{Q\bar Q}$ (the eikonal depends only on the pair's relative velocity); with that every such
entry certifies to $10^{-8}$ or better. The remaining $t\bar t+X$ and $t\bar b+X$ misses
($10^{-2}$ on the massive single pole of $e^+e^-\to t\bar t\gamma$, $10^{-3}$--$10^{-2}$ on the
double pole of $u\bar u\to t\bar tg$) are a width effect: MadLoop keeps the card $\Gamma_t$ in
the tree-like top propagators of the loop diagrams while the loop integrals cannot carry it, so
the poles miss the universal prediction at $O(\Gamma_t/m_t)$ where the emitted boson is soft;
with \code{DECAY 6} set to zero they agree to $10^{-8}$. The certifier therefore runs at zero top
width (the card is restored afterwards); the data keep the card width, which is what MadGraph
computes without the complex-mass scheme. The checker also keeps MadLoop's return code per
point (an exceptional point is excluded from the verdict, more than $10\%$ of them is a FAIL)
and uses a relative $10^{-3}$ on both poles. The last two failures, $us\to us$ and $ds\to ds$,
were the checker's orientation: it sampled its own $2$-body points with MadGraph's slot~$1$
along $-z$, which is what the generator hands MadLoop for the $e^+e^-$ family (stored row $0$,
$e^-$, lies along $+z$ and the \code{e+ e-} string puts $e^+$ in slot~$1$) but the mirror of
what it hands the four-quark modules, whose slot~$1$ is row $0$ itself. In that orientation those two modules return a double pole
anywhere between $0$ and $-16/3$ on about a third of the points, all flagged stable, and
evaluating the same point twice in one process can return a different born; a fresh
regeneration reproduces it, the other four-quark channels are insensitive, and a pure-QCD
born (\code{QED=0}) is exact either way. With the first beam along $+z$ both modules are exact
and repeatable on every point ($10^{-13}$ on the pole, identical borns on three evaluations of
each point), and $300$ generated events carry the right pole on all of them. The checker now samples in
the stored convention and applies the generator's own row-to-slot permutation before calling
MadLoop, so every module is certified on the momenta it sees in generation, and all $82$
rule-generated one-loop processes are in. Because MadLoop can hand back a wrong point flagged
stable, the generator also checks the double pole of every event against the universal value
and redraws the event when it is off (or when the return code marks the point exceptional),
and refuses the module if more than $2\%$ of its events had to be redrawn.

\paragraph{Sampling policy.} The recipe path now samples the mixture the divergence study
settled on (Sec.~\ref{sec:div-sampling}): $(1-f)$ of a pool drawn the legacy way ($\sqrt s$
uniform in the window, RAMBO angles, fiducial cuts; this is a dataset convention, not a
physical measure, and the notes called it one until now) and $f=0.35$ kept flat in
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$ from an oversampled ($6\times$), labelled candidate pool thinned without
replacement, so every amplitude extreme is covered by generation rather than by resampling.
Candidates reach the extremes through the IR-democratic $1\to2$ splitter for multi-leg finals
(half of the multi-leg candidates) and an adaptive $\sqrt s$ proposal: a first candidate pass is
histogrammed in $\log|\mathcal M|^2$, each candidate weighted by the inverse density of its value,
and the second pass draws $\sqrt s$ where those rare values live. Nothing reads a resonance mass;
a level criterion (the spread of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ within a $\sqrt s$ bin) was tried first and
does not find a pole, which raises the level but not the spread. On $e^+e^-\to u\bar u$ over
$[25,1000]$\,GeV the $Z$-pole share of a pool goes from $0.7\%$ (flat) to $10\%$ (the flat
subset alone sits at ${\sim}27\%$, against $32\%$ for the study's pole-covering proposal), the
max/min bin ratio of the $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ histogram from $66$ to $12$, the bulk keeps $56\%$
of the events, and a seed reproduces the pool (\code{tests/test\_sampling.py}). The policy lives
on the catalog entry (per entry or per recipe; the default is the legacy flat sampling and
\code{catalog\_v2} opts in), enters the recipe identity, and a shaped pool carries its own file
name. Until the first catalog\_v2 short tests the frozen val/test sets were forced onto the
uniform-$\sqrt s$ measure while the train pool was shaped, so the model was scored on a
distribution it never trained on; the train/test histograms of an $s$-channel pool made this
plain ($10$--$15\%$ of train events on the $Z$ pole against $2.6\%$ in test, a low tail only
in train). That was wrong. A fit from finitely many points carries an error field set by the
local sampling density, the training loss is that error integrated over the training measure,
and a validation set estimates that risk without bias only if it is drawn from the same
measure; the old validation estimated a different integral, weighted exactly where the
training density was not, and checkpoint selection, the HPO objective and the census all read
it. A shaped entry now shapes train, val and test alike, and every catalog\_v2 number from
here on is on the shaped measure; the earlier ones (Sec.~\ref{sec:catv2-census},
Table~\ref{tab:catv2_short}) are on the uniform one and compare with the later ones only in rank, not in value. The
one-loop (MadLoop) datasets are never shaped, so their roles always agreed.

\paragraph{Parameter axis.} Deliberately sparse: a few far-apart points per axis as decorated
variants of the families the axis moves, never a dense grid (\code{recipes/catalog\_v2\_scan.yaml};
\code{catalog\_v2\_train\_scan.yaml} is the base plus the $78$ variants): $\alpha_s(M_Z)\in\{0.09,
0.15\}$ on the $23$ hand-reasoned trees with an $\alpha_s$ power up to $2\to3$ (the backend is
shared, $\alpha_s$ enters the matrix element per event), $m_t\in\{150,195\}$\,GeV on the six
top-family entries (the threshold moves with it and the Yukawa follows the pole mass), and
$M_Z\in\{80,105\}$\,GeV on the ten $s$-channel fermion-pair entries (the needle moves). The
hold-outs on this axis are \emph{interpolation} points between the far points and the base
($\alpha_s=0.13$, $m_t=185$, $M_Z=95$; \code{catalog\_v2\_holdout\_scan.yaml}).

\paragraph{Hold-outs.} Two kinds. The free ones (relabels, crossings; one at every layer) are
covered by any base training. Axis removals need one extra pretraining each, and independent
removals can share a base: drop every $+g$ at $2\to4$ (multiplicity extrapolation), drop one
family's interference (the LO$\to$NLO map), drop one loop-induced entry, and the
vertex/particle/topology removals of the $2\to2$ layer ($ggg$, the Higgs family, the top
family, the pure $t$-channel family, the $WWV$ family).

\paragraph{The catalog of arXiv:2606.23791: $409$ to $467$ distinct processes (2026-10-14).} Its $1501$ tree
processes ($2\to1$ $10$, $2\to2$ $254$, $2\to3$ $1237$; pp partons only) fall into $409$ exact relabelling classes at
MadGraph's default coupling orders, which is what its events contain, and $467$ at all orders, which is what its
diagram files show ($616$ and $708$ without merging CP conjugates). The classes follow the vertices of each process's
diagrams: massless quark flavours are interchangeable on pure-QCD quark lines, only up or down type matters once an
electroweak vertex touches a light quark, and a $W$ on a light quark allows only the generation swap
(\code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/classify2.py}, \code{classes\_default.json}, \code{classes\_allorders.json}; \code{classify2.py}
imports a \code{classify} module that is in neither the repo nor any site, so these counts cannot be regenerated from
trunk, and \code{tools/equal\_processes.py} supersedes it). $96$ of
its $149$ held-out processes are exact relabels of training processes, so ``held out'' in its split is not
``unseen'' in ours, where a probe's whole class is held out (Sec.~\ref{sec:ladder}). Our catalog covers $81$ of the
$467$ classes (\code{overlap.py}, \code{overlap\_ours\_vs\_daniel.json}). No $|\mathcal M|^2$ is public (the Zenodo record
holds momenta only); generating all of it ourselves is $\approx19$ core-h, once a reader of its catalog and massive
$b$ beams ($347$ processes) are built. The earlier rule-based bounds without the diagrams' vertices ($484$ to $779$
classes, $519$ to $711$ under a liberal flavour and charge-conjugation rule, $75$ to $78$ of them ours) are superseded.

\paragraph{Generating it (2026-10-15, D45).} About $10^5$ training events per process (the user: ``For now let's do only
100k.''), with its MadEvent setup and its split as published. Settled by its setup and the user's answers: its default
coupling orders, massive $b$ beams, the $2\to1$ group dropped (``Drop them'': $1491$ processes, $139$ held out), the
hold-out ``Their split, report both'' (all $139$ and the $43$ without a relabel in training), the top mass ``173.0,
theirs''. The pipeline (a reader of its catalog, massive $b$ beams for $347$ processes; commits \code{9bbc825},
\code{77cae08}) was tested end to end, $gg\to gg$ labels against the analytic $|\mathcal M|^2$ to $1.7\times10^{-7}$.
Generated on one $48$-core CC node (FA-ccin2p3-17f91455): $1491$ processes, $0$ failed, a MadEvent phase of $3.6$\,h;
validation and test ($10^4$ each) in \code{\$WORK/datasets}, training ($10^5$) in the \code{\$SCRATCH} cache, diagram
sidecars \code{data/diagrams/dc\_*.diagrams.json} for all $1491$, $33$\,GB in \code{datasets}. A timing job
(FA-ccin2p3-85c90b04) put $10^5$ events at $7.6$ to $30$\,min of wall per process ($b g\to b\bar b b$ $458$\,s, of which
MadEvent $435$; $gg\to ggg$ $1823$\,s, MadEvent $1787$).


\paragraph{catalog\_v3, the census (D98, 2026-10-23).} The new catalog counts equality classes, not process strings
(\code{recipes/gen\_catalog\_v3.py}; \code{analysis/catalog\_v3/}). Stage 1 enumerates every $2\to2$ and $2\to3$ final
multiset of SM particles on every beam ($e^+e^-$, every quark-antiquark and quark-quark pair of $u,d,s,c,b$, every
quark-gluon pair, $gg$), every $2\to3$ plus a gluon or a photon, the four-fermion and multi-boson $2\to4$ finals, under
charge, baryon and lepton-number conservation, and collapses the beam order, the swap of the first two generations (exact in
MadGraph's \code{sm}: massless, Yukawa-free $u,d,s,c,e,\mu$, diagonal CKM) and charge conjugation (exact at $2\to2$; from
$2\to3$ on only up to the parity-odd terms the widths allow): $5192$ candidates. Stage 2, MadGraph on each: $1630$ exist
($123$ at $2\to2$, $507$ at $2\to3$, $1000$ at $2\to4$), and the $12$ BSM processes of the two models the particle table
already encodes (DMsimp's $Z'$, the 2HDM's scalars). Stage 3, $|\mathcal M|^2$ of every tree on shared points under every
leg relabelling (\code{tools/equal\_processes.py}, $32$ minutes on $16$ cores): $344$ classes of exactly equal processes
take out $452$ strings ($\nu_e=\nu_\tau$ finals, $b\bar b\to c\bar c=b\bar b\to d\bar d$ at QCD order, \dots), and $151$
near pairs (log-ratio spread $2\times10^{-6}$ to $10^{-2}$; $d\bar d\to HZZ$ against $u\bar u\to HZZ$, $e^+e^-\to HZZ$:
the fermion line's couplings factor out up to the $Z$ width) take out $75$ more. Distinct SM tree classes: $1103$ (the $12$ BSM
processes apart), by multiplicity and beam:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
 & $e^+e^-$ & quark pairs & quark-gluon & $gg$ & all \\ \hline
$2\to2$ & $9$ & $44$ & $9$ & $3$ & $65$ \\
$2\to3$ & $38$ & $214$ & $55$ & $14$ & $321$ \\
$2\to4$ & $95$ & $489$ & $109$ & $24$ & $717$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Of these, $359$ coloured classes at $2\to2$ and $2\to3$ can carry a one-loop QCD virtual, $51$ of them with a top.

\paragraph{catalog\_v3's pools and levels (2026-10-24).} Generated: stages 1, 2 and 3, $1934$ entries and
$5802$ pools, among them all $167$ one-loop strings (\code{recipes/catalog\_v3\_gen\_stage2.yaml}). Two fixes the
massive $b$ beams needed in generation: MadLoop STOPs on an energy imbalance above $\sim10^{-10}$\,GeV without its
phase-space improvement, and the sampler's events carry up to $2\times10^{-9}$, so the last final is set to the beams
minus the other finals (\code{tools/nlo\_virtual\_pipeline.py}); and $bg\to bW^+W^-$, where an internal top goes on
shell, has its double pole moved by the physical top width by up to $4.4\times10^{-3}$ ($1.2\times10^{-5}$ with the
width zeroed, as it was certified; \code{tools/pole\_width\_diag.py} over the $68$ strings with a $b$ or $t$: the only
one), so its pole guard is $10^{-2}$ against $10^{-3}$ for every other string. No string carries the heavy-quark
mass-scheme shift (the user's call, 2026-10-24). The audit (\code{analysis/catalog\_v3/pool\_audit.py}, every pool) finds them all at their
recipe sizes, no non-finite or zero $|\mathcal M|^2$, momentum conserved to $5\times10^{-11}$ of $\sqrt{\hat s}$, and
train and validation medians of $\log_{10}|\mathcal M|^2$ within $0.07$ validation spreads; $74$ one-loop pools change
sign, no tree pool does. A held-out process's level does not have to be extrapolated (\code{pool\_levels.py},
Fig.~\ref{fig:v3_levels}): at every multiplicity, each of the $54$ H1, $168$ H2 and $237$ H3 tree classes has its
median $\log_{10}|\mathcal M|^2$ inside the range of the $1127$ training classes, at most $0.10$ of their spread from
the nearest one; of the $29$ one-loop H5 pools, four ($b b\to b b$, $bg\to bgg$, $d\bar d\to\gamma\gamma\gamma$,
$gg\to b\bar bg$) lie outside the $121$ training one-loop levels at their $N$, at most $0.6$ of their spread away. So
the zero-shot level is a question of which training levels to read, not of reach.

\begin{figure}[t]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/catalog_v3/figs/pool_levels.pdf}
\caption{catalog\_v3: the median $\log_{10}|\mathcal M|^2$ of each pool's validation events against its particle count,
training classes and each held-out axis side by side at every $N$ (offset horizontally for legibility). (a) Tree classes:
training, H1 new combinations, H2 multiplicity, H3 particle content. (b) One-loop pools: training and H5.}
\label{fig:v3_levels}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{catalog\_v3's short pretrainings, read zero-shot (2026-10-25).} Four arms, one seed each, the same short
setup (\code{scripts/job\_catalog\_v3\_short.sh}: $4264$ steps, five epochs of the $1397$ training pools, the twin $3803$
over its $1246$; the sign head for the sign-changing one-loop pools, the MSE with the arithmetic mean, the diagram
encoder with the BSM layer's labels; $\sim1$ GPU-h each): the \emph{baseline}, one shared standardization of
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$ in GeV; the \emph{dimensionless target} (the emission scales of par.~\ref{par:dimless_qperp},
dimensionless inputs, coupling orders); the same \emph{with the context vector} (par.\ The context vector; read here
at $c=0$); and the dimensionless target trained on the \emph{twin}, without any pool with a $\tau$ or $\nu_\tau$ or
built on a quartic-gauge class. Each read every held-out axis on $2000$ validation events per process
(\code{tools/zero\_shot\_parent.py}; \code{analysis/catalog\_v3/short\_reads.py}, \code{data/short\_reads.json}): the
MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ (for a sign-changing one-loop pool, of $\log\max(|\mathcal M|^2,\text{floor})$, the sign
head's magnitude), split into the level, the squared mean error, which one number per process removes, and the shape,
what is left. Every probe of every axis was read ($617$ per arm, the twin $768$; the twin's $7$ sign-changing one-loop
$\tau$ pools were left out of its first read and read again with the magnitude scoring). The training
losses are on different targets and not compared; these reads all are on $\log|\mathcal M|^2$.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\caption{catalog\_v3's four short arms read zero-shot: the median over each axis's processes of the MSE of
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$ and, in brackets, of its level part. One seed each.}
\label{tab:v3_short}
\small
\begin{tabular}{lrcccc}
\hline
held-out axis & processes & baseline & dimensionless & with context & twin \\
\hline
new combinations (H1) & $54$ & $1.01$ ($0.17$) & $0.84$ ($0.06$) & $0.75$ ($0.06$) & $0.93$ ($0.13$) \\
$2\to4$ families (H2) & $168$ & $1.12$ ($0.20$) & $1.04$ ($0.11$) & $1.08$ ($0.09$) & $1.17$ ($0.11$) \\
the physical top (H3) & $237$ & $1.86$ ($0.48$) & $1.67$ ($0.52$) & $1.46$ ($0.35$) & $1.82$ ($0.55$) \\
one loop (H5) & $30$ & $1.25$ ($0.40$) & $1.12$ ($0.25$) & $1.34$ ($0.31$) & $0.85$ ($0.31$) \\
$\alpha_s=0.13$ (H6) & $8$ & $0.22$ ($0.03$) & $0.14$ ($0.01$) & $0.16$ ($0.03$) & $0.20$ ($0.01$) \\
$p_T\geq30$\,GeV (H7) & $40$ & $0.54$ ($0.03$) & $0.44$ ($0.01$) & $0.42$ ($0.01$) & $0.44$ ($0.01$) \\
$2\to5$ (H2) & $40$ & $46$ ($43$) & $1.21$ ($0.10$) & $1.03$ ($0.09$) & $1.31$ ($0.13$) \\
$\sqrt{\hat s}\in[1,10]$\,TeV (H7) & $40$ & $64$ ($39$) & $2.88$ ($0.34$) & $2.29$ ($0.18$) & $3.12$ ($0.43$) \\
$\tau$, $\nu_\tau$ never seen & $136$ & & & & $4.75$ ($0.67$) \\
quartic vertex never seen & $15$ & & & & $0.68$ ($0.15$) \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

What the table and Fig.~\ref{fig:v3_short} say:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{The dimensionless target extrapolates where the baseline does not} (one seed each). Past the training range the baseline's error is its level
  ($43$ of $46$ at $2\to5$, $39$ of $64$ at $1$ to $10$\,TeV): a level read in GeV has no rule for a new multiplicity or
  energy. The dimensionless target takes both to $1.2$ and $2.9$, better on every one of the $40$ probes of each (median
  per-probe ratio $0.03$ and $0.07$). Within the catalog's range it gains less: median per-probe ratio $0.78$ to $1.02$
  per axis, better on $116$ of the $237$ top probes and on $34$ of the $54$ new combinations.
  \item \emph{The context vector, read at $c=0$, is close to neutral zero-shot}: median per-probe ratio against the same
  target without it $0.91$ (top) to $1.22$ ($\alpha_s=0.13$, $8$ probes), better on $138$ of the $237$ top probes and on
  $13$ of the $30$ one-loop ones. Its use is the $K$-shot fit, read in the paragraph below.
  \item \emph{A particle never seen costs its shape, a vertex never seen does not.} With no $\tau$ or $\nu_\tau$ in
  training, the $136$ processes with one are read at $4.8$, most of it shape ($3.3$): the $\tau$'s mass and
  generation sit in its property vector, and what the network learned from the $e$ and $\mu$ does not carry
  over to it (the other arms trained on these processes, so there is no zero-shot reference for them).
  The $15$ processes built on a quartic-gauge class the twin never saw are read at $0.68$, as well as the new
  combinations. Removing those pools costs the twin a little elsewhere (median per-probe ratio against the full
  catalog $0.91$ to $1.13$ per axis).
  \item \emph{The sign of a one-loop pool.} On the $17$ of the $30$ held-out one-loop pools that change sign
  (\code{tools/signed\_ab\_score.py}), the baseline gets $5\%$ of the signs wrong (median per pool), the dimensionless
  target $18\%$, with it the context vector $19\%$ and the twin $12\%$; near the crossing (within a factor $10$ of zero,
  $7$ to $9\%$ of the events) $29$ to $39\%$ for every arm. Why the dimensionless target loses the sign is not
  measured (one guess: its per-event rescaling moves where the crossing sits in the target); one seed, so this is a
  lead, not a finding.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/catalog_v3/figs/short_reads.pdf}
\caption{catalog\_v3's short arms, per held-out process: the zero-shot MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ of one arm against
another's. (a) The dimensionless target against the baseline; (b) with the context vector (at $c=0$) against without it.
Triangles: the $2\to5$ probes and the classes at $\sqrt{\hat s}\in[1,10]$\,TeV, beyond the training range; circles:
every other held-out process.}
\label{fig:v3_short}
\end{figure}

% allow: M|^2
\paragraph{catalog\_v3's short arms read on pp events (2026-10-25).} The pp study asks whether training on the shaped
$\sqrt{\hat s}$ windows serves a user whose events come from proton beams (the user, 2026-10-25: ``this should be done
in parallel to what we are doing, build it, run a few checks, and from that we decide if we switch or keep what we are
doing''). Each of the $1396$ hadronic tree classes has a $13$\,TeV pool of MadEvent's unweighted pp events carrying the
class's $|\mathcal M|^2$ function as its window pools have it (the same parameter card, $\alpha_s(\sqrt{\hat s})$ run
from $0.118$; \code{recipes/gen\_catalog\_v3\_pp.py}). The four window-trained arms of par.\ catalog\_v3's short
pretrainings read every class on $2000$ validation events (zero-shot for the held-out classes, in distribution for
the $986$ training ones), as on the windows (\code{scripts/job\_catalog\_v3\_pp\_score.sh}: the baseline
(\code{FA-ccin2p3-1b299199}), the dimensionless target (\code{FA-ccin2p3-1c960b22}), with the context vector
(\code{FA-ccin2p3-4b1aabad}), the twin (\code{FA-ccin2p3-81e14672}); \code{analysis/catalog\_v3/pp\_compare.py},
\code{data/pp\_compare.json}, Figs.~\ref{fig:v3_pp}, \ref{fig:v3_pp_arms}); the $\hat s$-only arm (par.\ The shape
proxy's candidate in a network) has no pp read. Held out are the $48$ new combinations, $153$ $2\to4$ families and
$209$ top classes. Every pp pool, its $\alpha_s$ sidecar and its diagram file were checked on CC before the reads
(\code{tools/check\_pp\_pools.py}, no problem found); every class was read by every arm, none failed. Each class is set against the same arm on the same class's window pool: a
held-out class's zero-shot read of Tab.~\ref{tab:v3_short} ($2000$ events), a training class's validation loss in the
arm's own run at its best checkpoint ($200$ events). All numbers are the MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$, medians over
classes, one seed per arm.
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{On pp events every arm reads worse than on the windows, the training classes most.} The median per-class
    % src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/pp_compare.py @fb85838
    % claim: baseline.train.ratio=7.9
    % claim: dimensionless.train.ratio=6.4
    % claim: dimensionless_context.train.ratio=7.1
    % claim: dimensionless_twin.train.ratio=6.6
    % claim: dimensionless_twin.train.n_pairs=875
    % claim: dimensionless.H3.ratio=2.6
    % claim: dimensionless.H1.ratio=5.1
    % claim: dimensionless.train.mse=2.86
    % claim: dimensionless.train.mse_window=0.39
    % claim: dimensionless.train.n_pp_worse=957
    % claim: dimensionless.train.n=986
    ratio of the pp MSE to the window MSE is $7.9$, $6.4$, $7.1$ and $6.6$ on the training classes (baseline,
    dimensionless, with context, twin; the twin on the $875$ it trained on) and $2.6$ to $5.1$ on the held-out axes. The
    dimensionless arm reads its own training classes at $2.86$ on pp against $0.39$ on their windows, worse on pp in
    $957$ of $986$.
  \item \emph{The loss sits where no window event lies.} The window pools are drawn in the partonic rest frame with the
    % src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/pp_compare.py @fb85838
    % claim: support.train.pct_inside=56.5
    % claim: support.H1.pct_inside=48.6
    % claim: support.H2.pct_inside=54.8
    % claim: support.H3.pct_inside=45.6
    % claim: support.train.pct_fail_angle=37
    % claim: support.H2.pct_fail_angle=39
    % claim: support.H1.pct_fail_angle=43
    % claim: support.train.pct_above_window=4.7
    % claim: support.H3.pct_above_window=26
    % claim: support.H2.pct_fail_pair_mass=1.1
    % allow: 10 0.9 0.4 5 because they are the two cut sets' values, settings
    catalog's fiducial cuts on every massless visible final ($p_T>10$\,GeV, $|\cos\theta|<0.9$, $\Delta R>0.4$,
    $m_{ij}>10$\,GeV), and $\sqrt{\hat s}$ stops at the window's top; the pp pools carry MadEvent's lab cuts
    ($p_T>10$\,GeV, $|\eta|<5$) and $\hat s$ up to the beam's. Per class, $56.5\%$ of a training class's pp events pass
    the window's cuts inside its $\sqrt{\hat s}$ window ($48.6\%$, $54.8\%$ and $45.6\%$ on new combinations, $2\to4$
    and the top). The angular cut alone removes $37\%$ ($39$ to $43\%$ held out), a $\sqrt{\hat s}$ above the window's
    top $4.7\%$ ($26\%$ of a top class's events), the pair mass at most $1.1\%$, and $\Delta R$ and $p_T$ nothing.
  \item \emph{Inside what the windows cover, a held-out class reads on pp about as on its window.} On those pp events the
    % src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/pp_compare.py @fb85838
    % claim: dimensionless_twin.H1.ratio_inside=1.09
    % claim: dimensionless_twin.H3.ratio_inside=1.37
    % claim: dimensionless.H1.ratio_inside=1.19
    % claim: dimensionless.H2.ratio_inside=1.12

    % claim: dimensionless.H1.mse_pp_outside=4.67
    % claim: dimensionless.H2.mse_pp_outside=6.05
    % claim: dimensionless.H3.mse_pp_outside=4.01
    % claim: dimensionless.H1.mse_window=0.82
    % claim: dimensionless.H2.mse_window=1.03
    % claim: dimensionless.H3.mse_window=1.56

    % claim: dimensionless_twin.train.ratio_inside=1.56
    % claim: baseline.train.ratio_inside=2.16
    % claim: dimensionless.train.n_inside_worse=795
    % claim: dimensionless.train.n=986
    median per-class ratio to the window MSE is $1.09$ to $1.37$ over the held-out axes and arms
    (Fig.~\ref{fig:v3_pp_arms}; dimensionless:
    $1.19$, $1.12$, $1.28$ on new combinations, $2\to4$ and the top), while the events outside read at $4.67$, $6.05$
    and $4.01$ against window MSEs of $0.82$, $1.03$ and $1.56$. The training classes keep a gap inside: ratio $1.6$
    for the dimensionless arm ($1.56$ to $2.16$ over the arms), above the window in $795$ of $986$. What makes that gap
    is not measured: the pp events inside are distributed differently within the same support, and the window loss is
    the run's own validation, the split its checkpoint was selected on.
  \item \emph{Between the arms}, on pp as on the windows the dimensionless target reads below the baseline on new
    % src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/pp_compare.py @fb85838
    % claim: dimensionless.H1.mse=2.77
    % claim: baseline.H1.mse=4.43
    % claim: dimensionless.H3.mse=3.36
    % claim: baseline.H3.mse=5.17
    % claim: dimensionless.H2.mse=3.72
    % claim: baseline.H2.mse=3.36
    % claim: dimensionless.H2.mse_window=1.03
    % claim: baseline.H2.mse_window=1.09
    % claim: dimensionless.H2.n=153
    % claim: dimensionless_twin.H1.mse=6.23
    % claim: dimensionless_twin.H2.mse=4.49
    combinations ($2.77$ against $4.43$) and the top ($3.36$ against $5.17$). On $2\to4$ it reads below the baseline
    on the windows ($1.03$ against $1.09$ on the same $153$ classes, as in Tab.~\ref{tab:v3_short}) and above it on pp
    ($3.72$ against $3.36$). The twin reads worst on pp on new combinations and $2\to4$ ($6.23$, $4.49$;
    Fig.~\ref{fig:v3_pp_arms}).
\end{itemize}
% src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/pp_compare.py @fb85838
% claim: dimensionless_twin.H1.ratio_inside=1.09
% claim: dimensionless_twin.H3.ratio_inside=1.37
% claim: dimensionless_twin.train.ratio_inside=1.6
% claim: baseline.train.ratio_inside=2.2
So on the held-out axes what a pp user misses is mostly the windows' angular acceptance: inside what the windows cover
a held-out class reads on pp at $1.09$ to $1.37$ times its window MSE, while about two in five pp events lie at
$|\cos\theta|$ beyond the catalog's cut in the partonic frame, where no window pool holds an event, and their loss sets
the pp number. The training classes keep a gap inside the windows too, $1.6$ to $2.2$ times their window MSE over the
arms, which nothing here explains. The comparison measures the difference between the two cut sets as much as between the
two samplers.

    $2.03$ against $1.03$, ratio $1.94$, lower in $22$.
  \item \emph{The top:} on pp $3.79$ against $3.36$, ratio $0.88$, lower in $113$ of $209$; inside the coverage $2.50$
    % src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/pp_compare.py @d1dec67
    % claim: dimensionless_pp.H3.n=209
    % claim: dimensionless_pp.H3.mse=3.79
    % claim: pp_vs.dimensionless.H3.mse.window_trained=3.36

    % claim: dimensionless_pp.H3.mse_pp_inside=2.50

    % claim: pp_vs.dimensionless.H3.window.n_pp_lower=71
    % claim: dimensionless_pp.H3.level=1.85
    against $1.75$, ratio $1.11$; outside $4.24$ against $4.01$, ratio $0.87$; on the window pools $2.18$ against
    $1.56$, ratio $1.24$, lower in $71$. Half of its pp MSE is a constant offset per class (median level $1.85$).
\end{itemize}
% src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/pp_compare.py @d1dec67
% claim: pp_vs.dimensionless_twin.train.mse.ratio=0.14
% claim: pp_vs.dimensionless_context.H2.mse.ratio=0.28
% claim: pp_vs.baseline.H3.mse.ratio=0.78
% claim: pp_vs.dimensionless_twin.H3.mse.ratio=0.95
% claim: pp_vs.dimensionless.H3.window.ratio=1.24
% claim: pp_vs.dimensionless_context.H1.window.ratio=2.42
% claim: pp_vs.dimensionless.train.mse_outside.ratio=0.13
% claim: pp_vs.dimensionless.H2.mse_outside.ratio=0.20
% claim: pp_vs.dimensionless.train.mse_inside.ratio=0.59
% claim: pp_vs.dimensionless.H2.mse_inside.ratio=0.66
% claim: pp_vs.dimensionless.H2.window.ratio=1.94
% claim: pp_vs.dimensionless.H1.window.ratio=2.05
% allow: 6 because it is catalog_v4's approved window top in TeV, a setting
Against every window-trained arm the picture is the same: on the whole pp pool the ratio is $0.14$ to $0.28$ on the
training classes, new combinations and $2\to4$ and $0.78$ to $0.95$ on the top, and on the held-out window pools
$1.24$ to $2.42$. So, off the top, pp training buys most where the windows hold no event (ratio $0.13$ to $0.20$
outside the coverage), less inside it ($0.59$ to $0.66$), and costs about a factor two on the window pools of new
combinations and $2\to4$ ($1.94$, $2.05$). The top, held out whole and so never trained on pp, gains nothing. For
catalog\_v4, whose coverage is approved and validated (par.~\ref{par:v4_validation}): its acceptance in the lab variables, its
$\sqrt{\hat s}$ up to $6$\,TeV and its forward component aim at the events outside catalog\_v3's coverage, where the
largest part of the pp-trained arm's gain lies. What coverage cannot give is the part inside it, the same support
weighted differently, nor does it say whether a window sampler that also covers pp's region keeps its advantage on the
window pools; the catalog\_v4 short pretrainings, read on these pp pools and split the same way, measure both. One seed
per arm, short pretrainings.

pools cover, and on the window pool (held-out groups only). (a) Training classes, (b) new combinations, (c) $2\to4$
families, (d) the top.}

\end{figure}

% src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/coverage_check.py @27e8edd
% claim: n_rows=2014
% claim: n_sampled=1847
% claim: n_read=167
% claim: n_usable=1586
% claim: types.heavy_quark.n=105
% claim: types.bsm.n=12
% claim: types.probe_2to5.n=40
% claim: types.alpha_s.n=24
% claim: n_errors=0
% claim: pp.n=1396
% allow: 10 5 0.4 0.9 13 2000 1 because they are the pp card's and the current pools' cuts, the beam energy, the event count and the angle variable's constant the check is set to, no results
% allow: 0.99 15 6 30 -3 2 because they are the check's own choices, stated as such in the text, no results
% allow: M|^2
\paragraph{The catalog's coverage, a check (2026-10-25).} The pp reading above puts the miss in the angular acceptance,
so the user asked for a CPU check of a new coverage before anything is regenerated (``Yes, go ahead with what you
proposed.'', 2026-10-25: the check only, no regeneration, no GPU). Every one of catalog\_v3's $2014$ entries is in it
(\code{analysis/catalog\_v3/coverage\_check.py}, \code{scripts/job\_coverage\_check.sh}; runs \code{FA-ccin2p3-ec84e913}
for the tree classes and \code{FA-ccin2p3-33503e6c} for the rest; summary \code{data/coverage\_check.json}; $0$ entries
failed). The $1847$ tree pools are sampled three ways with the generator's own sampler and labelled by their compiled
backends, $2000$ events each: the $1586$ tree classes, the $105$ heavy-quark mass points, the $12$ BSM entries, the
$40$ $2\to5$ probes, the $24$ $\alpha_s$ points, the $40$ entries on a $1$ to $10$\,TeV window and the $40$ at
$p_T>30$\,GeV. The three samples are \emph{current}, the pools' cuts and measure; \emph{proposed}, the hadronic
classes cut as the $13$\,TeV pp run card cuts ($p_T>10$\,GeV, $|\eta|<5$, $\Delta R>0.4$, no pair-mass cut), the
$e^+e^-$ classes at $|\cos\theta|<0.99$ with the other cuts as now, $b$ and $\tau$ cut like jets and leptons, and $15\%$
of the events from a forward component (one final particle turned, with the whole final state, to an angle uniform in
$\log 1/(1-|\cos\theta|)$ up to the coverage's edge; nothing in it knows which process has a $t$-channel pole),
$\sqrt{\hat s}$ uniform on the class's window as now; \emph{wide}, the proposed coverage with $\sqrt{\hat s}$ log
uniform from the window's bottom to $6$\,TeV. The $1396$ hadronic tree classes are also read against their pp pools.
The $167$ one-loop and loop-induced pools are read without new samples (the last item but one says what that leaves
out, and why). Medians over a family's classes throughout; the figures show the tree classes
(Figs.~\ref{fig:v3_cov_range}, \ref{fig:v3_cov_pp}, \ref{fig:v3_cov_fwd}). Several ingredients of the proposed
coverage were the check's own choices when it ran, not the user's; each moves some of the numbers below. The user has
since approved the $b$ and $\tau$ cut, the $e^+e^-$ cut, the wide window and a forward component (the conclusion
below); the forward component's two shares and the falling measure stay the check's own, open for the user.
Cutting $b$ and $\tau$ like jets and leptons moves the proposed samples and ranges of the classes with a $b$ or $\tau$,
the $b$ and $\tau$ shares and the pp share inside the proposed coverage. The $e^+e^-$ angular cut at $0.99$ moves the
$e^+e^-$ classes' proposed samples and ranges. The forward component's share ($15\%$ of the events) and the third of
the flat part's candidates turned forward move the forward shares, the forward cover of the pp events, the share
outside the current cuts and the range's growth at the top. The wide window's top at $6$\,TeV moves the wide samples
and their range, the pp shares up to it and the effective sample sizes of the measures to it. The falling
$\hat s^{-3/2}$ measure moves its own effective sample size only. The entries at $p_T>30$\,GeV keep that threshold in
their proposed coverage.
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{The angular cut.} The current cuts and window hold $0.84$, $0.72$ and $0.49$ of a hadronic class's pp
    % src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/coverage_check.py @27e8edd
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.pp_in_cur_window=0.84
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.pp_in_cur_window=0.72
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_in_cur_window=0.49
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.pp_in_new_window=0.94
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.pp_in_new_window=0.99
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_in_new_window=0.92
    % claim: pp.in_new_min=0.99
    % claim: pp.fail_max.cos_max=0.012
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.growth_999_proposed=1.19
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.growth_999_proposed=1.08
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.growth_999_proposed=0.52
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.max_up_proposed=0.74
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.max_up_proposed=1.09
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.max_up_proposed=0.53
    % claim: ee_2to2.growth_999_proposed=0.06
    % claim: ee_2to3.growth_999_proposed=0.14
    % claim: ee_2to4.growth_999_proposed=0.03
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.outside_current_proposed=0.25
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.outside_current_proposed=0.20
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.outside_current_proposed=0.24
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_in_range_new=0.956
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_in_range_cur=0.969
    % allow: 5 0.1 99.9 2000 because they are the eta cut, the quantiles and the event count the check is set to, no results
    % allow: M|^2
    events ($2\to2$, $2\to3$, $2\to4$), the proposed coverage on the same window $0.94$, $0.99$ and $0.92$ (the rest lies
    above the window). The proposed cuts are the pp card's own, so up to the frame change every pp event passes them:
    at least $0.99$ of every class's (the boost to the partonic frame moves at most $0.012$ of a class's events past
    $|\eta|=5$). On the samples, a quarter of the proposed events lie outside the current cuts ($0.25$, $0.20$, $0.24$
    for hadronic $2\to2$, $2\to3$, $2\to4$), and the range of $\log_{10}|\mathcal M|^2$ ($0.1$ to $99.9\%$) widens by
    $1.19$, $1.08$ and $0.52$ decades, almost all of it at the top ($0.74$, $1.09$, $0.53$ on the maximum): the forward
    poles the current cut keeps out. The $e^+e^-$ classes change little ($0.06$, $0.14$, $0.03$ decades). At $2000$
    events the larger region is sampled more thinly: of the $2\to4$ pp events inside the proposed coverage, $0.956$ lie
    within the proposed sample's $|\mathcal M|^2$ range against $0.969$ inside the current one's.
  \item \emph{The $\sqrt{\hat s}$ window and its measure.} A pp event lies above its class's window top in $0.039$ of a
    % src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/coverage_check.py @27e8edd
    % claim: pp.train.above_window=0.039
    % claim: pp.H3.above_window=0.26
    % claim: pp.H3.in_new_window=0.74
    % claim: pp.H3.in_new_wide=1.00
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.pp_ess_uniform_window=0.73
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.pp_ess_uniform_window=0.77
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_ess_uniform_window=0.83
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.pp_ess_log_wide=0.82
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.pp_ess_log_wide=0.86
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_ess_log_wide=0.87
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.pp_ess_lumi_wide=0.91
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_ess_lumi_wide=0.80
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_ess_uniform_wide=0.78
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.growth_999_wide=2.53
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.growth_999_wide=3.18
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.growth_999_wide=3.87
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.min_down_wide=1.74
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.min_down_wide=3.24
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.max_up_wide=1.60
    % allow: 6 because it is the wide window's top in TeV, no result
    % allow: M|^2
    % allow: 2 -3 because they are the powers in the effective sample size's formula and in the measure, no results
    training class's events and $0.26$ of a top class's, so the proposed coverage on the current window holds only
    $0.74$ of a top class's pp events; up to $6$\,TeV it holds $1.00$, and no pp event of any class lies above. For the
    measure, the pp events are reweighted from each candidate, and the effective sample size per event
    $(\sum w)^2/(n\sum w^2)$ is $0.73$, $0.77$, $0.83$ from the current uniform window ($2\to2$, $2\to3$, $2\to4$),
    $0.82$, $0.86$, $0.87$ from log uniform to $6$\,TeV, and from a falling $\hat s^{-3/2}$ to $6$\,TeV $0.91$ on
    $2\to2$ but $0.80$ on $2\to4$ (uniform to $6$\,TeV, $0.78$ on $2\to4$). Log uniform has the best worst case
    across multiplicities. The wide window widens the $\log_{10}|\mathcal M|^2$ range by $2.53$, $3.18$ and $3.87$
    decades, on $2\to3$ and $2\to4$ mostly at the bottom ($1.74$ and $3.24$ on the minimum): the mass dimension of a
    $2\to n$ amplitude squared falls by two per extra final particle, so a higher $\sqrt{\hat s}$ lowers it; on $2\to2$
    it is the top that grows ($1.60$).
  \item \emph{$b$ and $\tau$.} The current cut keys on a massless final, so the $4.7$\,GeV $b$ and the $1.777$\,GeV $\tau$
    % src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/coverage_check.py @27e8edd
    % claim: b_tau.n=481
    % claim: b_tau.no_cut_now=165
    % claim: b_tau.fail_share=0.25
    % claim: b_tau.width_loss=0.67
    % claim: b_tau.fail_share_proposed=0.13
    % claim: b_tau.width_loss_proposed=0.25
    % claim: b_tau.pp_n=430
    % claim: b_tau.pp_in_new_mean=0.999
    % claim: b_tau.pp_in_new_uncut_mean=0.9998
    % allow: 4.7 1.777 because they are the generator's b and tau masses, no results
    % allow: 10 0.9 5 0.99 because they are the current and the proposed cuts, the proposed $e^+e^-$ angle being the check's own choice, no results
    % allow: M|^2
    are never cut: $481$ tree classes have one, $165$ of them no other cut leg and so no cut at all. Under the current
    thresholds ($p_T>10$\,GeV, $|\cos\theta|<0.9$, $\Delta R$ and pair mass as now), a quarter ($0.25$) of such a class's
    current events fail on the $b$ or $\tau$ alone, and they carry $0.67$ decades of its $\log_{10}|\mathcal M|^2$
    range: soft or collinear $b$ and $\tau$ the pools hold and an analysis would not. Under the proposed thresholds
    ($|\eta|<5$ and no pair-mass cut on hadronic classes, $|\cos\theta|<0.99$ and the pair mass as now on $e^+e^-$
    ones), the same two quantities, what the proposed coverage removes from what the pools now hold, are $0.13$ and
    $0.25$ decades. Cutting them costs the pp events almost nothing: on the $430$ such hadronic classes the
    proposed coverage holds a mean $0.999$ of the pp events, against $0.9998$ with $b$ and $\tau$ left uncut.
  \item \emph{The forward component.} The most forward final particle lies beyond $|\cos\theta|=0.9$ in $0.16$, $0.45$
    % src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/coverage_check.py @27e8edd
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.pp_forward_share=0.16
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.pp_forward_share=0.45
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_forward_share=0.62
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.forward_share_current=0
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.forward_share_current=0.13
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.forward_share_current=0.17
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.forward_share_proposed=0.34
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.forward_share_proposed=0.39
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.forward_share_proposed=0.43
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.pp_u_uncovered_mean_current=0.097
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_u_uncovered_mean_current=0.085
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_u_uncovered_mean_proposed=0.003
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.pp_u_ess_current=0.92
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.pp_u_ess_current=0.64
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_u_ess_current=0.36
    % claim: hadronic_2to2.pp_u_ess_proposed=0.80
    % claim: hadronic_2to3.pp_u_ess_proposed=0.82
    % claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_u_ess_proposed=0.76
    % allow: 0.9 because it is the current angular cut, no result
    and $0.62$ of a hadronic class's pp events ($2\to2$, $2\to3$, $2\to4$), in $0$, $0.13$ and $0.17$ of its current
    sample (only massive finals pass there) and in $0.34$, $0.39$ and $0.43$ of the proposed one. A mean $0.097$
    ($2\to2$) and $0.085$ ($2\to4$) of a class's pp events fall at angles the current sample never reaches, $0.003$ or
    less under the proposed one. Reweighting a sample to the pp events' angle distribution leaves an effective size per
    event of $0.92$, $0.64$, $0.36$ for the current samples and $0.80$, $0.82$, $0.76$ for the proposed: the forward
    component costs a little at $2\to2$, where the current cut leaves the pp events little to miss inside its reach, and
    doubles the effective size at $2\to4$.
  \item \emph{The other tree pools.} Sampled as the tree classes, they move the same way, by type (medians; the range's

    % allow: M|^2
    growth in decades, of it at the top, the share of the proposed sample outside the current cuts): the $105$
    heavy-quark mass points $0.27$, $0.16$, $0$ (none of their events lies outside the current cuts; the forward
    component is what changes them); the $12$ BSM entries $0.11$, $0.00$, $0.14$; the $40$ $2\to5$ probes $0.22$,
    $0.32$, $0.31$, and $5.11$ decades on the wide window; the $24$ $\alpha_s$ points $1.74$, $1.72$, $0.29$; the $40$
    entries on a $1$ to $10$\,TeV window $2.54$, $2.81$, $0.33$; the $40$ at $p_T>30$\,GeV $0.53$, $0.70$, $0.21$.
    Against pp, an $\alpha_s$ point reads its base class's pp pool ($21$ of them hadronic): $0.57$ of the events inside
    the current cuts and window, $0.99$ inside the proposed coverage on the window, $1.00$ up to $6$\,TeV (no range
    comparison, the labels are not the base's). The $13$\,TeV pp events rarely reach a $1$ to $10$\,TeV window: on the
    $34$ hadronic such entries $0.0025$ of the base's pp events lie inside the current cuts and window, $0.041$ inside
    the proposed coverage, too few for the pp comparison to say much. The heavy-quark mass points (a top off the pp
    events' shell), the BSM entries and the $2\to5$ probes have no pp pool. A $p_T>30$\,GeV entry reads its base's pp
    events, generated at $p_T>10$\,GeV, against its own cuts ($34$ of them hadronic): $0.071$ inside the current cuts and
    window, $0.11$ inside the proposed coverage (its $p_T>30$\,GeV kept) on the window, $0.18$ up to $6$\,TeV; most of
    the base's events lie below the entry's threshold. Of the events inside, $0.98$ lie inside the current sample's
    $\log_{10}|\mathcal M|^2$ range and $0.97$ inside the proposed one's. The 31M pool events of these entries take
    $7.0$, $6.8$ and $6.6$ CPU-hours under the current, the proposed and the wide coverage.
  \item \emph{The one-loop and loop-induced pools} are read, not sampled. Their labels come from MadLoop, which
    % src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/coverage_check.py @27e8edd
    % claim: loops.one_loop_hadronic.n=143
    % claim: loops.one_loop_ee.n=7
    % claim: loops.loop_induced_hadronic.n=15
    % claim: loops.loop_induced_ee.n=2
    % claim: loops.one_loop_hadronic.pool_width_999=4.47
    % claim: loops.one_loop_ee.pool_width_999=4.94
    % claim: loops.loop_induced_hadronic.pool_width_999=2.41
    % claim: loops.loop_induced_ee.pool_width_999=4.61
    % claim: loops.one_loop_hadronic.pp_in_cur_window=0.77
    % claim: loops.one_loop_hadronic.pp_in_new_window=0.99
    % claim: loops.one_loop_hadronic.pp_in_new_wide=1.00
    % claim: loops.one_loop_hadronic.pp_forward_share=0.31
    % allow: 0.1 99.9 6 0.9 because they are the quantiles, the wide window's top and the current angular cut, no results
    % allow: M|^2
    generation runs one process per pool in an isolated subprocess (MadLoop is a process-wide singleton that changes
    directory), so the check's worker pool cannot label them, and a one-loop event costs far more to label than a tree
    one. So nothing that needs a new labelled sample ran on them: no proposed or wide sample, hence no range growth, no
    share outside the current cuts, no $b$ and $\tau$ shares, no forward share of a sample, no reweighting and no cost.
    What needs no new label is read: the width of the existing validation pool's $\log_{10}|\mathcal M|^2$ ($0.1$ to
    $99.9\%$), $4.47$ decades on the $143$ hadronic one-loop pools, $4.94$ on the $7$ $e^+e^-$ ones, $2.41$ and $4.61$ on
    the $15$ hadronic and $2$ $e^+e^-$ loop-induced ones; and for a hadronic one-loop pool its tree's pp events against
    the loop pool's window, $0.77$ inside the current cuts and window, $0.99$ inside the proposed coverage on the window,
    $1.00$ up to $6$\,TeV, the most forward final beyond $|\cos\theta|=0.9$ in $0.31$ (the pp labels are the tree's, so
    no range comparison). The loop-induced pools have no tree and no pp pool: their range is all that is read.
  \item \emph{The generation cost.} The sampler labels $2.75$ events per kept event under every coverage and draws
    % src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/coverage_check.py @27e8edd
    % claim: cost.current.labelled_per_kept=2.75
    % claim: cost.current.drawn_per_kept=7.20
    % claim: cost.proposed.drawn_per_kept=7.21
    % claim: cost.current.label_time_share=0.97
    % claim: cost.n_pool_events=190M
    % claim: cost.current.cpu_h=31
    % claim: cost.proposed.cpu_h=30
    % claim: cost.wide.cpu_h=30
    $7.20$ against $7.21$, and labelling is $0.97$ of the time, so the new coverage costs what the old one did: at the
    check's rate the 190M events of the tree classes' pools take $31$ CPU-hours under the current coverage, $30$ under
    the proposed and the wide (a difference the check, which runs the three samples one after the other, does not
    resolve).
\end{itemize}
% src: python3 analysis/catalog_v3/coverage_check.py @27e8edd
% claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_in_new_window=0.92
% claim: hadronic_2to4.pp_in_cur_window=0.49
% allow: 6 because it is the wide window's top in TeV, the check's own choice, no result
% allow: 1 3 because they are in the user's words (the items of the proposal), no results
So on the tree classes the proposed coverage holds nine in ten of a class's pp events or more on the current windows
($2\to4$: $0.92$ against $0.49$), all of them up to $6$\,TeV, reaches the forward region the pp events populate, and
costs no more to generate; the other tree pools and the hadronic one-loop pools' kinematics move the same way. On it the
user decided the next catalog's design (``Okay, yes to 1 to 3, yes to electroweak production and the smaller fixes, no
to the decayed final states'', 2026-10-25): the proposed acceptance with $b$ and $\tau$ cut, $\sqrt{\hat s}$ log uniform
from threshold to $6$\,TeV, and a forward component in the mixture, plus electroweak production and the smaller fixes; it is being built as
catalog\_v4 (hand-off), and no catalog\_v3 pool has been regenerated. The forward component's two shares stay the
check's choices.

\paragraph{catalog\_v4's validation and pool checks (2026-10-26).}\label{par:v4_validation} The coverage
decided above, built as catalog\_v4 (\code{mg5\_pipeline\_final.py}, \code{recipes/gen\_catalog\_v4.py}), checked on
CPU before the regeneration (\code{analysis/catalog\_v4/validate\_v4.py}; runs \code{FA-ccin2p3-44e6a15b} and, with
the sampling fix, \code{FA-ccin2p3-2e1a0894}).

The sampled $35$ entries (every layer and beam, the three electroweak test processes, the heavy-quark, $\alpha_s$,
harder-cut and BSM points) built their backends ($12$ to $27$\,s each, mean $20$\,s). The diagram tool reproduces
catalog\_v3's recorded count on all $34$ SM entries; the QED bound is attained wherever it exceeds catalog\_v3's
minimal order ($10$ of $10$); $u\bar u\to d\bar d$ goes from $1$ to $4$ diagrams and $uu\to uuZ$ from $8$ to $24$
(the electroweak exchanges), $ud\to udh$ stays at $2$ (its vector-boson fusion was already the minimal order); the
massless $b$ removes the $b$-Yukawa Higgs diagrams ($b\bar b\to W^+W^-$ $4\to3$, $b\bar b\to gg\tau^+\tau^-Z$
$154\to100$). The cards: MT $=$ ymt $=172.5$ in the $33$ entries with no heavy-quark point, WT $=1.4803$ (the LO
width with a massless $b$), $8.73$ at $m_Q=300$\,GeV and $0$ at $m_Q=50$\,GeV (below $Wb$, the heavy quark is
stable); no $b$ mass or Yukawa in any card. The one-loop layer's $167$ entries are configured as designed, $6$ of
them on \code{loop\_qcd\_qed\_sm-no\_b\_mass}.

\emph{The pools} (the rerun registers each entry with its recipe's sampling block, as the regeneration does; $2000$
events per entry through the pipeline's generation path): all $35$ entries pass every check. Every event passes the
v4 acceptance recomputed independently of the pipeline's mask ($35$ of $35$; $0$ to $0.43$ of each pool lies outside
catalog\_v3's cuts), and every $\sqrt{\hat s}$ lies in its window ($35$). The log-uniform measure: $20000$ draws of the
sampler per entry against log-uniform, KS distance at most $0.0109$ against the $0.1\%$ critical $0.0138$ ($35$
pass). The forward component: the turned particle's $u=\log(1/(1-|\cos\theta|))$ against uniform up to the cuts'
edge, KS at most $0.0126$ ($35$ pass), the drawn $u$ replayed to $3\times10^{-12}$, four-momentum kept to
$6\times10^{-11}$ and every shell to $6\times10^{-16}$ by the turn; in the pools the most forward final lies beyond
catalog\_v3's $|\cos\theta|=0.9$ in $0.19$ to $0.49$ of the events. The shells: beams massless and $b$ finals
massless in all $35$, the tops of the $5$ entries with one on $172.5$ ($m_Q$ at a heavy-quark point), every final on
its mass to $1\times10^{-14}$ relative, four-momentum conserved to $2\times10^{-11}$; every label finite and positive. The
pool itself is not log-uniform in $\sqrt{\hat s}$, nor meant to be (Fig.~\ref{fig:v4_pool_checks}): its per-decade
CDF lies a median $0.112$ (at most $0.21$) from log-uniform, and a median $0.044$ (at most $0.11$) from log-uniform
after the cuts (the bulk and forward parts drawn locally through the same candidate path); the rest is the
$0.35$ flat in $\log|\mathcal M|^2$, which follows the amplitude. The cuts empty the threshold region of the
multi-leg classes: $d\bar d\to d\bar dgg$ has $0.018$ of its pool in its lowest decade ($0.019$ after the cuts,
$0.183$ log-uniform), $e^+e^-\to c\bar cg\nu_e\bar\nu_e$ $0.013$ ($0.018$, $0.227$); the window's lower edge is the
mass threshold, below the cuts' effective one, so those draws are spent on rejection. The cards now read their
model from the standalone ($35$ of $35$ pass the card check), \code{loop\_qcd\_qed\_sm-no\_b\_mass} imports on CC,
and MadGraph generates the $4$ loop strings tried ($4$ of $4$). MadGraph warns on the mixed-order virtuals ($b b\to
bb$ at QED$\le2$): their Born diagrams do not all carry the same QCD power, so the virtual is the QCD correction to
a Born of mixed orders. Not yet in the validation: a MadLoop pool. The one-loop entries' model differs from the
trees' (inherited from catalog\_v3, the loop models' own cards):

% allow: -1 because it is the inverse's exponent, no result
a massless $\tau$ and $\alpha^{-1}=128.93$ against the trees' $\tau$ mass and $132.507$.

\emph{The census} (every catalog\_v3 tree on its v4 string, Fig.~\ref{fig:v4_census}): $2370$ of $2404$ strings
exist; by layer $119/123$, $497/507$, $1715/1734$, $39/40$ ($2\to2$ to $2\to5$), with $213\to287$, $2713\to4024$,
$39384\to70281$, $5016\to8689$ diagrams, $1.76$ times as many overall; $865$ strings gained diagrams, $230$ lost some
(the $b$ Yukawa). The $34$ that vanish are all a Higgs on a $b$ line with nothing else to couple to it
($b\bar b\to h$, $h+\gamma/g$, $hh$, $bg\to bh$, $gg\to b\bar bh$, ...): MadGraph finds no diagram once the $b$ is
massless, so these classes leave the catalog unless the $b$ keeps a Yukawa; the user had them dropped (2026-10-25, ``Drop the b to higgs events.'';
Sec.~\ref{sec:ladder-open}, the regeneration item).
