\paragraph{Pretraining on it (2026-10-16, D59).} The sweep \code{dancat\_het\_pre}
(\code{sweep/sweep\_config\_dancat\_het\_pre.yaml}): the $1352$ training processes of its split ($10^5$ events each,
generated with its MadEvent setup), the user's heteroscedastic loss ranked on the validation NLL, Daniel's diagram
embedding at width $64$ on top of the usual inputs, $32$k steps at batch $16384$ ($12$ events per process per step,
$3.9$ passes), memory-mapped data at $40$\,GB, GP DyHPO over $2000$ candidates, $8$ trials in waves $3+3+2$ on CC
($1.8$ to $5.1$\,h each, $37$ GPU-h in all; Fig.~\ref{fig:dancat_het_pre}, \code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/dancat\_het\_pre.py}).
Best, the per-process pretraining (hp58): validation NLL $-4.10$ (lr $8.9\times10^{-3}$, EMA on with decay $0.94$, warmup $0.16$, L2
$5\times10^{-7}$), the MSE of $\mu$ on the standardized $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ $3.96\times10^{-3}$ on validation and
$3.97\times10^{-3}$ on test; the next, hp1733 at the same lr, $-4.07$ and $4.0\times10^{-3}$. The ranking follows the lr:
the two at $8.9\times10^{-3}$, the top of the window $[10^{-3},9\times10^{-3}]$, lead; $6.9$ and $7.2\times10^{-3}$ reach
$-3.96$; $4.0$ and $4.4\times10^{-3}$ $-3.77$ and $-3.84$; $1.7\times10^{-3}$ $-3.35$. Every trial but one is best at its
last step, still falling. hp1466 (lr $8.8\times10^{-3}$, L2 $4.8\times10^{-8}$, EMA decay $0.98$) blew up after step
$15.4$k (validation NLL $1.9\times10^{6}$, the MSE of $\mu$ $6.5$); its best checkpoint, step $15360$, is at NLL $-2.83$
and MSE $1.4\times10^{-2}$. Since the best lr sits at the top of the window and nothing has converged, a longer horizon or
a window reaching higher lr would likely move the optimum; neither is run. Every trial against each searched HP:
Fig.~\ref{fig:dancat_het_pre_hpo} (\code{dancat\_het\_pre\_hpo.py}); hp58 trained $281$\,min ($4.7$\,h) of the $5.1$\,h
between its submission and its end. Why a catalog step is slower than a rung-9 step (D79;
\code{scripts/job\_step\_profile.sh}, $500$ steps each on one V100, batch $16384$, the data wait against the step's
compute from \code{LLOCA\_PROFILE\_STEP}): hp58's configuration $0.474$\,s/step, rung~9 ($9$ processes, no diagrams,
MSE) $0.360$, so $32\%$ slower. Without the diagram encoder the catalog step is $0.412$; on $19$ catalog processes
with it, $0.394$; rung~9 with the diagram encoder $0.374$, with the het loss $0.361$. The data wait is $2$ to $5$\,ms in
every variant. So the extra $0.11$\,s is compute, about half of it the diagram encoder, which costs $0.062$\,s with
$1352$ processes in a batch and $0.014$ with $9$, and the rest the number of processes per batch; the het loss costs
nothing. Memory-mapped data was not timed (it does not support the sign head rung~9 uses), and need not be: the data
wait is under $1\%$ of the step. The $\sigma$-only stage on hp58 (D70; \code{tools/sigma\_stage.py}, $44$\,min on the full catalog) moves the
validation NLL from $-4.098$ to $-4.102$; its test calibration is not measured yet.

\paragraph{Its held-out split: zero-shot on $43$, in-class on $96$ (2026-10-16, D45).} hp58 scored on each of the $139$ held-out processes with
no data of them (\code{scripts/job\_catalog\_heldout.sh}, \code{tools/zero\_shot\_parent.py}, $0$ errors;
Fig.~\ref{fig:heldout_zero_shot}, \code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/heldout\_zero\_shot.py}). The parent standardizes each
process with its own statistics, so a process it has no data of is scored up to an affine map: $1-\rho^2$, the MSE after
the best $a+bh$ over the variance. Both groups, as agreed (``Their split, report both''): the $96$ held-out processes
that are exact relabels of a training process (in-class transfer, not zero-shot: our convention holds a process out with
its whole relabelling class), median $1-\rho^2=1.2\times10^{-3}$ ($10$ to $90\%$: $1.8\times10^{-4}$ to
$5.1\times10^{-3}$, worst $0.056$), and the $43$ without one, the only zero-shot ones in our sense, median $0.098$ ($5.9\times10^{-3}$ to $0.27$). The worst are
all $gg$-initiated with a top: $gg\to b\bar tW^+$ $0.94$, $gg\to t\bar tZ$ $0.88$, $gg\to t\bar bW^-$ $0.74$,
$gg\to t\bar tH$ $0.50$, $gg\to t\bar t\gamma$ $0.22$; then $u\bar d\to W^+HH$ $0.28$, $b\bar b\to HH$ $0.23$. Against the
draft's sec:cond on the same split (raw $R^2$, no map): $gg\to t\bar tH$ is at most $0.53$ there and $1-0.50=0.50$ here;
$gg\to u\bar dW^-$ is $0.35$ to $0.91$ there and $0.995$ here ($1-\rho^2=5.5\times10^{-3}$). Ours has the affine map for
free, theirs does not, so the comparison favours us by exactly the scale and offset.

standardized $\log|\mathcal M|^2$. hp1466 is drawn to its best checkpoint (cross); it blew up after it.}

\caption{The catalog pretraining (hp58) on the $139$ held-out processes of arXiv:2606.23791's split, with no data of them:

\paragraph{Predicting a process's normalization from the process (2026-10-22, D81).} A model normalized per process has
no prediction in $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ for a process without data: it needs that process's mean $\mu_p$ and spread
$\sigma_p$ of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$. The design agreed (D81, the statistics-predictor design): a map from the process alone to
$(\hat\mu_p,\hat\sigma_p)$, the \emph{statistics predictor}, first fitted on the training processes' measured statistics, then used to normalize the
training (each training process by the predictor fitted without its relabelling class, so the model learns to correct its
errors as it will have to on a new process), with a physics skeleton. Features, no momenta
(\code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/stats\_net.py}): the particle count $N$ (at fixed $\sqrt s$ the mass dimension $8-2N$
of $|\mathcal M|^2$ makes the level linear in $N$), the amplitude's coupling powers, the counts of each kind of
external particle incoming and outgoing apart, and the flavour structure: up- and down-type quarks and antiquarks per
side, an initial quark-antiquark pair of one flavour, a flavour carried into the final state, a final pair of one
flavour; each process takes the features of whichever of itself and its charge conjugate gives the smaller vector
($|\mathcal M|^2$ is conjugation invariant up to CP violation). Without the flavour structure the light quarks are one
kind, and $s\bar s\to d\bar uW^+$ took the mean of processes with the same counts and a very different level
($\hat\mu=-4.1$ against $\mu=-11.5$); a generation-pairing feature for the CKM structure was tried and did not help. Its
test, CPU only (\code{pool\_stats.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:stats_net}): fitted on the $1352$ training processes ($\mu_p,\sigma_p$
from $10^4$ train events each, within $0.012$ median of hp58's own statistics), scored on the $139$ held-out ones by the
level error $(\hat\mu-\mu)^2/\sigma^2$ and the spread ratio $\hat\sigma/\sigma$, the two parts of a zero-shot error the
normalization sets (Fig.~\ref{fig:zero_shot_parts}). Median level error over the $139$: one shared normalization $6.0$,
the mean linear in $N$ $2.9$, the physics features in a ridge regression $0.59$, with gradient-boosted trees on its
residuals $0.015$ ($90\%$ quantile $124$, $83$, $6.1$, $1.4$). On the $43$ held-out processes that relabel no training
process: $45$, $31$, $0.76$, $0.32$. Spread ratio, $10$ to $90\%$ of processes: $0.61$ to $2.49$ for one spread (both
baselines), $0.69$ to $1.42$ linear, $0.88$ to $1.24$ with the trees. Cross-fitted on the training processes, a
relabelling class out of the fit together: $3.0$, $1.7$, $0.46$, $0.064$. The $96$ relabels have exact twins in
training, so their numbers measure a lookup; the $43$ and the cross-fit are the test. Every choice in the predictor (features,
the trees' $800$ rounds of depth $3$ at rate $0.03$) is made on the cross-fit alone, never on the held-out processes;
the model's own particle table pooled per side and the number of Feynman diagrams as features lower hp58's held-out
MSE on its own ($1.27$ to $0.89$ on the $43$) but raise the cross-fit level error ($0.077$ to $0.12$), so they are not
used (\code{stats\_net.py --props --ndiag}).

The per-process pretraining (hp58) read with the predictor's statistics, $\hat\mu+\hat\sigma h$ with $h$ its output, is a prediction on its own (a zero-shot on the $43$; the $96$ relabel a training process): nothing from
the held-out process's data (\code{job\_catalog\_heldout\_preds.sh}, \code{heldout\_delta.py};
Figs.~\ref{fig:heldout_delta}, \ref{fig:heldout_dist} and \ref{fig:heldout_mse}). Median over processes of MSE$(\log|\mathcal M|^2)$ on the
$43$: $1.27$ ($10$ to $90\%$: $0.10$ to $4.1$), against $0.46$ with the process's own train mean and spread (two
numbers from its data, none fitted to the predictions) and $0.080$ after the best rescale and shift; on the $96$
relabels $0.12$, $0.0091$, $0.0055$. The median $|\Delta|$ per process on the $43$: $0.69$, $0.37$, $0.14$. The worst
on its own are $gg\to b\bar tW^+$ and its conjugate ($46$ and $43$; the predictor places the mean about $5$ too low) and $s\bar s\to
d\bar uW^+$ and its conjugate ($14$; its mean about $4$ too high). So the predictor's level error, not the shape of hp58's
prediction, is most of the gap on its own; the rescale-and-shift number bounds what a perfect predictor could reach with this
model. Not run: a pretraining normalized by the predictor.

$1352$ training processes: the fraction of held-out processes below (a) a level error $(\hat\mu-\mu)^2/\sigma^2$ and (b)
a spread ratio $\hat\sigma/\sigma$. The two baselines share one spread, so their curves in (b) coincide.}
\label{fig:stats_net}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{How much of a process's level physics fixes (2026-10-22, D82).} A dataset's mean and spread of
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$ depend on how its events were drawn (the $\sqrt s$ window, the sampling, cuts), which no
description of the process carries (the user), so a normalization for a process without data cannot use them.
Normalized by one standardization shared by all processes, the network has to learn each process's level itself; the
question is how much of that a fixed physics factor can take off first. Per event $y=\log|\mathcal M|^2-\log F$ with
$F=\hat s^{\,4-N}C_p$: $\hat s$ the event's own squared collision energy (the mass dimension $8-2N$ of $|\mathcal M|^2$),
$C_p$ the coupling product of the process's leading tree diagram (\code{diagram\_couplings.py}, from its diagram
sidecar, the sm model's couplings, dimensionful ones over $v$); one reference $s_0$ for the whole catalog, chosen on
the training processes (\code{norm\_factor\_stats.py} on all $10^5$ train events of each pool, CPU;
\code{norm\_factor\_check.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:norm_factor}). The spread (std) of the process levels across the $1352$
training processes: $5.4$ for $\log|\mathcal M|^2$, $3.8$ after $C_p$, $4.5$ after $\hat s^{\,4-N}$, $2.9$ after both
($\sqrt{s_0}=17$\,GeV; $4.2$ at $\sqrt{s_0}=1$\,TeV), against a median spread within a process of $2.1$ ($2.3$ after
the factor). On the $43$ new held-out processes, the level error $(m_p-M)^2/\sigma_p^2$ a network would make if it
learned no level at all: median $47$ for $\log|\mathcal M|^2$, $18$, $15$, and $4.8$ with both factors (the statistics
statistics predictor, fitted on the training statistics: $0.32$). The largest remain after both factors: the multi-Higgs processes
($s\bar c\to W^-HH$, $b\bar b\to ZHH$, $b\bar b\to HHH$ at $340$ to $500$), whose level the leading diagram's couplings
do not capture. So the factor removes about half the spread of levels and ten times the median level error, and leaves
a level spread larger than the spread within a process for the network to learn.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\pnl{../analysis/catalog_daniel/figs/norm_factor_legend.pdf} \\[1ex]
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/catalog_daniel/figs/norm_factor_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/catalog_daniel/figs/norm_factor_b.pdf}}
\caption{The level of each catalog process under four targets, with one standardization shared by all processes:
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$, minus the leading diagram's $\log C_p$, minus $(4-N)\log(\hat s/s_0)$ per event, minus both.
(a) the process means of the $1352$ training processes, each minus the median; (b) the level error against the shared
mean on the $43$ new held-out processes, the error of a network that learned no level.}
\label{fig:norm_factor}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{More data-free normalizations, and a learned level (2026-10-22, D83).} With two pretrainings running on targets that need no per-process statistics
(\code{scripts/job\_catalog\_norm.sh}): the \emph{shared} target ($\log|\mathcal M|^2$ with one standardization shared by
all processes) and the \emph{$\hat s$+coupling} target (the same after dividing by the factor of
Fig.~\ref{fig:norm_factor}, $\sqrt{s_0}=17$\,GeV), more targets
were scored on CPU from per-process moments of per-event quantities over every train pool
(\code{norm\_moments.py}, \code{norm\_moments2.py}, \code{norm\_explore.py}, \code{norm\_explore2.py};
Fig.~\ref{fig:norm_explore}), by the spread of the training levels, the median within-process std, the no-level
error on the $43$ new held-out processes, and a sampling sensitivity: the median over training processes of how far
the level moves, in units of $\sigma_p$, when the pool is reweighted flat in $\log\hat s$.
\begin{itemize}
  \item MadGraph divides the stored $|\mathcal M|^2$ by its averaging over initial spins and colours and by $n!$ for
    $n$ identical final particles; adding that back, with the colour sum of the final state, to the $\hat s$+coupling factor lowers the
    median no-level error on the $43$ from $4.8$ to $2.3$ (training level spread $2.9\to2.7$; shared target: $47$, $5.4$). Its
    $90\%$ quantile stays large ($250$).
  \item Inside a process $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ rises with $\hat s$: the within-process slope on $\log\hat s$ is $+1.1$ at
    $2\to2$ and $+0.6$ at $2\to3$ (median), against the $0$ and $-1$ of the mass dimension, and $\log\hat s$ explains
    $18\%$ of the within-process variance. A cut fixed in GeV does this: at higher $\hat s$ an event reaches closer to
    its soft and collinear limits. So the factor $\hat s^{\,4-N}$ raises the sampling sensitivity ($0.30\to0.39$).
  \item Per-event scales that see the singular regions (the mean log pair invariant, the smallest invariant, the mean
    nearest-neighbour invariant, a soft/collinear proximity), with one set of coefficients per particle count fitted
    by the within-process regression on the training pools, lower the within-process std from $2.1$ to $1.2$ and the
    sampling sensitivity to $0.19$, but raise the no-level error on the $43$ (to about $9$): they smooth the target
    and make it less tied to the sampling, without placing levels.
  \item A small network ($2\times64$, the process features of \code{stats\_net.py}, cross-fitted by relabelling class)
    predicting the remaining level from the process brings the median on the $43$ to $0.45$ on the shared target and $0.48$ on the $\hat s$+coupling one,
    $90\%$ quantiles $5.0$ and $21$. This is the level the amplitude network has to learn under a shared
    standardization.
  \item With the diagrams as descriptors too (\code{norm\_explore3.py}: the leading coupling product, the sum over
    diagrams against it, the number of diagrams, the massive internal lines; all known for a process without data),
    the same network places the shared target's level of the $43$ at a median level error of $0.045$ ($90\%$: $1.3$; cross-fit on the
    training processes $0.12$), ten times better than without them ($0.45$) and below the statistics predictor ($0.32$),
    and the multi-Higgs processes are no longer the worst ($gg\to t\bar tZ$ is, at $8$). On the $\hat s$+coupling target with IDEN and colour:
    $0.34$. The level reweighted flat in $\log\hat s$, less tied to how the pool drew $\hat s$, is easier to place
    without the diagrams ($0.18$ against $0.45$ on the shared target) and as easy with them ($0.066$). The pretraining's configuration feeds
    the diagrams to the amplitude network, so on the shared target it can learn levels the same way.
  \item The multi-Higgs processes are the worst in every target ($15$ processes, all held out, none in training).
    $q\bar q\to ZHH$ has the leading coupling product of $q\bar q\to ZH$ (with dimensionless couplings one more Higgs
    costs nothing) and a level $3.6$ lower; one cost per kind of final particle fitted on the training levels does
    not reach it either (median level error $12$ to $23$ on them), which fits a known cancellation between the
    diagrams of Higgs-pair production.
\end{itemize}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\pnl{../analysis/catalog_daniel/figs/norm_explore2_legend.pdf} \\[1ex]
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/catalog_daniel/figs/norm_explore2_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/catalog_daniel/figs/norm_explore2_b.pdf}}
\caption{Data-free normalizations of the catalog: the shared target (one shared standardization), the $\hat s$+coupling
target (with the factor $(s_0/\hat s)^{4-N}/C_p$), the same with MadGraph's averaging and symmetry factor and the final colour sum, and the last
two with a small network predicting the remaining level from the process. (a) the training processes' levels; (b)
the level error on the $43$ new held-out processes.}
\label{fig:norm_explore}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The shared and the $\hat s$+coupling pretrainings (2026-10-22, D83).} The per-process pretraining's
(hp58's) config and HPs, $50$ validations, both arms on the
same $1352$ processes, read on the $139$ held-out with nothing taken from them (\code{scripts/job\_catalog\_norm.sh},
\code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/catalog\_norm\_heldout.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:catalog_norm}). The $\hat s$+coupling target: the per-event factor of
Fig.~\ref{fig:norm_factor} (leading coupling product and $(s_0/\hat s)^{4-N}$, $s_0=289$\,GeV$^2$, no IDEN or colour)
and one shared standardization; the shared target: one shared standardization. \emph{The shared run blew up}: $50$ consecutive steps with
non-finite gradients at step $8734$ (of hp58's horizon); it is read at its best checkpoint, saved before that. The $\hat s$+coupling run ran
to the end. MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ per process (the factor cancels in the difference), median with the
$10$ to $90\%$ range:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
 & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$43$ new} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{$96$ relabels} \\
 & on its own & after rescale and shift & on its own & after rescale and shift \\ \hline
per-process, statistics predicted & $1.27$ ($0.10$ to $4.1$) & $0.080$ & $0.12$ & $0.0055$ \\
$\hat s$+coupling & $2.41$ ($0.033$ to $17$) & $0.135$ & $0.0045$ ($0.0004$ to $0.024$) & $0.0042$ \\
shared (blew up) & $0.60$ ($0.17$ to $3.4$) & $0.22$ & $0.067$ & $0.058$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The rescale-and-shift columns are medians only. ``After rescale and shift'' is an oracle (the best affine map, fitted on the test truth). Split into the squared mean
error (level) and the variance of the error (shape), median over the $43$ new (\code{catalog\_norm\_heldout.py}):
$\hat s$+coupling $2.3$ and $0.14$, shared $0.11$ and $0.23$; the level's share of each process's error, median over
the $43$, is $0.90$ for $\hat s$+coupling and $0.38$ for shared ($0.85$ for the per-process pretraining with predicted
statistics). So the $\hat s$+coupling target learns the shape best, and on the relabels it is $27\times$ better than the
per-process pretraining with predicted statistics and better even than that pretraining after the oracle map; but on the new processes most of its error is the level its factor sets wrong.
The multi-Higgs processes are the bulk of it ($Z HH$, $W^\pm HH$, $HHH$ sit $3.6$ to $5.2$ too high: their leading
coupling product is $ZH$'s, as Fig.~\ref{fig:norm_factor} found), then $gg\to Wq\bar q'$ ($3.6$ too low). The shared target, with no
factor, places levels by learning them from the particles and is the best of the three on the new processes on its own.
Correcting the $\hat s$+coupling predictions afterwards by IDEN and colour or by the sum over diagrams makes it worse (median $2.05$ to
$9.8$ on the new, and $0.5$ to $1.9$ on the relabels): its network has learned its own levels, so a better factor has
to be in its training target.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/catalog_daniel/figs/catalog_norm_mse_interpolation.pdf}\\[1ex]
\includegraphics{../analysis/catalog_daniel/figs/catalog_norm_mse_extrapolation.pdf}
\caption{The $139$ held-out processes read on their own: MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ per process for the per-process pretraining read with
the predicted statistics, the $\hat s$+coupling target and the shared target (at its best checkpoint, before it blew
up), grouped as the held-out split, ordered by $\hat s$+coupling within each group.}
\label{fig:catalog_norm}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Few-shot calibration, and surrogates computed from momenta alone (2026-10-22, D83).} Median MSE of
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$ over the $43$ new and the $96$ relabels. \emph{Few-shot} (\code{norm\_explore5.py},
\code{norm\_explore7.py}; $k$ events of the new process drawn at random, $5$ draws, scored on the rest): the per-process pretraining with a
rescale and shift fitted on them reaches $0.137$ and $0.0081$ at $k=3$, $0.091$ and $0.0071$ at $k=10$, $0.082$ and
$0.0057$ at $k=100$ (the oracle: $0.080$, $0.0055$); the $\hat s$+coupling target needs only a shift ($0.167$, $0.0048$ at $k=3$); the shared target $0.28$, $0.066$
at $k=10$; shrinking the fitted slope toward $1$ helps at $k=3$ ($\hat s$+coupling $0.22\to0.14$, shared
$0.36\to0.29$). It holds only for representative events: the $\hat s$+coupling target shifted on the $10$ lowest-$\hat s$ events gives $0.21$, on the $10$ highest $0.62$, against
$0.16$ at random ($2\to3$ only). \emph{A CPU stand-in for shape transfer} (\code{norm\_explore6.py}: a $3\times128$
network on $400$ events per process and summary invariants, ten candidate targets, three seeds) cannot rank them: every
target lands at $0.40$ to $0.57$ on the new and $0.67$ to $0.70$ on the relabels, against $0.004$ to $0.06$ for the real
models; too weak to test with. \emph{Surrogates from momenta alone} (\code{norm\_surrogate*.py}, \code{data/surrogate*.json};
$2000$ events per process, the labels used only to score; the momenta, the cuts and the sampling cost nothing): per
event, within a process, the correlation with the true $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ (median, $r^2$ in brackets) is $0.83$
($0.69$) on the new for the product of the diagram sidecars' propagator factors, $0.67$ ($0.46$) on the training
processes, $0.44$ ($0.20$) on the relabels, $0.39$ at $2\to2$ and $0.68$ at $2\to3$; adding an eikonal factor over the
coloured massless legs gives $0.70$ ($0.50$) on training. The mass dimension, the eikonal and Parke--Taylor alone have
$r^2\le0.21$ on the catalog (constant for most of it: no final gluon or photon, or $2\to2$). As level predictors across
processes no surrogate's mean beats the statistics predictor (best: dimension with IDEN and colour, $2.7$ to $3.1$;
the predictor $0.32$), but in
multiplicity it extrapolates where the learned map does not: fitted on $2\to2$ and scored on $2\to3$, $0.34$ against the
descriptor network's $7.1$. \emph{Extrapolation within a family} ($u\bar u\to Z+ng$ and $u\bar d\to W+ng$, $n=1..5$, the
transfer study's pools): the eikonal and Parke--Taylor surrogates explain $81$ to $96\%$ of each process's spread, rising
with $n$, and a level and log-std map fitted on $n\le3$ places $n=4$, $5$ at a level error of $0.005$ and $0.014$ ($Z$),
$0.004$ and $0.013$ ($W$), the log-std to $0.02$ to $0.03$ (the dimension factor $0.09$ to $0.47$). The two give identical
maps on these families, not yet checked as a coincidence or a fallback. The catalog's maps carried to these families
(up to $8$ particles) are poor at low $n$ ($4.6$ to $14$ at $n=1$). \emph{One loop}: not captured, and not yet a clean
test: the tree-to-one-loop level shift is $+0.04$ to $+4.3$ against about $-2.9$ from a Catani-style factor, the tree
surrogates correlate at $0.06$ to $0.29$ with the one-loop targets, and the one-loop pools are sampled uniformly where the
tree pools use the mixture, and what they store was not checked. Reading: a soft-collinear factor computed from the
momenta alone carries most of the per-event structure of the multi-gluon families and extrapolates their level and
spread in $n$; on the catalog the diagram propagators carry the most. Their use is as a per-event input to a model with
one shared standardization, which has nothing fixed outside the network for a new process.

\paragraph{Short design runs at seen multiplicities (2026-10-22, D87).} The per-process pretraining's (hp58's) configuration with $10^4$ training and
$200$ validation events per process ($1/10$ of its), $4126$ steps (5 epochs), events in memory, a $150$-step
$\sigma$ stage reusing the loaded data, about $1$\,h each (\code{scripts/job\_catalog\_norm.sh <arm> short},
\code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/catalog\_short\_heldout.py}); read on the $139$ held-out on their own as
Fig.~\ref{fig:catalog_norm}. Median MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$, with the level's median share of each process's error:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
 & $43$ new & level share & after rescale and shift & $96$ relabels \\ \hline
shared, full length (blew up; best checkpoint) & $0.604$ & $0.38$ & $0.22$ & $0.067$ \\
shared, short & $0.603$ & $0.24$ & $0.24$ & $0.069$ \\
$\hat s$ only, short ($s_0=289$) & $0.652$ & $0.20$ & $0.20$ & $0.060$ \\
$\hat s$+coupling, short & $2.55$ & $0.76$ & $0.17$ & $0.053$ \\
$\hat s$+coupling, full length & $2.41$ & $0.90$ & $0.135$ & $0.0045$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The short runs reproduce the full-length ones where both exist (shared $0.603$ against $0.604$ on the new; $\hat s$+coupling $2.55$ against
$2.41$), so they are a usable design tool at a tenth of the cost. The coupling product is what fails: $\hat s$+coupling is $4\times$
worse than shared on the new processes at either length, three quarters of it level. The $\hat s$ factor alone is level
with shared: $0.65$ against $0.60$ in the median, better in the tail (the $90\%$ point $2.6$ against $3.3$) and on the relabels
($0.060$ against $0.069$), with the level error slightly lower ($0.092$ against $0.11$). So at multiplicities seen in
training the dimension factor costs nothing and gains a little, and the coupling product should go. The $\hat s$+coupling target's relabel advantage
($0.0045$) appears only at full length, with $10\times$ the events and $8\times$ the steps. $\sigma$ after the
$150$-step stage, scored from \code{sigma\_stage} (the MSEs are identical to the main run's, $\mu$ being frozen): pull
std $1.15$ (shared), $1.27$ ($\hat s$ only) and $1.16$ ($\hat s$+coupling) on the relabels, $2.9$, $2.5$ and $4.7$ on
the new processes, against $1.15$, $1.22$, $1.13$ and $2.6$, $2.5$, $4.2$ before the stage: the short stage leaves
$\sigma$ almost where the main run put it. The short runs stop before
step $8734$, where the full-length shared run blew up, so they say nothing on its stability.

\paragraph{Extrapolation in multiplicity: the units of the target (2026-10-22, D88).} Every target of the form
$T=\log|\mathcal M|^2-(4-N)\log\Lambda^2$ with one shared standardization is well defined; they differ in the
reference scale $\Lambda$: the shared target as run is $\Lambda=1$\,GeV (an arbitrary unit: in TeV a $2\to3$ process moves by
$\log10^6=13.8$ against a $2\to2$), the $\hat s$ factor's $s_0$ is a $\Lambda$ fitted on the catalog (the same as a mean affine in $N$),
and $\Lambda^2=\hat s$ per event gives the dimensionless $|\mathcal M|^2\hat s^{N-4}$, the only member that depends on
no unit and no fit. The test: the per-process pretraining's configuration trained on the $236$ $2\to2$ training processes only ($10^5$ events
each, $4126$ steps, the short runs' settings otherwise; \code{scripts/job\_catalog\_norm.sh Bgev|Dless extrap}), read on
the held-out $2\to3$ processes, a multiplicity it never saw. With $4-N=0$ on every training event, the shared target in TeV and in GeV have the same training targets, as do the $s_0=289$ variant and the dimensionless one, so two networks give four readings, differing
only by a constant at $N=5$. Median MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ per process (level and shape: squared mean error and
error variance; \code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/catalog\_extrap\_heldout.py}, which also derives the read-back offsets):
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
 & shared, GeV & shared, TeV & dimensionless & $s_0=289$\,GeV$^2$ \\ \hline
$2\to3$, $42$ new & $83$ ($82$, $1.2$) & $24$ ($23$, $1.2$) & $11$ ($10$, $1.5$) & $8.6$ ($7.3$, $1.5$) \\
$2\to3$, $79$ relabels & $28$ ($22$, $2.8$) & $89$ ($83$, $2.8$) & $43$ ($40$, $3.9$) & $7.6$ ($3.7$, $3.9$) \\
$2\to2$, $17$ relabels & $0.013$ & $0.013$ & $0.012$ & $0.012$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The unit choice moves the shared target's error on the new multiplicity by $3.5\times$ either way, so it extrapolates
by accident of units.
The dimensionless target removes that arbitrariness but does not place the level: its $2\to3$ predictions are a median
$4.8$ too low ($10$ to $90\%$: $0.7$ to $10$); a linear fit of that offset on the leading coupling product leaves $3.9$ of
its $23$ (median squared), so the missing level is mostly the coupling cost of the extra particle, which a network that
saw only $2\to2$ cannot know. The $s_0=289$ reading is best, but $s_0$ was fitted on the full catalog, $2\to3$ included,
so it is not data-free here. The shape does not transfer from $2\to2$ either ($1.2$ to $3.9$ after the level, $0.9$ to
$3.4$ after the oracle rescale and shift, against $0.14$ for $\hat s$+coupling trained with $2\to3$). Dimensional analysis fixes the units,
not the magnitude: a sampling- and unit-agnostic target is necessary for extrapolating in multiplicity but not sufficient,
and a test from $2\to2$ alone is too far a step to rank targets on shape.

\paragraph{The dimensionless round: one axis at a time (2026-10-23, D90, D92, D93).} The short design runs of D87 (the
catalog's $2\to2$ and $2\to3$ training processes, $10^4$ events each), read on held-out sets that change one thing a user
can change: new processes at seen multiplicities (the catalog's $43$), the collider energy alone ($40$ catalog $2\to3$
training processes regenerated at $100$\,TeV), one more particle ($20$ of those $2\to3$ processes plus a gluon, $20$ plus a
photon, \code{recipes/gen\_catalog\_2to4.py}, a multiplicity no run saw), the cuts on that particle ($p_T\geq30$ instead of
$10$\,GeV) and one more particle at $100$\,TeV ($16$ of the $20$ gluon probes: the other four failed generation at that
energy, four retries each). Arms: the $\hat s$ factor with the fitted $s_0=289$\,GeV$^2$ (D87), the dimensionless target
with $\Lambda^2=\hat s$ and with $\Lambda^2$ the geometric mean of the event's pair invariants $2p_i\cdot p_j$ (the seed-$42$
run diverged at step $2505$ on $ud\to ud$, a $2\to2$ process; the table is its seed-$1$ rerun, which did not), and three on
the dimensionless target with dimensionless inputs ($p/\sqrt{\hat s}$, $\log_{10}(m/\sqrt{\hat s})$): with hp58's diagram
encoder (arXiv:2606.23791, topology only), with its pooling weighted per event by each diagram's propagator virtualities
(\code{model.diagram\_encoder\_llm4lhc.virtuality\_pool}), and without diagrams
(\code{scripts/job\_catalog\_norm.sh}; \code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/catalog\_short\_heldout.py},
\code{catalog\_2to4\_heldout.py}, \code{dimless\_round.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:dimless_round}). Median MSE of
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$ per process, $2\to4$ as $+g$ / $+\gamma$, with the median offset (mean error) in brackets:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
 & $43$ new & $2\to3$ at $100$\,TeV & $2\to4$, $p_T\geq10$\,GeV & $2\to4$ at $100$\,TeV \\ \hline
$\hat s$ only, $s_0=289$ & $0.65$ & $0.081$ & $4.6$ / $4.0$ ($-0.9$ / $+0.4$) & $4.6$ / $4.8$ \\
dimensionless, $\Lambda^2=\hat s$ & $0.72$ & $0.104$ & $45$ / $26$ ($-6.5$ / $-4.8$) & $43$ / $28$ \\
dimensionless, $\Lambda^2$ pairs & $0.77$ & $0.094$ & $15$ / $6.3$ ($-3.6$ / $-2.0$) & $14$ / $6.7$ \\
dimensionless inputs, encoder & $1.57$ & $0.062$ & $34$ / $17$ ($-5.7$ / $-3.9$) & $33$ / $19$ \\
\quad with virtuality pooling & $1.15$ & $0.064$ & $38$ / $25$ ($-6.0$ / $-4.7$) & $35$ / $27$ \\
\quad without diagrams & $1.67$ & $0.085$ & $37$ / $19$ ($-5.9$ / $-4.1$) & $35$ / $20$ \\
\quad with the coupling orders & $0.48$ & $0.077$ & $52$ / $30$ ($-7.0$ / $-5.3$) & $55$ / $30$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The energy is not the problem: every arm reads the $2\to3$ processes at $100$\,TeV with a median MSE of $0.06$ to $0.11$ and offsets of
at most $0.03$. Nor are the cuts: from $p_T\geq10$ to $30$\,GeV the $2\to4$ offset moves by
at most $0.6$ in any arm (under $0.3$ in three of the seven), small against the offsets themselves. The extra particle is: at $13$ and
$100$\,TeV alike, every dimensionless arm puts the $2\to4$ processes $2$ to $7$ too low, most of its
error, and the $100$\,TeV probes repeat the $13$\,TeV numbers. Of the unit-free references the event's pair scale is the
best by a factor $3$ to $4$ (it shrinks with a soft or collinear particle, which $\hat s$ does not), and the result is the
same on both seeds ($11.5$ / $6.2$ at seed $42$'s best checkpoint). The fitted $s_0$ does better still, with offsets under
$1$: $\sqrt{s_0}=17$\,GeV is of the order of the catalog's cuts, and the catalog it was fitted on has no $2\to4$ process, so
here it is an honest extrapolation, but it is a property of one set of cuts that the pair scale gets from the event. Diagrams on
their own (the encoder against none, same target and inputs) lower the error by $6$ to $30\%$: $1.57$ against $1.67$ on
the new processes, $0.056$ against $0.080$ on the $96$ relabels, $34$ / $17$ against $37$ / $19$ at $2\to4$. Weighting the pooling
by virtuality lowers the new processes' error further, to $1.15$, and leaves the relabels as they are ($0.059$), but does
nothing for the extra particle ($38$ / $25$). It gives the encoder a per-event view of which diagrams dominate, not the
cost of an emission. The dimensionless inputs cost on the new processes ($1.57$ against $0.72$ with the same target and raw
inputs) and gain at $2\to4$ ($34$ / $17$ against $45$ / $26$).

\paragraph{The coupling orders in the target (D93).}\label{par:dimless_orders} The other half of the D93 target divides each
event's $|\mathcal M|^2$ by $(4\pi\alpha_s)^q(4\pi\alpha)^e$, with $q$, $e$ the process's QCD and QED orders and $\alpha_s$
the event's own (the value MadEvent labelled it at, \code{tools/recover\_alphas.py}; \code{data.target\_physics\_orders},
arm \code{DlessInOrd}: the dimensionless inputs and encoder row with this factor, same steps, HPs and seed). It is exact
for a single coupling monomial, so mixed-order entries (four-quark, heavy-quark pairs) are refused; none is in the
catalog's training set. Of the $1491$ catalog pools, $11$ did not reproduce in the $\alpha_s$ recovery; $10$ are purely
electroweak ($q=0$, no $\alpha_s$ needed), and the eleventh, the probe $g\bar d\to\bar dZHg$, is a regenerated sample at
both cuts (\code{d4r\_}, \code{d4c30r\_}), so that $2\to4$ cell is not paired event by event with the other rows. On new
processes the factor is the largest gain of the round, $1.57$ to $0.48$, the level error falling from $0.86$ to $0.19$
and the shape error from $0.42$ to $0.26$, with the relabels unchanged ($0.051$ against $0.056$) and the energy axis at
$0.077$. At $2\to4$ it costs: $52$ / $30$ against $34$ / $17$, through the offset ($-7.0$ / $-5.3$ against $-5.7$ / $-3.9$),
at both cuts (the offset moves by $0.4$ between them) and at $100$\,TeV. Removing the couplings moves a $+g$ and a $+\gamma$
target in opposite directions ($-\log 4\pi\alpha_s\approx-0.4$, $-\log 4\pi\alpha\approx+2.4$), yet both offsets grow by
about $1.4$, so the change is in how the network extrapolates to one more particle, not the factor's arithmetic. One seed
each.

\paragraph{Particle content and perturbative order (D93).}\label{par:dimless_particle} The last two axes, read with the
coupling-order arm (Fig.~\ref{fig:dimless_axes}; \code{catalog\_top\_heldout.py}, \code{catalog\_loop\_heldout.py},
\code{dimless\_axes.py}). Particle content: a twin of \code{DlessInOrd} trained without the catalog's $142$ processes
with a top quark ($1210$ processes, \code{recipes/catalog\_daniel\_train\_notop.yaml}; no relabelling class mixes top and
non-top; two Higgs bosons needed no run, the catalog's split already holds all $15$ such processes out), read on all $162$
processes with a top. Trained with them, the $142$ read at $0.054$ and the $20$ held out at $0.16$; never having seen a
top, the twin reads them at $15$ and $10$, a factor $230$ and $50$ per process at the median, with median $|$offset$|$
$3.3$ and $2.0$: an unseen particle is as far out of reach as one more particle (the top enters only through its row of
the particle table, mass and quantum numbers, which no training process varied).
\label{par:dimless_loop} Perturbative order: the $57$ one-loop pools on CC whose target is positive in every split (QCD
and electroweak virtuals and loop-induced, $e^+e^-$ and quark initial states, every $\sqrt s$ window;
\code{recipes/catalog\_loop\_probes.yaml}) with the tree of the same process and window ($42$; $30$ of them have the particle content of a catalog training
process, $4$ of a held-out one, the $8$ $e^+e^-$-initiated ones of none, all generated by our own sampler on a flat
partonic $\sqrt s$ window rather than the catalog's pp events), read with no further training; the $55$
one-loop pools whose target changes sign cannot be read by a $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ model and are left out (hand-off). On the
$79$ probes both arms can score, the trees read at $0.35$ (DlessIn) and $0.33$ (with the orders; quark-initiated $0.20$
and $0.16$, $e^+e^-$-initiated $3.5$ and $3.6$, an initial state the catalog lacks) and their one-loop partners at $9.0$ and
$10.1$: the stripped virtual of the same process and window is a median $18$ and $26$ times its tree's error (DlessIn on all
$99$: $11$). Most of that error is the level, not the shape (next paragraph): once one labelled event fixes the offset the one-loop
pools read at $1.0$ (with the orders), and the shape part alone is $0.50$, against $0.22$ for their trees.

\centering

\caption{Per-process MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$, ECDF. Left: the $162$ catalog processes with a top quark, read by the
coupling-order design run and by its twin trained without any top. Right: the trees (dashed) and one-loop pools (solid) of
the order set, on the $79$ probes both arms score, without and with the coupling orders in the target.}
\label{fig:dimless_axes}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\pnl{../analysis/catalog\_daniel/figs/dimless\_round\_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/catalog\_daniel/figs/dimless\_round\_b.pdf}
\caption{The short design runs on the held-out sets, median MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ per process. Left: the catalog's
$43$ new processes and $40$ of its $2\to3$ training processes at $100$\,TeV. Right: the $2\to4$ probes, a training $2\to3$ process plus a gluon (filled) or a photon (open), at
$13$\,TeV with $p_T\geq10$ and $30$\,GeV and at $100$\,TeV.}
\label{fig:dimless_round}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{What a few labelled events fix, and where the level errors come from (2026-10-23).} The user's question: one
event of a process is cheap next to its dataset, and should already fix most of a zero-shot error. Measured on the saved
reads of the dimensionless round, no new run (\code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/kshot\_level.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:kshot_level}):
per process, $K$ validation events drawn at random, the offset taken as the mean error on them and subtracted, the MSE of
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$ measured on the remaining events, $200$ draws; median over processes. With the coupling orders
(\code{DlessInOrd}), from $K=0$ to $1$ to $8$: $2\to4$ $+g$ $52\to5.0\to2.9$, $+\gamma$ $30\to4.6\to2.6$, the tops never
seen $14\to2.9\to1.7$, the one-loop pools $10\to1.0\to0.57$; their floors (the error's variance, what any constant per
process can reach) are $2.6$, $2.3$, $1.5$ and $0.50$. On the $43$ new processes the prior is already better than one event
($0.48$ at $K=0$, $0.53$ at $K=1$, $0.30$ at $K=8$, floor $0.26$), so a calibration has to weigh the prediction against the
events, not replace it. Weighing them (a prior on the offset from the other probes of the same set, its
mean and spread leave-one-process-out, combined with the $K$ events by their precision): one event no longer hurts there
($0.45$), and at $K=0$ the prior alone takes $2\to4$ $+g$ from $52$ to $4.7$, $+\gamma$ from $30$ to $4.4$ and the one-loop
pools from $10$ to $5.1$, but the tops only from $14$ to $13$. The $2\to4$ offset is one number for the whole axis (with the
orders $-6.7$, spread $1.4$ over the $20$ processes), the tops' is not. An affine correction (offset and scale) needs $K\geq8$ to beat the offset alone and lowers the floor
by $10$ to $50\%$. So on every out-of-distribution axis $85$ to $95\%$ of the zero-shot error is one number per process;
what is left is $2\to4$ at $\sim2.5$ and unseen tops at $1.5$, a real shape error, against $0.2$ to $0.5$ elsewhere.
Where the level errors come from (\code{level\_range.py}):
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{$2\to4$: outside the range the training spans.} The median $2\to4$ $+g$ target lies above the training levels
  (the $96$ relabels as their proxy) in every dimensionless arm, and the predictions stay inside: $\Lambda^2=\hat s$ true
  $13.5$, predicted $8.1$, the relabels' median $5.0$ and maximum $17.5$; with the coupling orders true $16.6$, predicted $9.9$,
  $70\%$ of the probes above the training maximum $15.2$. With the fixed $s_0=289$\,GeV$^2$ the $2\to4$ levels fall inside
  ($2.1$ against a median $0.4$, none above the maximum) and the prediction follows ($1.4$). A dimensionless $|\mathcal M|^2$
  still grows by roughly $\hat s/k_T^2$ per extra soft or collinear particle, $k_T$ that particle's resolution scale
  (here near the $10$\,GeV cut); a scale of the order of the cuts cancels that growth, $\hat s$ does not, and the network does
  not extrapolate a level past what it was trained on.
  \item \emph{Loop-induced pools: a missing loop factor.} The four loop-induced pools are predicted too high by $+7.4$ to
  $+11.0$ (DlessIn, median $+10.7$; with the orders $+8.2$), close to $\log(16\pi^2)^2=10.1$, the factor a squared one-loop
  amplitude carries and no tree does; the stripped virtuals sit at a median $-3.1$ ($-2.1$), from $-7.6$ to $+7.1$. Four
  pools, so a pointer rather than a measurement.
  \item \emph{Tops: thresholds.} The twin trained without tops puts the $t\bar t$ processes too high (quark-initiated,
  $29$, median $+5.0$) and the single-top ones too low ($88$, $-1.3$; quark-gluon, $28$, $-3.9$; its $10$ top pairs $-2.0$), with spreads of $3$ within each
  group: the network has never seen a mass that moves a threshold, so it cannot know that a $2m_t$ threshold suppresses the
  pair, and it places the top as a light quark.
\end{itemize}
No paper we found trains one regressor on many processes and tests the level of unseen ones (survey, 2026-10-23). The
closest remedies are single-process: factorisation-aware emulators that write $|\mathcal M|^2$ as Catani-Seymour dipoles
times learned coefficients (arXiv:2107.06625; at one loop, arXiv:2302.04005) and multi-jet surrogates built on the lower
multiplicity times a splitting factor (arXiv:2512.11036). The interatomic-potential convention of per-element reference
energies (MACE-MP-0, arXiv:2401.00096) is the nearest analogue of a level fixed by composition.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/catalog\_daniel/figs/kshot\_level.pdf}
\caption{Zero-shot error after a per-process offset fitted on $K$ labelled events, median over processes of the MSE of
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$ on the other events (mean over $200$ draws of the $K$). Left: dimensionless inputs and the diagram
encoder (\code{DlessIn}); right: the same with the coupling orders in the target (\code{DlessInOrd}; the tops are read by
its twin trained without any top). $K=0$ is the zero-shot read.}
\label{fig:kshot_level}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The candidate targets on the pools themselves, before any training (2026-10-23, D83).} Three reads from the
data alone. (1) \emph{Where the $2\to4$ shape error sits} (\code{residual\_kinematics.py}: each saved zero-shot
prediction joined to its event by value, all $40$ probes matched): the error, offset removed, follows how soft or
collinear the event is, correlation $+0.6$ to $+0.7$ with the clustering scales against $\hat s$, the softest fifth of
the events predicted $1.2$ to $1.6$ too low and the hardest $0.9$ to $1.3$ too high, in both arms and for $+g$ and
$+\gamma$ alike; five kinematic variables together explain $70$ to $74\%$ of it. The network learned the cost of one emission
in $k_T$ and does not double it for two. (2) \emph{Candidate targets} (\code{norm\_targets\_stats.py} on CC, per event over
$2\times10^4$ events of each of the $1670$ pools; \code{norm\_targets.py} at home): each target's per-process level read
three ways, the range against the training processes', a level model from composition alone (ridge on the counts of each
kind of particle incoming and outgoing, the reference-energy convention of interatomic potentials), and the
within-process spread. The candidates: $\hat s$, the fixed $s_0$, the pair scale, and the emission history of
\code{physics\_scales.py} (each particle beyond a $2\to2$ core carries its own Lorentz-invariant scale, the smallest
$s_{ak}s_{bk}/s_{ab}$ over the other legs plus $m_k^2$, every particle type alike; a $k_T$ clustering in the lab frame
gives the same picture but a trained run sees randomly Lorentz-transformed momenta), each with and without the coupling
orders. Median squared level error of the composition model, with the orders:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
 & cross-fit & $43$ new & $2\to4$ $+g$ & $2\to4$ $+\gamma$ & $2\to4$ $+g$, $p_T\geq30$ \\ \hline
$\hat s$ & $2.6$ & $0.48$ & $9.4$ & $3.0$ & $4.0$ \\
$s_0=289$\,GeV$^2$ & $2.4$ & $0.88$ & $2.9$ & $2.0$ & $16.9$ \\
pair scale & $2.5$ & $0.66$ & $3.3$ & $1.3$ & $3.2$ \\
emission history & $2.7$ & $3.3$ & $1.7$ & $0.96$ & $1.8$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The emission history keeps every $2\to4$ level inside the training range ($0.4$ interquartile ranges above the training
median, against $1.9$ for $\hat s$, where $35\%$ of the $+g$ probes lie above the training maximum), is the only target
that survives the cut change ($s_0$ goes from $2.9$ to $16.9$ when the cut moves to $30$\,GeV), and has the smallest
spread within a process ($2.4$ against $3.8$ for $\hat s$ at $2\to4$, $1.34$ against $1.61$ on the $43$ new). It costs on
processes whose final state is all massive ($Z HH$, $W HH$, $ZZZ$, $t\bar tH$: $-2$ to $-3$ where $\hat s$ is within
$\pm0.5$), where no emission is soft and $\hat s$ is the right scale; that is the $43$ new column. A composition model is a
proxy for what a network can learn, not a prediction of it, so the candidate goes to a short run (next). (3) \emph{The
sign-changing one-loop pools under $\mathrm{asinh}(V/(F r_0))$} (\code{signed\_band.py}, $53$ of the $55$ pools with val
events): one shared $r_0$ (the median of the pools' $0.5\%$ quantiles of $|VF|$) puts more than $10\%$ of the events in the
linear band on $14$ of them and all of them on two, since the pools' median $\log|VF|$ spans $-3.5$ to $10.7$; the user's
objection to the linear part holds for a shared $r_0$. The zero crossings themselves are thin: a median $3.4\%$ of a pool's
events lie within a factor $10$ of zero relative to its median, $0.2\%$ within a factor $100$. So $r_0$ must follow the
pool's level, which makes the signed target a consequence of the level problem: with the level normalized, one $r_0$
serves all. Following a \emph{predicted} level (\code{lin\_shift}, the $53$ pools): with $r_0$ off its pool's $0.5\%$
quantile by a level error $e$ (nat), the linear band holds a median $0.9\%$, $1.5\%$, $4.8\%$, $25\%$ of the events at
$e=+0.5,+1,+2,+3$, more than $10\%$ on $4$, $6$, $17$, $37$ pools; an $r_0$ set too low costs nothing ($0.16\%$ at $e=-1$).
The zero-shot level error on the held-out one-loop pools is $1.6$--$3$ ($|$offset$|$, DlessInOrd and DlessInOrdQ,
\code{qperp\_ab.py}), so $r_0$ has to sit that far below the predicted level, $r_0=\hat q\,e^{-3}$; the
signed target then needs no more than the level the catalog already predicts. The offsets' sign makes that margin
mostly free (\code{signed\_r0\_margin.py}, \code{data/signed\_r0\_margin.json}: the $46$ zero-shot one-loop offsets
crossed with the $53$ pools' \code{lin\_shift} curves, held at their end values past $\pm3$): the zero-shot level is
predicted too low on most pools (median offset $-1.6$ nat for DlessInOrd, $-3.0$ for DlessInOrdQ; $90\%$ quantile
$+5.3$ and $+1.9$), so already at $m=0$ the median share in the linear band is $0.04$--$0.1\%$. What the margin buys is
the tail: the share of (pool, offset) pairs with over $10\%$ of the events linear falls, for DlessInOrdQ, from $7.6\%$
at $m=0$ to $5.5\%$ at $m=3$ and $1.5\%$ at $m=5$ (DlessInOrd $9.9\%$, $7.8\%$, $6.7\%$; a lower bound wherever the
offset exceeds $m+3$, $0$--$13\%$ of offsets by $m$ and arm). So $m=3$ stands for the A/B with DlessInOrdQ's level, and the few
over-predicted pools, not the median, set how far below it has to go. The same reading on catalog\_v3's $62$ signed one-loop pools of stage 2a
(\code{signed\_band.py recipes/catalog\_v3\_gen\_stage2a.yaml}, \code{data/signed\_band\_v3.json}; one of them
entirely negative) agrees: a shared $r_0$ puts more than $10\%$ in the linear band on $11$, the median pool has $7.6\%$
of its events within a factor $10$ of zero ($0.7\%$ within $100$), and an $r_0$ off its own quantile by $e=+0.5$, $+1$,
$+2$, $+3$ nat leaves a median $0.9\%$, $1.4\%$, $4.1\%$, $12\%$ linear, over $10\%$ on $2$, $3$, $8$, $42$ pools; at
$e=-1$, $0.2\%$ and none. The signed set scored zero-shot on the magnitude (2026-10-23, collected 10-24: FA-ccin2p3-e1e5d61f for
DlessInOrd, FA-ccin2p3-f20eb068 for DlessInOrdQ; \code{loopsigned\_mag.py}, \code{data/loopsigned\_mag.json}): $23$
of the $49$ probes score, the other $26$ (every $2\to3$ with mixed coupling orders, $u\bar u\to d\bar dg$, $t\bar tZ$,
\dots, and their one-loop twins) stop on the order design's assertion that a mixed-order entry's coupling powers are
not exact. On the $12$ one-loop probes that score, the magnitude's MSE is $8.9$ (DlessInOrd) and $6.8$ (DlessInOrdQ),
mostly level (offset $-2.7$, $-2.3$; $1.2$, $1.3$ after it, correlation $0.73$, $0.56$); their $11$ tree twins score
$0.03$, $0.05$. So the sign-changing one-loop pools miss on the level as the positive ones do, and their shape after
the offset is $40\times$ worse than their trees'.

\paragraph{The signed pools' A/B: the sign head ahead in one seed (2026-10-24).} The short run of the emission-scale
target (par.~\ref{par:dimless_qperp}) with catalog\_v3's $48$ sign-changing one-loop training pools added, and two targets
for them (\code{scripts/job\_catalog\_norm.sh}, arms \code{SgnR0} and \code{SgnHead}; one seed each, $0.9$ GPU-h each):
the \emph{shifted signed log}, $\mathrm{sgn}(x)\log(1+|x|/r_0)$ with $r_0$ three nats below each pool's $0.5\%$ quantile of
$|x|$ (\code{data.signedlog\_scale\_shift} $-3$), and the \emph{sign head}, $\log|x|$ floored at its $1\%$ quantile plus a
sign logit. Both train on the MSE with the arithmetic mean and no $\sigma$ stage, since the sign head refuses the het
loss: a departure from the short setup, the same for both arms, so their losses are not comparable to the emission-scale
run's. The pools are the $62$ sign-changing ones of the first one-loop batch (stage 2a; the $68$ strings with a $b$ or $t$
leg were generated later): $48$ training, $14$ held out (H5). Scored on one footing (\code{tools/signed\_ab\_score.py};
\code{signed\_ab.py}, \code{data/signed\_ab.json}): the MSE of $\log\max(|x|,\text{floor})$ with one floor for both arms
(the sign head's own target space, so the metric favours it by construction), the wrong-sign fraction, and both within a
factor $10$ of zero. On the $48$ pools trained on, the sign head's magnitude error is $0.45$ against the shifted signed
log's $0.78$ (median per pool; ratio $1.7$, $10$--$90\%$ $1.0$--$2.9$, the signed log better on $5$), with the same
$1.3\%$ of signs wrong; within a factor $10$ of zero ($13\%$ of the events) the signed log is better ($1.45$ against
$1.75$, signs wrong $8.2\%$ against $9.2\%$). On the $14$ held-out pools, zero-shot: $1.9$ against $4.7$ (ratio $2.8$,
the signed log better on $3$), signs wrong $14\%$ against $18\%$. In this single-seed A/B the signed log's linear band,
even three nats below the level, costs the magnitude more than it gains at the crossing, where it is ahead; the sign head
is the target the catalog\_v3 runs use, a second seed and a crossing-weighted metric still to come.
Already in place for the training pools: \code{data.signedlog\_quantile} $0.01$ sets each signed pool's scale from its
own train pool.
Two negatives and one exact factor from the same pass. Putting every massive particle at $\hat s$ in the emission history
(no soft or collinear enhancement, \code{qperp\_logs(massive\_shat=True)}) is worse on the $43$ new ($8.8$) and at $2\to4$
$+g$ ($2.4$), so the all-massive finals are not the whole of that column's cost. Weighting the $K$ calibration events by the
$\sigma$ head ($1/\sigma^2$) changes nothing on the $43$ new ($0.53$, $0.34$, $0.30$ against $0.53$, $0.34$, $0.28$ at
$K=1,4,16$): the head's $\sigma$ is not informative off the training processes. The loop factor is exact: a loop-induced
$|\mathcal M_1|^2$ carries $(16\pi^2)^{-2}$, which removes $10.1$ from the four loop-induced offsets of
\code{DlessInOrd} ($+7.6$ to $+9.5$), leaving $-2.6$ to $-0.6$.

\paragraph{The emission scales in training (D99, 2026-10-23).}\label{par:dimless_qperp} The candidate through a short run:
\code{DlessInOrdQ}, the coupling-order run with $\hat s^{N-4}$ replaced by the product of the event's emission scales
(\code{data.target\_physics\_dim\_ref=qperp}, \code{physics\_scales.qperp\_logs}; inputs, encoder, steps, HPs and seed as
\code{DlessInOrd}), read on every set \code{DlessInOrd} was (\code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/qperp\_ab.py},
Fig.~\ref{fig:qperp_ab}; one seed each). Median MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ per process, $\hat s$ against the emission
scales, with the per-process ratio's median:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lccc}
 & $\hat s$ & emission scales & ratio, median ($10$--$90\%$) \\ \hline
$2\to4$ $+g$ / $+\gamma$ & $52$ / $30$ & $2.2$ / $2.7$ & $0.05$ / $0.07$ \\
$2\to4$ at $p_T\geq30$\,GeV, $+g$ / $+\gamma$ & $46$ / $26$ & $2.7$ / $2.5$ & $0.06$ / $0.09$ \\
$2\to4$ at $100$\,TeV, $+g$ / $+\gamma$ & $55$ / $30$ & $3.2$ / $3.2$ & $0.06$ / $0.09$ \\
$43$ new & $0.48$ & $0.75$ & $1.22$ ($0.29$--$3.8$) \\
$96$ relabels & $0.051$ & $0.064$ & $1.05$ \\
$2\to3$ at $100$\,TeV & $0.077$ & $0.086$ & $1.21$ \\
trees of the loop probes & $0.33$ & $0.31$ & $0.95$ \\
one-loop pools & $10.2$ & $10.0$ & $1.06$ \\
$162$ top processes, trained with & $0.060$ & $0.068$ & $1.00$ \\
$162$ top processes, never seen (twin) & $14.4$ & $10.3$ & $0.83$ ($0.38$--$1.34$) \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The extra particle stops being the failure: the $2\to4$ offset goes from $-7.0$ / $-5.3$ to $-1.0$ / $-0.15$ and its
shape error from $2.6$ / $2.3$ to $1.5$ / $1.8$, every $2\to4$ probe improves ($90\%$ of the ratios under $0.28$), at both
cuts and at $100$\,TeV, where the fixed $s_0$, the best earlier $2\to4$ arm ($4.6$ / $4.0$), is tied to one set of cuts.
The CPU proxy called both sides: the composition level at $2\to4$ $+g$ went from $9.4$ to $1.7$ and on the $43$ new from
$0.48$ to $3.3$; trained, the $43$ new cost a factor $1.6$ at the median MSE ($0.48$ to $0.75$; level $0.19$ to $0.31$,
shape $0.26$ to $0.48$) and per process a median $1.22$ with a wide spread (worst $b\bar b\to HHH$, $0.45$ to $5.6$; the
$Zhh$ and $ZZZ$ finals go both ways by $4\times$), the size of one seed's noise on some processes and not on others. On the
one-loop pools the shape error halves ($0.50$ to $0.22$) while the level stays the error ($10$), which is the loop factor and
the stripping convention, not the kinematics. On the particle-content axis the twins trained without any top read the
$162$ top processes at $14.4$ and $10.3$: the emission scales help a little (median per-process ratio $0.83$) but leave
an unseen particle out of reach, with a larger shape error ($1.5$ to $2.2$), as expected of a target that does not know
where a $2m_t$ threshold sits.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/catalog\_daniel/figs/qperp\_ab.pdf}
\caption{The coupling-order design run with $\hat s$ against the same run with the event's emission scales: per-process
MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$, ECDF. Left: the $80$ $2\to4$ probes ($+g$ and $+\gamma$, $p_T\geq10$ and $30$\,GeV). Right: the
$43$ new processes (solid) and the one-loop pools (dashed). One seed each.}
\label{fig:qperp_ab}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Where the cost on the new processes comes from (D83, at home, 2026-10-24).}\label{par:qperp_cost} The
composition proxy of par.\ \ref{par:dimless_qperp}'s neighbours (\code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/norm\_blend.py}) puts the
dimensionless target and the emission scales on the $43$ new processes at $0.48$ and $3.3$ and on the $+g$ probes at $9.4$
and $1.7$, the GPU A/B's order on both sets, and every mixture $\hat s^{1-\alpha} q^{2\alpha}$ in between ($\alpha=0.25$, $0.5$,
$0.75$) trades one for the other monotonically: no mixture keeps both. The cost is not spread over the $43$. Split by final
state, the proxy puts it on the $20$ whose finals are all massive bosons ($WWW$, $ZZZ$, $ZHH$, ...), $0.08$ to $3.5$ (a
bias of $-1.6$ in the log), while the other $23$ \emph{gain} ($2.2\to1.35$). Two generic repairs leave it: a step's scale saturating at
$\hat s$, $q^2\hat s/(q^2+\hat s)$ (all bosons $3.3$, others $1.37$; a hard massive boson's $q^2$ is still far below
$\hat s$, $\log q^2/\hat s\approx-2.5$ on these finals), and every massive particle at $\hat s$ ($11$ and $4.1$: it shifts the
level of every training process with a $W$, $Z$, $H$ or top, so the composition model's boson coefficients move for
all of them). Giving the level model each pool's label-free mean of $L_q-(N-4)\log\hat s$ as an input makes the new
processes worse ($5.6$) and the $+g$ probes as good as the emission scales ($2.2$). The GPU runs say otherwise, and they are what counts: split the
same way, the two arms' per-process MSE is $0.38\to0.64$ on the $20$ all-boson finals (their offset improves, $-0.50\to-0.10$)
and $0.50\to1.49$ on the other $23$, where the \emph{shape} doubles ($0.28\to0.63$; within-process variance of the error,
medians). The proxy reads only the process level and assumes the network fits any target's shape alike; the emission
scales give a narrower target spread ($1.61\to1.34$) that the network fits worse. $L_q$ is a minimum over legs and pairs, so
the target has kinks wherever the minimising pair changes; a per-process shape proxy says that is the cost
(\code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/shape\_learn.py}: one small regressor, the same for every target, on each of the $43$
pools' invariants, $15000$ events to fit and $5000$ to score): the held-out shape error, median over the $43$, is
$0.0026$ for the dimensionless target, $0.0038$ for the emission scales and $0.0027$ with their soft minimum
($-\tau\log\sum e^{-\log q^2/\tau}$ over the candidates, $\tau=0.3$; $0.0029$ at $\tau=0.1$), the GPU's order again. On the composition proxy it keeps the emission
scales' $2\to4$ level ($+g$ $1.82$ against $1.74$ and the dimensionless $9.35$; $+\gamma$ $1.07$; $+g$ at $p_T\ge30$
$1.78$). On the GPU it does not pay (DlessInOrdQs, D99, the short run of DlessInOrdQ with $\tau=0.3$, one seed;
\code{qperp\_ab.py}): the $43$ new $0.88$ against the hard minimum's $0.75$ and the dimensionless $0.48$ (shape $0.44$,
$0.48$, $0.26$), the $96$ relabels $0.074$, $0.064$, $0.051$, and on the $2\to4$ probes it keeps most of the emission
scales' gain but not all ($+g$ $3.97$ against $2.18$ and $51.8$; $+\gamma$ $3.32$, $2.65$, $29.6$). The kinks are not what
the $43$ new pay for, and the shape proxy's ranking of the three targets did not carry to the network.

\paragraph{The context vector (2026-10-24).} The emission-scale target's short run (par.~\ref{par:dimless_qperp}) with a
learned $c_p\in\mathbb R^{16}$ per training process added to every particle's encoding and zeroed on half the events
(\code{model.context\_dim}, \code{context\_dropout} $0.5$; arm \code{DlessInOrdQc}, one seed; trained on
\code{recipes/catalog\_daniel\_train.yaml}, its run config's \code{processes\_file}, so every probe below is held out), so its
zero-shot is the $c=0$ model. That model costs a little against the same target without the context on the held-out processes (\code{qperp\_ab.py}): the
$43$ new $0.97$ against $0.75$ (median per-process ratio $1.03$, $10$--$90\%$ $0.50$--$3.3$), the $96$ relabels
$0.073$ against $0.064$ (ratio $1.11$). What the context is for, the fit of $c_p$ on $K$ labelled events
(\code{tools/kshot\_context.py}; trunk and heads frozen, $c$ from $0$, $300$ Adam steps at
$0.05$, $5$ draws of the $K$ events per process, scored on the other $1995$ to $2000$ validation events), against a
per-process offset on the same $c=0$ predictions and the same draws, plain and shrunk as in Fig.~\ref{fig:kshot_level}
(\code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/kshot\_context\_cmp.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:kshot_context}; medians over processes, $K=0$,
$1$, $4$, $16$):
\begin{itemize}
  \item \emph{The $43$ new: the context does more than the level.} Zero-shot $0.97$; plain offset $0.83$, $0.55$, $0.50$;
  shrunk $0.72$, $0.54$, $0.50$; context $0.59$, $0.38$, $0.21$. The offsets stop at the floor ($0.46$, the variance of
  the $c=0$ error, what any constant per process reaches); the context goes under it from $K=4$ and reaches half of it
  at $K=16$, so it corrects the shape too. It beats the shrunk offset on $63$, $67$ and $77\%$ of the processes (median
  ratio $0.97$, $0.72$, $0.59$). The shrunk offset weighs the $K$ events with the process's error variance over its whole pool, which $K$ events do not give: an optimistic baseline, so the context's wins over it are conservative.
  \item \emph{$2\to4$: it does not.} $+g$: zero-shot $2.77$, shrunk prior alone $1.69$; plain $2.39$, $1.82$, $1.45$;
  shrunk $1.70$, $1.56$, $1.43$; context $2.43$, $2.10$, $1.48$; floor $1.34$. $+\gamma$: zero-shot $1.87$, prior alone
  $2.12$; plain $2.47$, $1.78$, $1.46$; shrunk $1.67$, $1.50$, $1.46$; context $2.36$, $2.23$, $1.68$; floor $1.37$. The
  context beats the plain offset only once ($+\gamma$ at $K=1$, $2.36$ against $2.47$), never goes under the floor, and wins against the shrunk offset on $10$, $25$,
  $50\%$ ($+g$) and $0$, $20$, $45\%$ ($+\gamma$) of the probes. Neither a constant nor a learned per-process vector at
  the input reaches the $2\to4$ shape error ($\sim1.4$): the network does not extrapolate past the multiplicities and levels
  it was trained on (par.\ What a few labelled events fix), and a vector that moves the embedding inside the trained
  range cannot carry it there.
\end{itemize}
One seed, five draws, the fit's step count and rate fixed (not tuned): a pointer on the $43$ new (where its gain is
large against the draw spread) and a clear negative on $2\to4$. In the plan of the level item this is the condition
for its design (ii), the $2\to4$ head built on the clustered $2\to3$ prediction.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/catalog_daniel/figs/kshot_context_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/catalog_daniel/figs/kshot_context_b.pdf}} \\[1ex]
\pnl{../analysis/catalog_daniel/figs/kshot_context_c.pdf}
\caption{The held-out processes of the context-vector model after $K$ labelled events: a per-process offset on the zero-shot ($c=0$)
prediction, plain or shrunk towards the other processes' offsets, against the fit of the context vector $c_p$ with the
network frozen; median over processes of the MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ on the other events (mean over $5$ draws). The
floor is the variance of the zero-shot error. (a) The $43$ new processes; (b) the $2\to4$ probes with a gluon,
(c) with a photon.}
\label{fig:kshot_context}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Design (ii) on the data: the $2\to4$ probes factorised onto their parents (2026-10-25).} Each $2\to4$
probe is a $2\to3$ training process plus one gluon or photon on the same beams, so its $|\mathcal M|^2$ can be read
against its parent's. Per probe, on $2000$ validation events (\code{tools/factorised\_2to4.py},
CPU): the softest particle of the extra flavour removed by the Catani-Seymour initial-initial map (hep-ph/9605323; the
beam it is closer to emits, the final momenta Lorentz-transformed, so masses and momentum conservation hold for any final
state), the parent's exact $|\mathcal M|^2$ at the mapped event from its compiled standalone at the probe event's own
$\alpha_s$ (the backend reproduces each parent's own pool exactly, median and largest $|\log|$ ratio $0$). In
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$, the per-probe mean removed (the level is the offset's business), median over the $20$ probes of each
set (\code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/factorised\_2to4.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:factorised_2to4}): the probe's own spread $7.1$
($+g$) and $6.2$ ($+\gamma$); after dividing by the parent's $|\mathcal M|^2$, $2.5$ and $3.1$; after also removing a
correction learned on the other $39$ probes (a gradient-boosted regression of the log ratio on the splitting variables
$s_{ak}/\hat s$, $s_{bk}/\hat s$, the nearest final's $s_{jk}/\hat s$, $p_{T,k}^2/\hat s$, $y_k$, $1-x$, $\hat s$, the
emitted flavour and the nearest emitter's kind; leave-one-probe-out), $0.73$ and $1.11$. The network's own $2\to4$ floor
(the variance of its zero-shot error, Fig.~\ref{fig:kshot_context}) is $1.34$ and $1.37$: the learned factorisation goes
under it on $17$ of the $20$ $+g$ probes and $14$ of the $20$ $+\gamma$. The two budgets differ: the floor is
the network's zero-shot error with no labels of the probe, while the correction learned the true $|\mathcal M|^2$ of
the other $39$ probes, so the comparison says what a head trained on other $2\to4$ processes could reach, not that the
zero-shot network is beaten at equal information. The six it misses: the four photon probes with
a charged lepton in the final state ($5.5$ to $20$, no better than the spread: a photon collinear to a final lepton is
not the initial-state emission the map assumes, and few other probes teach the correction that region), and two $+g$
probes with four coloured final particles ($3.8$, $1.9$). Without the four lepton probes the $+\gamma$ median is $0.98$.
With the network in place of the exact parent (\code{tools/factorised\_2to4\_net.py}: the context-vector run's own
prediction of each parent at the mapped events, the parent's $c_p$ from its table; its MSE there is small, median $0.14$
over the $40$ probes, $10$--$90\%$ $0.05$--$0.71$), the correction refitted the same way: $0.91$ ($+g$) and $1.26$
($+\gamma$), under the floor on $16$ and $11$ of the $20$. The factorised form keeps most of its gain with the network's
own $2\to3$; how far it carries beyond emitted gauge bosons (an added $Z$, $W$, $H$ or quark pair has no universal soft
or collinear factor) is a question for catalog\_v3, where the study moves (the user, 2026-10-25).

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/catalog_daniel/figs/factorised_2to4.pdf}
\caption{Each $2\to4$ probe's variance of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ (the per-probe mean removed): its own, after dividing by
its $2\to3$ parent's exact $|\mathcal M|^2$ at the mapped event, and after also removing a correction learned on the
other probes, against the network's zero-shot shape floor on the same probes. Left: a gluon added; right: a photon.}
\label{fig:factorised_2to4}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The $\sigma$ head on the held-out processes (2026-10-22).} The per-event $\sigma$ of the het loss, read on
the $139$ held-out processes (\code{tools/zero\_shot\_parent.py} now saves it;
\code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/catalog\_sigma\_heldout.py}; pulls $(q-t)/\sigma$ in
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$, the median over processes of each process's pull standard deviation and of its fraction of events
within one $\sigma$; a calibrated $\sigma$ gives $1$ and $0.68$). The per-process pretraining after its $\sigma$ stage, read with each
process's own statistics: $1.86$ and $0.38$ on the $96$ relabels, $13.5$ and $0.06$ on the $43$ new. The $\hat s$+coupling target after its $\sigma$
stage, on its own: $1.32$ and $0.58$ on the relabels, $13.3$ and $0.02$ on the new ($9.3$ with each process's level error
removed). The shared target at its best checkpoint (it blew up before a $\sigma$ stage), on its own: $0.66$ and $0.87$ on the relabels,
$1.20$ and $0.34$ on the new. The $\sigma$ stage calibrates $\sigma$ on the training processes, and it does not carry
to processes the model has not seen: on the new ones the errors are $10$ to $13$ times the $\sigma$ the model gives, so
$\sigma$ does not flag a process outside the training set. The shared target's $\sigma$, never recalibrated, is too wide on the relabels
and about the right size on the new processes, but its level errors put $66\%$ of the events outside one $\sigma$.

\paragraph{How robust is the diagram-descriptor level (2026-10-22, D83).} The $0.045$ of the exploration above (the shared target's level
of the $43$ new placed by a $2\times64$ network on the process features and the diagram descriptors) is one draw
(\code{analysis/catalog\_daniel/norm\_explore4.py}, level error $(\hat m_p-m_p)^2/\sigma_p^2$, median over the $43$).
Ten ensembles of three seeds give a median of $0.087$, ranging from $0.016$ to $0.19$ (the $90\%$ point $1.06$); other
sizes give $0.04$ to $0.10$ at width $128$ or $64$ and $0.34$ to $0.53$ at width $32$ or three layers. Cross-fitted by
relabelling class on the training processes it is $0.12$; cross-fitted by multiplicity (a network that never saw a
$2\to2$ process placing the $2\to2$ ones, and the same for $2\to3$) it is $10.4$, so the descriptors interpolate between
processes of a seen multiplicity and do not extrapolate in it. For comparison, the level read off the first $k$ events
of each held-out process: $0.059$ at $k=10$ and $0.0067$ at $k=100$ (the sampling error of a $k$-event mean, $\approx1/k$).
So ten events of a new process place its level as well as the best data-free map, and a hundred do $7$ to $13\times$
better (against the single draw and the ensembles' median); what a pretraining has to learn from the particles is the
shape, whose share of each process's error is a median $0.62$ for the shared target on the new processes (the pretrainings paragraph above).

\paragraph{Generation cost.} A timing prebuild of $17$ representative processes at small
pools (one chunk per core, the mixture policy on) gives the per-core rates: LO trees
$2$--$15\times10^3$ stored events per second ($2\to2$ fastest, the four-fermion and
$t\bar t gg$ $2\to4$ finals slowest) even though the mixture labels six candidates per
stored event; one-loop $2\to2$ and massive $2\to3$ $150$--$800$ events per second; the
gluonic one-loop $2\to3$ entries ($u\bar u\to Zgg$, $u\bar u\to u\bar u g$, $u\bar u\to
t\bar t g$) $50$--$100$ events per second, so their $5\times10^4$-event train pools take $8$--$17$
minutes each and set the tail of every prebuild. At the pool sizes adopted ($10^5$ events per
LO process, $5\times10^4$ per one-loop process, a tenth of each for val and test) the whole
$478$-entry recipe is about six CPU-hours of generation plus the $432$ backend compiles,
i.e.\ well under an hour of wall on one $48$-core \code{htc} node and no GPU, so the sizes
were not worth trimming. Two things bit on the way: \code{--auto-workers} read the node's
$512$ logical CPUs instead of the $48$-core allocation and launched $432$ MadGraph outputs
at once, and with the default OpenBLAS pools every MadLoop subprocess counted $512$
threads against the per-user process limit of $1024$, so forks failed with \code{EAGAIN}
after $9281$ of $9569$ chunks. The job scripts now pin one BLAS thread per process, the
worker count comes from the SLURM allocation, a chunk already on disk with the right row
count is reused across reruns, and a transient fork failure retries the chunk instead of
aborting the pool. The rerun picked up the $9281$ chunks and finished the recipe in $25$
minutes: $478$ train pools of $10^5$ ($5\times10^4$ at one loop) events, val and test
pools of a tenth each, $11$\,GB of train data and $2.5$\,GB frozen, every amplitude finite
and non-zero across the $4.5\times10^7$ train events; the $14$ hold-outs and $5$
interpolation hold-outs were generated the same way.

\paragraph{Audit of the generated pools.} Since the $448$-run failures were distributional
(Sec.~\ref{sec:bigrun}), every train and val pool was audited before training
(\code{analysis/catalog\_v2/check\_pools.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:pool_audit}; it scans every
train pool on disk, $508$ with the hold-outs and the leftovers of earlier short recipes): the sampling
policy actually recorded, the share of events within $10$\,GeV of $M_Z$ where the window
contains it, the flatness of the $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ histogram (largest over smallest bin
count across the central $98\%$) and the standardized-log tail. Every train pool of the recipe
carries the mixture: the $2\to2$ $s$-channel pools that lost the $Z$ needle last time now put
$13$--$19\%$ of their events within $10$\,GeV of the pole ($ee\to\mu\mu$ $17\%$, $ee\to u\bar
u$ $19\%$, against $2.6\%$ flat), no mixture pool has a standardized-log event beyond $6\sigma$
(median maximum $3.4\sigma$, where the flat pools reached $7\sigma$), and the flatness sits
at a median of $10$ (the $m_t$ family $6$, the $M_Z$ family $20$, the worst the leptonic
$Z$-peak pools at $65$, halved from their flat versions). The one gap the audit found was
the $14$ hold-outs: their recipe had no sampling header, so their train pools were flat while
the scan hold-outs were shaped; the header is now written by the generator and the pools
regenerated. At the time of this audit val and test were flat by construction, so it reports
them only for window coverage, which is complete everywhere; they have since been shaped like
train (Sec.~\ref{sec:catalog}, the sampling policy).

\paragraph{The needle is not in the data.} The audit above measured the sampling, not
the learning outcome, and the first catalog\_v2 wave (Sec.~\ref{sec:catv2-census}) found the
$s$-channel $2\to2$ family still far above the bulk on the flat validation measure. The check that
should have preceded the sweep is a learnability probe, and it is decisive: the seven
$s$-channel pools ($ee\to\mu\mu,\tau\tau,u\bar u,d\bar d,b\bar b,\nu_\mu\bar\nu_\mu,
\nu_e\bar\nu_e$) trained \emph{alone} for $3000$ steps at $\mathrm{bs}=4096$ (lr
$4.1\times10^{-3}$, the catalog encoding) reach $2$--$4\times10^{-4}$ on their flat validation
pools, the pure-$Z$ $ee\to\nu_\mu\bar\nu_\mu$ included, which sat at $0.56$ in the
$478$-process run. The same $ee\to u\bar u$ with the same hyperparameters and horizon reached
only $0.27$ inside a twelve-process recipe of four-quark families
(\code{recipes/collision\_ab.yaml}). So the pools support the resonance and the residual
failure is an interaction with the other processes in the batch, not a sampling defect;
the suspect is the loss aggregation (Prin.~\ref{prin:geomean}: the geometric mean scales a
process's gradient by the inverse of its own MSE, so the worst-fitted processes get the
least push).

\paragraph{The aggregation is the cause.} The twelve-process recipe rerun with the
arithmetic mean (\code{loss\_aggregation: mean}), everything else identical (same seed,
horizon, batch and learning rate; \code{scripts/job\_needle\_probe.sh} with \code{AGG=mean}),
settles the question (Table~\ref{tab:agg_ab}). $ee\to u\bar u$ goes from $0.27$ to
$5.7\times10^{-4}$, i.e.\ to what the family reaches alone, and the two one-loop processes
the geometric mean had left at $10^{-3}$ come down by $25\times$; the price is a factor
$2$--$4$ on the processes the geometric mean had pushed furthest ($10^{-5}$ to a few
$10^{-5}$). Under the arithmetic mean every process ends within $[2\times10^{-5},
6\times10^{-4}]$; under the geometric mean the spread is five decades. This is
Prin.~\ref{prin:gradcomp} seen directly: a laggard's gradient is divided by its own large
loss, so in the joint run it never gets the steps the resonance needs, and no amount of sampling
can hand them back. The wave-0 census (Sec.~\ref{sec:catv2-census}), where the $s$-channel and $M_Z$ pools sit at $0.04$--$0.6$ while the bulk is at $10^{-2}$ and below,
is the same mechanism at $478$ processes. What to aggregate with at that scale is a decision
for the re-measure wave (Sec.~\ref{sec:catalog-open}); the geometric mean's rationale (a
$2\to4$ process at $10^{-2}$ must not swamp a $2\to2$ at $10^{-6}$) still holds, but a
$2\to2$ needle at $0.5$ is above every $2\to4$ plateau, so on this catalog the arithmetic
mean's emphasis lands on the failures.

\paragraph{The floor at the bulk's scale does nothing.} The $\tau$-floored geometric mean
(\code{training.loss\_aggregation\_tau}), proposed for the $448$ run as the minimal repair of
Prin.~\ref{prin:gradcomp} with $\tau$ set just above where the bulk converges, was run on the
same recipe at $\tau=10^{-4}$ (third column of Table~\ref{tab:agg_ab}): $ee\to u\bar u$ stays at
$0.30$, the two one-loop laggards stay at $10^{-3}$, and the bulk ends $2$--$4\times$ worse
than under the exact geometric mean. The weights say why. The floor caps a converged process
at $1/\tau$ but leaves the laggard at $1/m_{\rm lag}$, so their ratio is $m_{\rm lag}/\tau$,
here $3000$ against $30\,000$ unfloored; a factor of ten out of four decades does not change
who gets the steps. The floor acts only once $\tau$ reaches the laggard's own scale, and then
it is the arithmetic mean for every process below $\tau$. So $\tau$ is not a repair with a
natural value but the threshold that decides which processes count as done, with a useful
range of $10^{-3}$ to $1$ on this catalog; it is a hyperparameter of the wave (hand-off,
Sec.~\ref{sec:catalog-open}).
