\begin{principle} A flat-sampled surrogate does not extrapolate \emph{into} an unseen
divergence: held out, $e^+e^-\to u\bar u g$'s soft/collinear region is mispredicted by
$\sim\!e^{3}$ in the deepest bin and the model saturates against a ceiling. But the
divergence is \emph{data-recoverable}: adding back only $5\%$ of the divergent-region
events cuts the MSE $\sim\!24\times$, and the add-back curve then saturates toward the
in-support baseline.
\end{principle}

\subsection{Two extensions: the hardest process, and separating the two limits}
\label{sec:div-extensions}

Because the leave-out base held out \emph{all five} gluon processes, both extensions below
reuse the same base22 foundation with \emph{no new pretraining}; only the add-back
fine-tunes differ.

\paragraph{The hardest process ($u\bar u gg$) is also data-recoverable.} On the same
$y_{\min}<c$ ($15$th-percentile) held-out region (Fig.~\ref{fig:div_addback_uugg}), pure
extrapolation on the six-particle $u\bar u gg$ gives MSE $0.22$ (deepest $y_{\min}<10^{-3}$
bin $0.98$), and adding the divergent region back drives it monotonically to $0.024$ at
$f\!=\!1$ ($\sim\!9\times$). Two differences from $u\bar u g$ mark it as the harder process:
recovery is \emph{gradual} rather than the sharp $24\times$-by-$f{=}0.05$ drop (only
$2.4\times$ at $f{=}0.05$), and the in-support floor is $\sim\!10\times$ higher ($0.024$ vs
$0.0028$). But unlike $u\bar u g$ the $f{=}0$ model does \emph{not} flatly plateau: having
learned the process from the FAR region it tracks the analytic $1/y_{\min}$ ramp through most
of the plane, degrading only in the very deepest soft$+$collinear corner, a partial, not
total, extrapolation failure.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{addback_curve_uugg.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{$e^+e^-\to u\bar u gg$ add-back curve} (hardest process, same base22).
  Left: held-out deep-IR ($y_{\min}<c$) MSE/MAE vs add-back fraction $f$; right: MSE split
  by $y_{\min}$ sub-bin. The divergence is data-recoverable ($0.22\to0.024$) but recovery is
  more gradual and the floor $\sim\!10\times$ higher than $u\bar u g$: the harder process
  needs more in-region data.}
  \label{fig:div_addback_uugg}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Which limit is harder to extrapolate into: soft vs collinear.} Splitting the
$u\bar u g$ hold-out into a \emph{pure-soft} region ($x_g<c$, any angle) and a
\emph{pure-collinear} region ($y_{\min}<c$ at hard $x_g$, soft pole off) separates the two IR
limits (Fig.~\ref{fig:div_soft_coll}), giving two findings. (i) Both single-limit cuts are
far easier to extrapolate into than the $y_{\min}$ cut that \emph{conflates} them: $f{=}0$
MSE is $0.076$ (soft) and $0.047$ (collinear) versus $0.71$ for the combined cut: the
difficulty lives in the soft$\cap$collinear \emph{overlap}, not either limit alone. (ii) A
crossover: the collinear pole is easier to \emph{extrapolate into} (lower $f{=}0$ error, its
DGLAP structure being partly inferable from the FAR region), but the soft pole \emph{recovers
faster} once any in-region data is added: by $f{=}0.05$ soft ($0.008$) has overtaken
collinear ($0.014$) and stays below it to $f{=}1$. The eikonal $1/x_g^2$ enhancement is hard
to guess but simple to learn from a few examples; the collinear residue is guessable but
richer (the full $P_{qq}(z)$), hence slower to pin down.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{soft_vs_coll_addback.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{Soft vs collinear extrapolation, $e^+e^-\to u\bar u g$.} Held-out-region
  MSE vs add-back fraction for the pure-soft ($x_g<c$) and pure-collinear ($y_{\min}<c$ at
  hard $x_g$) cuts. Collinear is easier to extrapolate into ($f{=}0$), but soft recovers
  faster and crosses below it once any in-region data is added back.}
  \label{fig:div_soft_coll}
\end{figure}

\begin{principle} The difficulty of extrapolating into a divergence is concentrated in the
soft$\cap$collinear \emph{overlap}: single-limit hold-outs ($x_g$ or $1-x_q$ alone) are an
order of magnitude easier ($f{=}0$ MSE $\sim\!0.05$--$0.08$) than the combined $y_{\min}$ cut
($0.71$). Across limits, collinear is easier to extrapolate but soft is easier to recover
with a little data; and the hardest process ($u\bar u gg$) is data-recoverable but needs more
in-region data than $u\bar u g$.
\end{principle}

\subsection{Deeper/cleverer sampling, optimal allocation, and heteroscedastic
uncertainty}\label{sec:div-sampling}

\emph{Settled picture of this line of work. The training-density question resolves into three
layers, which we call \textbf{coverage}, \textbf{emphasis} and \textbf{steering}.
\textbf{Coverage is the dominant lever, and it must be \emph{generated}, not resampled}: a fixed pool
has a coverage ceiling (resampling only duplicates the few extreme events), so a divergence is fixed
by importance-\emph{generating} fresh events, shaped flat-in-$\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$ (the general,
process-agnostic criterion); the deployable density is a uniform$+$flat \emph{mixture} at a modest
fraction ($f\!\approx\!0.25$--$0.5$), not pure flat. Validated on two structurally-distinct
singularities: the $u\bar u g$ soft/collinear IR (antenna sampler) and the $ee\to u\bar u$
$s$-channel $Z$ resonance (fraction sweep). \textbf{Emphasis ($\sigma$-reweighting) is a scoped,
second-order layer}: the coupled $(\mu,\sigma)$ fit fails for a structural reason
and is replaced by a two-stage recipe (train $\mu$, then fit a frozen-$\mu$ $\sigma$ head); the
resulting $\sigma$ helps \emph{only where coverage is capped}: on a starved, un-regenerable pool it
is robustly ${\approx}$oracle ($3$-seed), on a base you can already generate well it is a null.
Equimarginal (water-filling) allocation frames the optimum ($p^\star\!\propto\!\sqrt{q\cdot
\mathrm{diff}}$), consistent with the interior mixture. \textbf{Steering ($\sigma$-driven
on-the-fly generation)} beats uniform generation on a coordinate-free loop, and helps \emph{iff} the hardness is
\emph{concentrated} (the concentration principle), with three sharpening results: (i) the learned
$\sigma$ rises inside every divergence, and its base-arm \emph{contrast}
$\langle\sigma\rangle_{\rm sing}/\langle\sigma\rangle_{\rm bulk}$ is a one-forward-pass \emph{pre-flight}
predictor of the steering gain (no A/B needed); (ii) it transfers to a \emph{massive} final state
($b\bar b g$), where the contrast correctly predicted a $Z$-localised gain and the $m_b$-dead-cone
softening of the IR, in advance; (iii) the keep rule's shape is settled, since a nested polynomial confirms
\emph{positive curvature} is the right direction but does not robustly beat the simple $\sigma^\gamma$
temperature over seeds, and the $\sigma$-tail \emph{floors} at a level $8\times$ more data does not move
(at fixed optimizer budget, so the binding constraint is capacity, not coverage), giving a stopping
rule for how much to generate. Tooling under \code{analysis/divergences/};
heteroscedastic code on worktree \code{worktrees/wt-heterosc} (branch \code{heterosc}). Remaining/future
work is collected in the hand-off, Sec.~\ref{sec:div-open}.}

\paragraph{Deeper and antenna phase-space sampling.} The production
$u\bar u g$ set is flat \textsc{rambo} (uniform in Lorentz-invariant phase space), so the deep
IR is starved (median $y_{\min}\!\approx\!0.15$; only $\sim$5 of $10^6$ events below
$y_{\min}=10^{-5}$). \code{gen\_uug\_sampling.py} adds two samplers over a lowered-cut fiducial
region ($p_T\!>\!1$, $m\!>\!0.3$, $\Delta R\!>\!0.05$): \emph{antenna}
($\mathrm{d}N\!\propto\!\mathrm{d}a/a\,\mathrm{d}b/b$ in the gluon invariants $a=1-x_u$,
$b=1-x_{\bar u}$, so $y_{\min}=\min(a,b)$ reaches $<10^{-6}$) and \emph{mixture} (fraction $f$
antenna $+\,(1-f)$ uniform). Labels use the compiled mg5 C++ standalone at fixed
$\alpha_s=0.118$, verified bit-identical to the production data; since we fit
$\ln|\mathcal{M}|^2$ pointwise, importance sampling needs \emph{no} target reweighting: only
the density of training points changes. Data: \code{data\_deep\_\{uniform,antenna,mixture,
mix025,mix075\}/}; common deep-IR test \code{uug\_deep\_test.npz}.
A/B (same base22, 400k events, 4000 steps; \code{finetune\_deep\_sampling.sh},
\code{eval\_deep.sh}, \code{plot\_deep\_sampling.py}): antenna crushes the pole, with median
relative error in $y_{\min}\!\in\![10^{-6},10^{-5})$ drops $47.9\%\!\to\!1.4\%$; overall
MSE$(\Delta\ln|\mathcal{M}|^2)$ $0.108\!\to\!0.0017$ ($\sim$62$\times$; Fig.~\ref{fig:deep_sampling_antenna}). The fraction sweep
$f\in\{0,.25,.5,.75,1\}$ (\code{plot\_fraction\_sweep.py}, fig
\code{deep\_sampling\_fraction\_sweep}) shows a \emph{broad interior optimum} $f\in[0.25,0.75]$:
$f{=}0$ catastrophic ($\sim$200$\times$), $f{=}1$ $\sim$2$\times$ worse than the basin; the fine
location inside the basin is single-seed noise ($f{=}0.5$ sits above both neighbours). Metrics
reported: MSE and MAE of $\Delta\ln|\mathcal{M}|^2$ (both scale-invariant, since the residual is
$\ln$ of the amplitude ratio) plus median relative error.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \pnl{deep_sampling_uniform_vs_antenna_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{deep_sampling_uniform_vs_antenna_b.pdf} \\[1ex]
  \includegraphics{deep_sampling_uniform_vs_antenna_c.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{Antenna sampling crushes the pole ($e^+e^-\to u\bar u g$).} Deep-IR error
  for the production flat-\textsc{rambo} set vs the antenna sampler, same base22 / $400$k events /
  $4000$ steps. Flat \textsc{rambo} starves the deep IR by construction (median
  $y_{\min}\!\approx\!0.15$); the antenna proposal, which is flat per decade in the gluon invariants,
  takes the median relative error in $y_{\min}\!\in\![10^{-6},10^{-5})$ from $47.9\%$ to $1.4\%$ and the
  overall MSE from $0.108$ to $0.0017$ ($\sim\!62\times$). Because we fit $\ln|\mathcal M|^2$
  pointwise, this needs \emph{no} importance reweighting: only where the training points sit changes.}
  \label{fig:deep_sampling_antenna}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{deep_sampling_fraction_sweep.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{The antenna/uniform mixture has a broad interior optimum.} Deep-IR error vs
  the antenna fraction $f$. Both endpoints are bad: $f{=}0$ (pure \textsc{rambo}) is catastrophic
  ($\sim\!200\times$) and $f{=}1$ (pure antenna) is $\sim\!2\times$ worse than the basin, because it
  starves the $O(1)$ bulk, while any $f\in[0.25,0.75]$ sits in a flat basin. The fine location inside
  the basin is single-seed noise. This ``mix the coverage criterion in at a modest fraction, do not adopt
  it as the base'' shape recurs for the $Z$ resonance (Fig.~\ref{fig:eeuu_genmix_fraction_sweep}).}
  \label{fig:deep_sampling_fraction_sweep}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Float32 label floor.} $s_{ij}=s\,y_{\min}$ is a near-cancellation of $O(s)$ momenta,
so float32 storage imposes a relative floor $\sim\varepsilon/y_{\min}$: measured (recomputing
$|\mathcal{M}|^2$ from float32- vs float64-rounded momenta) at median $0.39\%$ in
$[10^{-6},10^{-5})$, $\times{\sim}8$ per decade, negligible by $10^{-3}$. All current models sit
well \emph{above} this (short trainings), so they are optimisation-limited, not precision-limited. If ever
precision-limited, reparametrise (feed $\ln y_{\min}$ / pair masses as features) rather than store
float64.

\paragraph{Optimal allocation of a fixed data budget.} For a fixed data
budget the optimum is the equimarginal (water-filling) allocation: sample until the marginal
(test-weighted) error reduction is equal everywhere, $p^\star\!\propto\!\sqrt{q\cdot\text{diff}}$
for test weighting $q$, neither uniform nor pure antenna, consistent with the interior optimum
above. An online adaptive per-$y_{\min}$-bin importance sampler was prototyped
(\code{adaptive\_ir.py}, branch \code{adaptive-ir}); the controller math is CPU-validated
(unbiased IS correction with mean weight $=1$; variance-optimal proposal
$\pi_b\!\propto\!Q_b\sqrt{E_b}$). It is \emph{not} wired into the training loop. Caveat: the
variance-reduction proposal ($\pi\!\propto\!Q\sqrt{E}$, fixed objective, faster convergence) is
\emph{not} the finite-data allocation optimum ($\pi\!\propto\!\sqrt{QC}$). Deprioritised in favour
of the heteroscedastic head below.

\paragraph{The heteroscedastic $(\mu,\sigma)$ head.}
Goal: a learned per-event uncertainty $\sigma(x)$ as an attention/sampling signal
(process/divergence-agnostic, no hand-binning). All on branch \code{heterosc}.
\emph{Wiring:} HETEROSC existed for the MLP configs but was never wired for the $\mu$P
LLoCa net; now the net emits $2\times$\code{out\_shape} channels (mean, softplus $\sigma$) when
\code{loss=HETEROSC} (\code{models/lloca.py}); \code{net.loss} interpolates \code{training.loss};
$\sigma$ is split out in \code{\_batch\_loss\_lloca}; \code{reset\_output\_head} shape-filters the
1ch$\to$2ch load; \code{extract\_sigma.py} re-asserts the worktree on \code{sys.path} (hydra
imports \code{models.lloca} lazily and the analysis helpers prepend the main repo).
\emph{$\beta$-NLL:} \code{training.heterosc\_beta} weights the NLL by detached
$\sigma^{2\beta}$ (Seitzer 2022); $\beta{=}1$ restores $\mu$'s MSE-like gradient.
\emph{Bugs found+fixed:} (i) \textbf{geometric\_mean aggregation is invalid for the signed NLL}:
$\log(\text{clip})$ of negative per-process losses zeros the gradient of every well-fit process, so
$\mu$ collapses to the mean; forced arithmetic mean for HETEROSC in both the training aggregate
(\code{\_aggregate\_per\_process\_loss}) and the 22-proc validation combine (checkpoint selection).
This was the dominant failure ($\mu$-MSE $\sim$1 across \emph{all} configs, including $\beta{=}1$).
(ii) \code{float(mse\_val)} in the base \code{\_validate}/\code{\_step} consumers (single-dataset
finetunes delegate there; a detached CUDA tensor crashed \code{np.mean}). \emph{Foundation
sweep:} \code{sweep\_heterosc\_foundation.sh} (same 22-proc leave-uug-out recipe as base22,
\code{loss=HETEROSC}, lr$\in\{4\mathrm{e}{-}3,1.6\mathrm{e}{-}2\}\times\beta\in\{0,.5,1\}$, 6000
iters). After the fix $\mu$ fits: best lr$=4\mathrm{e}{-}3$, $\beta{=}1$ gives in-distribution val
$\mu$-MSE $=0.025$, and $\beta$ dominates ($\beta1\!\ll\!\beta0.5\!\ll\!\beta0$, a $\sim$9$\times$
gap $\Rightarrow$ the Seitzer $\sigma$-inflation effect is real). Rank: \code{rank\_mu\_mse.py}.

\paragraph{The coupled fit fails for a structural reason; two-stage $\sigma$ works. (Withdrawn 2026-10-15: the mechanism below is wrong, see the correction that follows.)}
The coupled $(\mu,\sigma)$ NLL underfit $\mu$ ($\mu$-MSE $\sim$1 or $\gg$ the MSE baseline) across
$\sim$40 GPU-h of runs and three sweeps. Mechanism (\code{grad\_decomp.py}, \code{clip\_test.sh}):
amplitudes are \emph{deterministic} (no aleatoric noise), so the Gaussian likelihood is degenerate
(true $\sigma\!\to\!0$) and at $\beta{=}0$ the $\sigma$-readout gradient
$\sigma^{-1}(1-r^2/\sigma^2)$ \emph{blows up} as $\sigma$ shrinks; \code{clip\_grad\_norm} is a
\textbf{global} norm, so it throttles the \emph{whole} step ($\mu$ included) to $\sim$10\% on
nearly every step (median grad-norm 51 vs clip 5, binds 97.5\% of steps). The earlier
\code{clip\_test} missed this because it ran at $\beta{=}1$, where $\sigma$'s gradient is damped.
\emph{Fix: two-stage} (\code{twostage\_sigma.sh}, \code{twostage\_sigma2.sh}, \code{calib\_eval.py}):
train $\mu$ with plain MSE, freeze trunk$+\mu$ (\code{training.heterosc\_sigma\_only}), then train
\emph{only} the 258-param $\sigma$ readout row at $\beta{=}0$ (a proper scoring rule with $\mu$
fixed). Result: \textbf{calibrated $\sigma$ at zero accuracy cost}: pull std $1.0$, reliability
slope $\sim$1.1, $\mu$-MSE bit-identical to the MSE model, on both the deep-IR uug finetune and the
22-proc base22 foundation (which tracks error across $4.5$ decades of per-process scale). Cost:
$\sim$3 min (single process), $\sim$31 min (base22). So the NLL loss and $\sigma$ head were never
broken; they must not be trained \emph{jointly} with $\mu$. A naming trap worth recording: Seitzer's
$\beta$-NLL is $\mathrm{detach}(\sigma^{2\beta})\!\cdot\!\text{NLL}$, so $\beta{=}0$ is the standard
Gaussian NLL (works); the catastrophic runs were all $\beta{=}1$.

\paragraph{Correction (2026-10-15): fitting $\mu$ and $\sigma$ together works for $\mu$ on one process; calibration is open.}
The ``deterministic, hence degenerate'' mechanism was a hypothesis (commit \code{ba2c0ae}) and is
withdrawn. The failures trace to choices made on top of the user's loss (\code{losses.py:32-35},
commit \code{4ef5b76}: the plain Gaussian NLL, $\sigma$ through a softplus, selection on the NLL):
the $\beta$-NLL pinned at $\beta{=}1$, selection on that $\beta$-NLL (minimised at $r^*=1/e$), the
geometric mean of a signed NLL across processes, and short horizons (commits \code{49cfe64}, \code{cbae458},
\code{da17ce9}); the global gradient clip, shared by $\mu$ and $\sigma$, is a further suspect. The first clean joint run of the
user's loss (\code{training.loss=HETEROSC}, \code{heterosc\_beta=0}, the new
\code{training.heterosc\_select=nll}; \code{scripts/job\_het\_ab.sh}) on $e^+e^-\to t\bar t$ at
$D=10^4$, $8$k steps, seed 2, at the MSE baseline's own HPs (\code{tp\_scr\_ee\_ttbar\_d8/trial\_0144},
the best of its 8-trial DyHPO sweep), V100, \code{sign\_head} off (a positive probe): $\mu$-MSE (val, standardized
$\log|\mathcal M|^2$) $1.37\times10^{-7}$ against the MSE baseline's $3.57\times10^{-7}$, i.e.\
$2.6\times$ lower error; test $6.4\times10^{-8}$, mean $|\Delta|=10^{-4}$. The $\mu$-MSE falls steadily
to the last step, which is also the best checkpoint. On the test split (\code{analysis/transfer/het\_ab.py}, figures
\code{het\_ab\_delta}, \code{het\_ab\_pull}, \code{het\_ab\_calib}; Fig.~\ref{fig:het_ab}) the median $|\Delta|$ is
$5.0\times10^{-5}$ against the MSE model's $1.4\times10^{-4}$. Calibration is not there yet: pull mean
$0.00$, std $0.46$, so $\sigma$ is about $2\times$ too large; $\sigma$ barely varies ($\approx4$ to
$6\times10^{-4}$ over its deciles) while the actual error spans $10^{-4}$ to $6\times10^{-4}$, so it
ranks events only weakly; the NLL was still falling at $8$k steps. One seed, one probe, HPs tuned for MSE. A first submission failed on
\code{sign\_head} with \code{HETEROSC} (asserted incompatible) and left an empty
\code{runs/zz\_het\_ab/tp\_scr\_ee\_ttbar\_d8\_\_trial\_0144\_s1}.

\caption{The user's heteroscedastic loss against the MSE baseline on $e^+e^-\to t\bar t$, $D=10^4$, test split, one
seed. Left: pulls $\Delta/\sigma$ (standard deviation $0.46$, a calibrated model has $1$). Right: predicted $\sigma$
against the realised error per decile of $\sigma$.}

\paragraph{The GP sweep of the user's loss (2026-10-15, D57).} The same setup swept for its own HPs: the MSE baseline's
config verbatim (\code{sweep/sweep\_config\_tp\_het\_ee\_ttbar\_d8.yaml}) with \code{HETEROSC}, $\beta=0$, selection and
ranking on the validation NLL, a plain GP over $2000$ candidates (the likelihoods project's surrogate, now
\code{dyhpo.surrogate: gp}), $8$ trials in waves $3+3+2$ through \code{sweep/drive.py} (the user's answers: ``NLL'',
``GP, 8 trials, 2000 candidates''). Before it, a signed objective was a silent random search: \code{-log10} mapped every
NLL $\le0$ to one label (fixed by \code{dyhpo.objective\_scale}, commit \code{8d31b58}). Best trial \code{hp1550}: lr
$2.3\times10^{-3}$, EMA off, validation NLL $-7.63$. On the test split, in MSE$(\log|\mathcal M|^2)$ from the raw
predictions (\code{tools/eval\_delta.py}, V100): $2.0\times10^{-8}$ against the MSE baseline's $9.6\times10^{-8}$
($4.8\times$ lower; the one run at the baseline's HPs: $2.3\times10^{-8}$); median $|\Delta|$ $4.4\times10^{-5}$ against
$1.4\times10^{-4}$ (\code{analysis/transfer/het\_ab.py --sweep}, figures \code{het\_ab\_sweep\_*}). Five of the eight
trials beat the baseline's test error ($2.0$ to $4.8\times$ lower); the three with lr $\le3.1\times10^{-4}$ are $4$ to
$6\times$ worse than it. Calibration is unchanged: pull
std $0.42$, $\sigma$ $\approx2\times$ the realised error and nearly flat ($3.7$ to $5.7\times10^{-4}$ over its deciles,
against errors from $7\times10^{-5}$ to $6\times10^{-4}$), monotone in it. Every trial selected its last step: the NLL
falls to $8$k (\code{analysis/transfer/het\_sweep.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:het_sweep}), so the horizon is short for this loss.
Scored with the validation weights (\code{training.track\_train\_eval}, D58), the training split sits at or just below
validation; the earlier validation-below-training curve was the EMA weights against the live ones.

\paragraph{The het loss trained longer (D61).} \code{hp1550} retrained at $8$k, $16$k and $32$k steps, all seed $1$, no sweep, with the
cosine spanning the whole run (\code{scripts/job\_het\_ab.sh}; a first $16$k/$32$k attempt kept the trial's
\code{increment\_steps}, restarted the cosine at $8$k and is discarded). Each run is best at its last step again
(Fig.~\ref{fig:het_long}, top). Test MSE$(\log|\mathcal M|^2)$: $2.9\times10^{-8}$, $1.2\times10^{-8}$,
$5.1\times10^{-9}$; median $|\Delta|$ $6.2$, $1.9$, $1.2\times10^{-5}$. The MSE baseline at the same length:
$9.6\times10^{-8}$ at $8$k (\code{tp\_scr\_ee\_ttbar\_d8} trial $144$), $1.7\times10^{-8}$ at $32$k
(\code{tp3\_scr32k\_ee\_ttbar\_d8}, the better of its two chosen points on validation); the het loss stays
$3.3\times$ and $3.2\times$ lower. The $8$k run at seed $1$ ($2.9\times10^{-8}$) against the sweep trial at seed $42$
($2.0\times10^{-8}$) is compatible with the seed spread (two seeds). Calibration does improve with length (Fig.~\ref{fig:het_long}, bottom): at $8$k
$\sigma$ spans $1.6\times$ over its deciles while the error spans $8\times$; at $32$k $\sigma$ spans $4.5\times$, the
error $30\times$, and the deciles lie across the diagonal (median $\sigma$ over the RMS error $1.68$, $0.89$, $0.60$ at
$8$k, $16$k, $32$k; pull std $0.42$, $0.44$, $0.60$). $\sigma$ still varies less than the error: about $5\times$ too large
on the best-predicted decile and $1.5\times$ too small on the worst. Since every horizon is still best at its last step, we
speculate that $\sigma$ is calibrated only late because of the schedule: the NLL keeps spiking while the lr is high and
$\sigma$ appears to resolve the per-event error only in the cosine's tail; no run tests this (\code{analysis/transfer/het\_long.py}).

\paragraph{Recalibrating $\sigma$ (2026-10-15).} The user: ``I believe the calibration is something we need to figure
out before we continue, try to force it to be well calibrated''. The raw $\sigma$ ranks the events correctly (the
deciles are monotone) but its range is compressed. A two-parameter map $\sigma'=a\,\sigma^b$, fitted by the Gaussian NLL
on the validation split and scored on test, calibrates every horizon (Fig.~\ref{fig:het_recal}): $b=3.9$, $2.1$, $2.1$ at
$8$k, $16$k, $32$k; test pull std $1.00$, $1.01$, $0.97$ (raw $0.42$, $0.44$, $0.60$); the RMS error over $\sigma'$
per decile within $0.79$ to $1.15$ at $32$k ($0.76$ to $1.47$ at $16$k, $0.89$ to $1.34$ at $8$k); test NLL lower by
$0.65$, $0.67$, $0.42$. The tails are heavier than Gaussian: $|{\rm pull}|<1$ holds for $0.72$ to $0.74$ of the events
against $0.68$, with a few events beyond $4$. $\mu$ is unchanged, so the accuracy numbers above stand
(\code{analysis/transfer/het\_long.py}, figure \code{het\_long\_recal}). The map is a measurement of the miscalibration, not a fix: a Gaussian NLL trained
to convergence should be calibrated without one. Diagnosis on the $32$k run: $\sigma$ is as compressed on the training
split it was fitted on (pull std $0.59$, deciles $0.22$ to $1.42$), so this is not overfitting. Gradient clipping binds on
every step of every het run (pre-clip median norm $417$ at $8$k, $731$ at $32$k, against \code{clip\_grad\_norm}$=5$; the
MSE baseline binds on $4\%$), but it is not the cause: at $8$k and the same HPs, clip $1000$ ($14\%$ of steps) and no
clipping leave $\sigma$ as compressed (test pull std $0.72$, $0.83$; worst decile $2.0$, $2.6$) and $\mu$ far worse
(val MSE $8.2\times10^{-6}$, $4.7\times10^{-5}$ against $1.7\times10^{-7}$; the lr was found under full clipping, so this
does not rank the clip values). Training only the $\sigma$ readout on the frozen $32$k trunk and $\mu$
(\code{heterosc\_sigma\_only}, $4$k steps, no L2, clipping never binds) calibrates it: test pull std $1.03$,
$|{\rm pull}|<1$ for $0.69$ of the events, deciles $0.65$ to $1.24$ except the worst at $2.4$; validation NLL $-9.68$
against $-9.18$, $\mu$ unchanged. So the features carry the error, and joint training leaves $\sigma$ fitted to errors
$\mu$ has since outgrown. The same refit with the trial's L2 ($\lambda=1.6\times10^{-8}$) ends at the same validation NLL
($-9.68005$ against $-9.68008$), so L2 on the readout plays no part; L2 on the trunk during joint training is not excluded
but is of the same size. A DyHPO sweep with \code{clip\_grad\_norm} searched (D68, the user: ``maybe sweeping the value is
better?''; \code{sweep\_config\_tp\_het\_clip\_ee\_ttbar\_d8.yaml}, the het sweep with the clip log-uniform in $[1,1000]$,
8 trials) ranks the clip with lr: the four best trials all clip on $100\%$ of steps (clip $1.0$ to $4.3$, validation NLL
$-7.52$ to $-7.25$), the four with clip $152$ to $769$ reach $-7.09$ to $-5.93$. Its best ($-7.52$, clip $1.04$, lr
$4.4\times10^{-3}$, EMA on) does not beat the fixed-clip sweep's $-7.63$, so the fixed $5$ stands. The short $\sigma$-only stage
(D69, \code{sweep\_config\_tp\_het\_sigonly\_ee\_ttbar\_d8.yaml}: $250$ steps, its lr and warmup searched, 8 trials) reaches
validation NLL $-9.764$ at \code{training.lr} $0.097$, against $-9.680$ for the $4$k-step refit at the trial's lr $2.3\times10^{-3}$
(both are fine-tunes of the joint model, so \code{fine\_tune.lr\_scale} $0.1$ multiplies them: the effective rates are
$9.7\times10^{-3}$ and $2.3\times10^{-4}$); the six trials with lr $\approx0.1$ are within $0.01$ of their final NLL by step $150$ to $170$ and past the $4$k refit by step
$100$. The best lr sits at the top of the searched range. Its calibration on test (Fig.~\ref{fig:het_sigonly}, \code{analysis/transfer/het\_sigonly.py}): pull std
$1.02$, $|{\rm pull}|<1$ for $0.67$ of the events ($0.68$ for a Gaussian), the RMS error over $\sigma$ per decile $0.73$
to $1.22$ except the worst-predicted decile at $2.15$; the $4$k refit gives $1.03$, $0.69$ and $0.65$ to $1.24$ (worst
$2.42$), the joint model $0.60$, $0.93$ and $0.22$ to $1.56$. Since D70 (the user: ``the short end of training with the trunk frozen is the default'') every HETEROSC run ends
with this stage: \code{run.py} warm-starts a $\sigma$-only fit from the run's best checkpoint
(\code{training.heterosc\_sigma\_stage\_steps} $250$, effective lr $0.01$, warmup $0$, no L2, EMA off) into
\code{<run\_dir>/sigma\_stage}; $\mu$ is the run's own. It costs $17$ to $19$\,s of training on the single-process $8$k cells ($17$ to $20$\,min for the joint run, so
$1.5$ to $1.8\%$) and $12.6$\,min on the catalog's hp58 against its $281$\,min ($4.5\%$; $44$\,min of wall, most of it
loading the $1352$ pools). Checked on a $500$-step het run of $ee\to t\bar t$ ($D=10^4$,
\code{runs/zz\_het\_ab/tp\_scr\_ee\_ttbar\_d8\_\_trial\_0144\_s1\_stagetest}): $258$ trainable parameters, validation
NLL $-3.82\to-4.65$ in $16$\,s.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_sigonly.pdf}
\caption{The $\sigma$-only stage on the $32$k het model ($e^+e^-\to t\bar t$, $D=10^4$), test split: the joint model, the
$4000$-step refit at the trial's lr, and the best $250$-step refit (\code{training.lr} $0.097$, effective $9.7\times10^{-3}$). Left: the RMS actual error against the
median predicted $\sigma$ per $\sigma$ decile, with the calibrated diagonal. Right: the pulls, with $\mathcal N(0,1)$
dashed.}
\label{fig:het_sigonly}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The $\Delta$ cells retrained with the het loss (D47; 2026-10-16 and 10-21, all $179$ cells).} Each cell of the
$\Delta$ plots' $8$k grid at $D=10^4$ (\code{delta\_audit.json}) retrained with the user's loss at its own config and
HPs (CLAUDE.md A/B step 3), the same seed and, for a fine-tune, the same parent with its readout's $\mu$ row kept and the
$\sigma$ row zeroed (a \code{sign\_head} parent's second row is its sign logit), then the $\sigma$-only stage (D70) and
\code{tools/eval\_delta.py} on its test split (\code{scripts/job\_het\_delta.sh}; the four signed one-loop probes on
their signed-log target). All run on CC: the $72$ cells first trained on CC and lxplus in $18$ jobs (2026-10-16), the
$107$ first trained on Jean Zay in $20$ (2026-10-21, after copying them and their $11$ parents to CC). One blew up:
\code{tp3\_uu64fte\_ee\_nnbar\_d8} trial $114$, at step $1360$ (validation NLL $10^{12}$, then $50$ non-finite gradient
steps and the run aborts); its best checkpoint, step $400$ at NLL $-2.65$ against $-7$ to $-9$ for the other cells, went
through the $\sigma$ stage on its own (\code{scripts/job\_het\_delta\_stage.sh}): $29\times$ the MSE cell's median
$|\Delta|$, pull std $1.06$. It is drawn apart and left out of the numbers below. Fig.~\ref{fig:het_delta_summary}
(\code{het\_delta\_summary.py}; per-probe figures \code{delta\_het\_*} from \code{delta\_plots.py --het},
\code{pull\_het\_ladder\_*} and \code{pull\_het\_summary} from \code{het\_delta\_pulls.py}). Each het cell is compared with the MSE evaluation the $\Delta$ plots draw for it (\code{delta\_plots.pick}: the
precision it was trained at; $3$ of them reruns of a pruned trial). On the $146$ $2\to2$ cells the het $\mu$ has a
median $|\Delta|$ $0.64$ times the MSE cell's (median over cells; lower in $119$ of $146$, from $0.03\times$ on the
one-loop $ee\to d\bar d$ synthetic cell to $7\times$ on $u\bar u\to Zg$ from rung 9; per probe from $0.15$ on the
electroweak one-loop $ee\to d\bar d$ to $0.98$ on $ee\to d\bar d$), and the pulls are calibrated: std $1.01$, $1.02$,
$1.03$ at the quartiles, $138$ of $146$ within $0.9$ to $1.2$, $|{\rm pull}|<1$ for $0.61$ to $0.80$ of the events. The
eight outside: five between $0.81$ and $0.89$, and three above $1.2$ ($ee\to b\bar b$ one-loop from rungs 1 and 8,
$3.42$ and $1.33$; $ee\to\nu\bar\nu$ from rung 7, $1.31$) whose std is set by a few events (largest $|{\rm pull}|$ $236$
and $62$) while $|{\rm pull}|<1$ stays $0.65$ to $0.77$. With more final-state particles both fail. On the $19$
$2\to3$ cells ($3$ each of $ee\to d\bar dg$ and $ee\to t\bar tg$, $13$ of $u\bar u\to Zgg$) the het $\mu$ is worse
($1.55\times$ the MSE median $|\Delta|$, $0.90$ to $3.78$, lower in $2$ of $19$) and $\sigma$ too small on test (pull
std $2.14$ at the median, $0.99$ to $3.28$, $3$ of $19$ within $0.9$ to $1.2$; $|{\rm pull}|<1$ $0.46$ to $0.69$;
$u\bar u\to Zgg$ worst). On the $13$ $2\to4$ cells ($u\bar u\to Zggg$) the same: $1.63\times$ ($1.16$ to $2.48$, lower in
none), pull std $1.48$ at the median ($1.05$ to $3.45$, $2$ of $13$ within $0.9$ to $1.2$), $|{\rm pull}|<1$ $0.34$ to
$0.62$. The cause appears to be overfitting rather than the stage, as measured on the first $12$ of these
$32$ cells: the training split's pulls have std $0.46$ to $0.91$ (calibrated or wider than needed), validation and test $0.99$
to $2.99$, while the $15$ $2\to2$ cells checked ($ee\to d\bar d$, $u\bar u\to gg$) have $0.99$ to $1.12$ on all three
splits. The stage fits $\sigma$ to the training residuals, which at $D=10^4$ on these processes are smaller than the
residuals on unseen events. Under the A/B protocol the het loss losing at the MSE cell's HPs calls for its own sweep
before a verdict on $\mu$; neither that nor a $\sigma$ fitted on held-out residuals is run.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_delta_summary.pdf}
\caption{The $179$ $\Delta$ cells retrained with the het loss against the MSE cells, test split. Left: the median
$|\Delta|$ of the het $\mu$ against the MSE model's, one point per cell. Right: the fraction of
events with $|{\rm pull}|$ beyond $k$, the median cell of each group with the band holding $10$ to $90\%$ of its
cells, against $\mathcal N(0,1)$ (dashed); a pull std and one fraction are two numbers of this curve and do not define a
Gaussian. Cross and dotted: the cell that blew up, at its best checkpoint.}
\label{fig:het_delta_summary}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The het loss with its own HPs on the cells with more than two final-state particles (D75, 2026-10-22).}
The $2\to3$ and $2\to4$ cells came out worse with the het loss at the MSE cells' HPs, so eight of them got a DyHPO
sweep of their own (\code{sweep/gen\_het23\_sweeps.py}): scratch and rung~1 of $e^+e^-\to d\bar dg$, $e^+e^-\to t\bar
tg$, $u\bar u\to Zgg$ and $u\bar u\to Zggg$, each the MSE cell's sweep (search space, horizon $8$k, data, parent,
surrogate and candidate pool) with the loss switched, $8$ trials from scratch and $5$ for rung~1, $52$ in all; the
objective is the validation NLL, and every trial ends with the $\sigma$ stage. Each best trial against its MSE cell
on the test split (\code{scripts/job\_het23\_eval.sh}, \code{analysis/transfer/het23\_summary.py},
Fig.~\ref{fig:het23}): median $|\Delta|$, het over MSE, $0.99$, $1.21$, $1.07$, $1.59$, $0.85$, $1.04$, $1.23$, $1.57$ in
the order above. Against the het retrain at the MSE HPs the own sweep is better in five cells (by $3.5\times$ on
$e^+e^-\to t\bar tg$ from scratch and $2.2\times$ on $u\bar u\to Zgg$ at rung~1) and worse in three ($e^+e^-\to d\bar dg$
and $e^+e^-\to t\bar tg$ at rung~1, $u\bar u\to Zggg$ at rung~1), all three fine-tune sweeps of $5$ trials. So with its
own HPs the het loss draws level with MSE on $\mu$ in half the $2\to3$ cells (within $7\%$ in three of six, $15\%$
better on $u\bar u\to Zgg$ from scratch, $21\%$ and $59\%$ worse at rung~1 of $e^+e^-\to d\bar dg$ and $t\bar tg$) and
stays behind at $2\to4$ ($1.23$, $1.57$). The NLL objective picks the trial whose $\sigma$
fits best, not whose $\mu$ does, which can explain a best-NLL trial that loses on $|\Delta|$ to a trial at other HPs.
The $\sigma$ of these best trials is too small: the pull std is $1.6$ to $3.0$ (a calibrated $\sigma$ gives $1$).
HPs: the warmup splits apart between the two losses (het in the lower half of the range in $7$ of $8$ cells, MSE in
its upper half in $5$), $\lambda$ sits near the top of its range for MSE in $7$ of $8$ and is spread for het, EMA is on in $6$
of $8$ for both but at decay $0.95$ to $0.995$ for het against $0.90$ to $0.92$ for MSE in $5$ of its $6$, and lr
overlaps; the scratch sweeps of
one probe pair chose one candidate twice ($e^+e^-\to d\bar dg$ and $t\bar tg$, the same point; $u\bar u\to Zgg$ and
$Zggg$, the same point but for lr). So the het HPs are consistent among themselves and differ from the MSE ones.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/het23_delta.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/het23_hps.pdf}}
\caption{The het loss on eight $2\to3$ and $2\to4$ cells with its own DyHPO sweep. Left: median $|\Delta|$ on the
test split per cell (1, 2: $e^+e^-\to d\bar dg$ scratch, rung~1; 3, 4: $e^+e^-\to t\bar tg$; 5, 6: $u\bar u\to Zgg$;
7, 8: $u\bar u\to Zggg$) for the MSE cell, the het retrain at its HPs (D47) and the het sweep's best trial. Right:
each searched HP of each cell's best run as its position in the search range ($\log$ for lr, $\lambda$, $\eta_{\min}$),
cells left to right in the same order; an open marker on EMA is a run with EMA off.}
\label{fig:het23}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The other $2\to3$ and $2\to4$ cells at the shared het HPs (D82, 2026-10-22).} The $24$ remaining cells of
the four probes with more than two final-state particles, retrained with the het loss at the HPs the scratch het sweeps
chose (Fig.~\ref{fig:het23}: one point for both $e^+e^-$ probes, one for both $u\bar u$ probes with each probe's lr;
\code{scripts/job\_het\_delta.sh --shared}, \code{analysis/transfer/het\_shared\_summary.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:het_shared}).
All $24$ finished ($4$, lost to the full \code{/sps/lpnhe} disk at the first attempt, were rerun:
$u\bar u\to Zgg$ at rungs $7$ and $8$, $u\bar u\to Zggg$ at rung $8$, $e^+e^-\to d\bar dg$ fine-tuned from the soft star).
Median $|\Delta|$, het over MSE: median $1.39$ over the $13$ $2\to3$ cells and $1.36$ over the $11$ $2\to4$ cells,
against $1.59$ and $1.79$ for the het retrain at each MSE cell's own HPs; the shared HPs beat the het retrain at the MSE
cell's HPs in $19$ of the $24$, and MSE in $1$ ($e^+e^-\to t\bar tg$ fine-tuned from the dead-cone star, $0.45$). So the het
HPs help, and the het loss stays $30$ to $40\%$ behind MSE on $\mu$ at these multiplicities.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_shared.pdf}
\caption{The $2\to3$ and $2\to4$ $\Delta$ cells retrained with the het loss, median $|\Delta|$ on the test split against
the MSE cell: at the het sweeps' shared HPs (filled) and at the MSE cell's own HPs (open, D47). All $24$ cells.}
\label{fig:het_shared}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The same $24$ cells with the checkpoint picked on the $\mu$ MSE (D85, 2026-10-22).} D82's cells select the
main run's checkpoint on the validation NLL. Here they select on the validation $\mu$ MSE
(\code{training.heterosc\_select=mu\_mse}), with the same HPs, data, steps and seeds; the $\sigma$ stage after it still
selects on the NLL with $\mu$ frozen (\code{scripts/job\_het\_delta.sh --shared-mumse},
\code{analysis/transfer/het\_mumse\_summary.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:het_mumse}). Eight cells were trained by two duplicate
jobs at once in the first attempt and were rerun into a clean directory, so all $24$ are single, complete runs. Median
$|\Delta|$, het over MSE: $1.11$ over the $13$ $2\to3$ cells and $1.16$ over the $11$ $2\to4$ cells, against $1.39$ and
$1.36$ when selected on the NLL. The $\mu$ MSE selection is lower than the NLL selection in $23$ of the $24$ cells (median
ratio $0.81$ at $2\to3$, $0.90$ at $2\to4$), and below MSE in $3$ (the $t\bar tg$ cell of D82, $e^+e^-\to t\bar t g$ from the
dead-cone star, plus $u\bar u\to Zgg$ at rung $8$ and $u\bar u\to Zggg$ at rung $2$, both within $4\%$). So
the checkpoint choice was $71\%$ of the het loss's gap to MSE on $\mu$ at $2\to3$ and $54\%$ at $2\to4$: the two
criteria pick different steps, and the NLL's has the worse $\mu$. The remaining gap is $11$ to $16\%$.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_mumse.pdf}
\caption{The $24$ cells of Fig.~\ref{fig:het_shared}, het loss at the shared HPs, median $|\Delta|$ on the test split
against the MSE cell, with the main run's checkpoint picked on the validation $\mu$ MSE (filled) or the validation NLL
(open, as in Fig.~\ref{fig:het_shared}).}
\label{fig:het_mumse}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The $147$ $2\to2$ cells with the $\mu$-MSE checkpoint, and how far two runs of one config land (D89, 2026-10-23).}
D47's $2\to2$ cells retrained with the checkpoint on the validation $\mu$ MSE, everything else as D47 (config, HPs, data,
seed, parent; the config files differ in \code{heterosc\_select} alone; \code{scripts/job\_het\_delta.sh --mumse},
\code{analysis/transfer/het\_mumse22\_summary.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:het_mumse22}). Read as D85 read its $24$ cells, the
rerun is worse: median $|\Delta|$ $0.77$ times the MSE cell's ($91$ of $146$ below it) against D47's $0.64$ ($119$ of
$146$), and $1.15$ times D47 cell by cell, lower in only $56$; on the one-loop probes up to $130\times$ (electroweak
$e^+e^-\to d\bar d$). That reading is wrong, because the two runs of a cell are not the same run: they already differ at
the first validation (step $80$: NLL $-0.59$ against $-0.70$ on the cell above), and their best validation $\mu$ MSE (each run's minimum)
differs by more than $2\times$ in $69$ of the $146$ cells ($10$ to $90\%$ of the ratio: $0.20$
to $5.9$, median $0.97$). Inside one run the checkpoint choice barely matters at $2\to2$: the $\mu$ MSE at D47's NLL pick over its
run's $\mu$-MSE minimum is $1.00$ at the median and $1.03$ at the $75$th percentile ($1.29$ at the $90$th). At $2\to3$ and $2\to4$ (the
$32$ run directories of D82 and D85) two runs of one config agree to $0.90$ to $1.15$ ($10$ to $90\%$), none $2\times$ apart, and the NLL pick
costs $16\%$ in $\mu$ at the median, so D85's conclusion stands there. At $2\to2$ the het numbers carry a run-to-run factor of
about $2$ either way per cell (D47's $0.64$ is one draw of it), and the selection is not what separates them. The pulls stay
calibrated with either pick (std $0.99$, $1.01$, $1.02$ at the quartiles; $125$ of $146$ within $0.9$ to $1.2$, against
$138$). The cell that blew up in D47 (\code{tp3\_uu64fte\_ee\_nnbar\_d8} trial $114$) is left out of these numbers, as there; its rerun is $51$ times the MSE cell's median $|\Delta|$.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_mumse22.pdf}
\caption{Run-to-run spread against the checkpoint choice, per cell, from the runs' validation $\mu$-MSE curves. Solid:
the best (minimum) validation $\mu$ MSE of the rerun over that of the first run of the same config ($2\to2$: D89 over D47, $146$ cells; $2\to3$,
$2\to4$: D85 over D82, $32$ runs). Dashed: inside the first run, the $\mu$ MSE at the NLL-selected checkpoint over the
run's minimum.}
\label{fig:het_mumse22}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Het HPs of the finale fine-tune, stage 1 (D91, 2026-10-23).} The het fine-tune benchmark needs het HPs, and only
the largest cells had them. Stage 1 sweeps four probes that have no twin in the big catalog, one of each kind
($e^+e^-\to d\bar d$, $e^+e^-\to W^+W^-$, $e^+e^-\to d\bar d$ at one loop, $u\bar u\to Zggg$), at $D=10^2$ and $10^4$: the finale's
own fine-tune cells (same search space, horizons $2816$ and $8000$ steps, data, parent), with the het loss and the checkpoint
on the validation $\mu$ MSE, $5$ DyHPO trials each (\code{sweep/gen\_finale\_het\_sweeps.py}, \code{sweep/drive.py};
\code{analysis/transfer/het\_finale\_s1.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:het_finale_s1}). All $40$ trials finished; $8$ were killed by a
full filesystem and rerun at their own HPs (\code{drive.py --retry-lost}), so none carries an imputed loss. The best
learning rates span $1.1\times10^{-3}$ to $3.6\times10^{-3}$ ($e^+e^-\to d\bar d$ $1.3$ and $1.1$, $W^+W^-$ $3.6$ and $3.4$, one loop $1.5$
and $1.3$, $Zggg$ $3.5$ and $2.8$, in $10^{-3}$, at $D=10^2$ and $10^4$), a factor $3$, and each probe's two cells agree within
$25\%$; $\lambda$ sits at $2\times10^{-9}$ to $5\times10^{-8}$ in seven cells ($3\times10^{-7}$ in the eighth), the warm-up
fraction at $0.10$ to $0.18$, and EMA is on in five. The optimum is not shared, though: at $D=10^4$ the trials at
$\log_{10}\mathrm{lr}>-2.65$ are $29$ to $330$ times worse than the best for the two $d\bar d$ probes, while $W^+W^-$ is
$2.9$ times worse at $D=10^2$ at the $d\bar d$ probes' learning rate, so no single point is within a factor $1.5$ of every
cell's best. In $3$ of the $8$ cells the best trial is the lowest learning rate tried, so those optima are not bracketed.
For the one probe whose MSE cell's best trial is on record at $D=10^4$ ($W^+W^-$, trial $193$, $\mathrm{lr}=8.5\times10^{-3}$),
the het optimum is $2.5$ times lower.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_finale_s1.pdf}
\caption{Stage 1 of the het fine-tune benchmark: each trial's validation $\mu$ MSE over its cell's best against its learning
rate, at $D=10^2$ (left) and $10^4$ (right), $5$ trials per probe.}
\label{fig:het_finale_s1}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Stage 2: the big-catalog pretraining fine-tuned with the het loss (D94, 2026-10-23).} Since stage 1's optima are
not shared, the rest is swept: the finale's other three no-twin probes ($e^+e^-\to\nu\bar\nu$, $t\bar t$, $b\bar b$ at one loop)
at $D=10^2$ and $10^4$, and the per-process pretraining on the arXiv:2606.23791 catalog (hp58, its final model) fine-tuned on
all seven at $D=10^1$ to $10^4$, the finale's cells with the parent and its diagram encoder swapped in, the learning-rate
window widened to $[7\times10^{-4},6\times10^{-3}]$, $5$ trials each ($34$ sweeps, $170$ trials, none failed;
\code{sweep/gen\_finale\_het\_sweeps.py --stage 2}; \code{analysis/transfer/het\_stage2.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:het_stage2}).
Every value is the sweep's best trial at its best checkpoint, the validation MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ ($\mu$ for het)
in raw units; all arms of a probe and $D$ share one standardization, and the two one-loop pools are positive on every
event, so the MSE cells' sign head is inert there and their target is the het one. hp58 against the finale, both het, at
$D=10^2$ and $10^4$ (ratio hp58 over finale):
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcc}
 & $D=10^2$ & $D=10^4$ \\ \hline
$e^+e^-\to d\bar d$ & $1.3$ & $0.78$ \\
$e^+e^-\to W^+W^-$ & $1.3$ & $0.99$ \\
$e^+e^-\to\nu\bar\nu$ & $1.8$ & $0.87$ \\
$e^+e^-\to t\bar t$ & $0.27$ & $0.67$ \\
$u\bar u\to Zggg$ & $2.6$ & $0.71$ \\
$e^+e^-\to d\bar d$, one loop & $7.8$ & $6.8$ \\
$e^+e^-\to b\bar b$, one loop & $6.0$ & $230$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
In $13$ of hp58's $28$ cells the best of the first five trials was the highest learning rate tried, among them all four
$d\bar d$ one-loop cells and $b\bar b$ at $D=10^3$ and $10^4$, where the loss still fell steeply with the rate. Those $13$
sweeps were extended in place (D97, \code{sweep/extend\_sweep.py}: the window to $[7\times10^{-4},2\times10^{-2}]$, $50$ new
candidates in the added zone, the five observations and the surrogate kept) and run five trials further; the table is
after that. The extension lowered $9$ of the $13$ bests: $b\bar b$ at one loop by $50\times$ at $D=10^4$ ($0.028$ to
$5.4\times10^{-4}$) and $8\times$ at $10^3$, $\nu\bar\nu$ by $3\times$ at $10^4$, the one-loop $d\bar d$ by $2\times$ at
$10^2$; the other four were within $1\%$. Nine are now bracketed (a higher rate was tried and lost); in four the best is
again the highest rate tried ($d\bar d$ at $D=10$, $\nu\bar\nu$ at $10^2$, the one-loop $b\bar b$ at $10^4$, the one-loop
$d\bar d$ at $10^2$, at $1.7\times10^{-2}$), and in three of those the surrogate kept its new trials below $6\times10^{-3}$,
so their optimum stays unbracketed. After the extension, on the five tree probes hp58 is level with the finale or better at
$D=10^4$ ($0.67$ to $0.99$) and behind at $D=10^2$ ($1.3$ to $2.6$) except $t\bar t$, ahead at both. On the two one-loop
probes it stays far behind ($7$ and $230\times$ at $10^4$). The finale's het cells sit at the other edge: $5$ of $14$ have
their best at the lowest rate tried. Against the MSE cells (different losses, so only as context): at $D=10$ hp58 is below scratch on
$\nu\bar\nu$, $t\bar t$, $Zggg$ and the one-loop $b\bar b$ ($0.51$ against $1.76$, $4.5\times10^{-4}$ against $5.4\times10^{-3}$, $0.34$ against
$1.82$, $0.43$ against $0.63$), level on $W^+W^-$ and the one-loop $d\bar d$, and above on tree $d\bar d$ ($2.2$ against $0.80$).

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_stage2_legend.pdf}\\[1ex]
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_stage2_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_stage2_b.pdf}} \\[1ex]
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_stage2_c.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_stage2_d.pdf}} \\[1ex]
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_stage2_e.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_stage2_f.pdf}} \\[1ex]
\pnl{../analysis/transfer/figs/het_stage2_g.pdf}
\caption{The seven no-twin probes against the fine-tune events $D$, each cell its sweep's best trial: the big-catalog
pretraining hp58 fine-tuned with the het loss (stars; $13$ of its $28$ sweeps extended to a learning rate of
$2\times10^{-2}$, D97; in four the best is still the highest rate tried, so those four are upper bounds), the finale fine-tuned with the het loss (open diamonds, $D=10^2$, $10^4$) and the MSE cells of
the ladder (scratch, rung~9, synthetic, finale). MSE of $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ ($\mu$ for het) at the best checkpoint.}

\paragraph{The $\sigma$ step costs $\sim$1 min, not the recipe's 600 iters.} $\sigma$-only is
a frozen-trunk 258-param readout, so the whole trajectory is one short run. Probing the
$\sigma$-\emph{ranking} per validation step (\code{sigma\_speed\_probe.py} $\to$
Spearman$(\sigma,|r|)$ global + per-process median; \code{sigma\_speed.sh}, constant-lr to avoid
the cosine-horizon confound) shows the ranking \emph{saturates by iter $\sim$20--30} ($\sim$1 min),
long before calibration converges: 600 iters is overkill \emph{for ranking}. The apparent 23-min
base22 run was \emph{validation} overhead, not the fit: \code{LLOCA\_PROFILE\_STEP} split it
$55$\,s pure training ($182$\,ms/iter) vs $\sim$45\,s $\times$30 full 22-proc validations. Key
subtlety: cross-process ranking is strong ($\rho\!\approx\!0.85$), within-process weak
($\rho\!\approx\!0.29$ for base22, $0.46$ for single-proc uug) and \emph{does not} improve with more
training: global Spearman \emph{under-rates} a learned head because it weights bulk ordering
(irrelevant to reweighting) equally with the high-error tail.

\paragraph{Imperfect $\sigma$ is tolerable, but the metric must be equal-footing.} Since a
reweighting proposal $\pi\!\propto\!Q\sigma^\alpha$ renormalizes, only $\sigma$'s order/magnitude
\emph{relative} to the true error matters, not calibration per se. Test: resample the antenna pool
by $\pi\!\propto\!Q\,\mathrm{score}^\alpha$ at several signal qualities (\code{build\_reweight\_pools.py}
without replacement to a fixed $N$; \code{finetune\_sigma\_reweight.sh}; deep-IR eval), plus a
magnitude-controlled rank-vs-magnitude split (\code{build\_rank\_pools.py}: same weight multiset
$\Phi=\mathrm{sort}|r|$ reassigned by different orderings). \textbf{Metric correction (important):}
the eval must weight each $y_{\min}$ decade \emph{equally} (log-flat-per-decade, the antenna-study
metric), \emph{not} \code{np.mean}$(d^2)$ over all events: the antenna test set is $\sim$50\%
below $y_{\min}\,10^{-3}$, so event-weighting is deep-IR-dominated
(\code{q2\_metrics.py}). Under equal footing: the real $\sigma$ head captures
\textbf{$\sim$80\% of the perfect-oracle reweighting gain} (event-weighted $87$\%) and improves
\emph{every} decade including the bulk. But the rank/magnitude split \emph{flips} versus the
event-weighted view: \textbf{magnitude dominates}: ordering-alone gives only $11$\%, because
after antenna training the worst-fit decade is the \emph{bulk} $[10^{-1},10^0)$ (MSE $10^{-2}\!\gg\!$
deep-IR $8\!\times\!10^{-4}$), and only $\sigma$'s calibrated magnitude targets it; deep-IR-following
heuristics (rank, weak $\sigma$) starve the bulk and can \emph{net-hurt} (a $\rho{=}0.29$ synthetic
$\sigma$ scores $-9$\% under equal footing). So calibration matters more than a pure-rank framing
suggests (Figs.~\ref{fig:q2_logflat}, \ref{fig:q2_rankmag}; supplementary \code{q2\_confirm},
\code{q2\_dense}).

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \widerow{\includegraphics{q2_logflat.pdf}}
  \caption{\textbf{The metric must weight each $y_{\min}$ decade equally.} $\sigma$-reweighting gain
  under the log-flat-per-decade metric. The antenna test set is $\sim\!50\%$ below
  $y_{\min}\!=\!10^{-3}$, so a plain event-weighted \code{mean}$(d^2)$ is deep-IR-dominated and
  flatters any deep-IR-following heuristic. Under equal footing the real $\sigma$ head captures
  $\sim\!80\%$ of the perfect-oracle reweighting gain ($87\%$ event-weighted) and improves \emph{every}
  decade, bulk included.}
  \label{fig:q2_logflat}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \widerow{\includegraphics{q2_rankmag.pdf}}
  \caption{\textbf{Magnitude dominates ranking (a controlled rank-vs-magnitude split).} The same
  weight multiset $\Phi=\mathrm{sort}|r|$ reassigned by different orderings, so ordering and magnitude
  are varied independently. Ordering \emph{alone} recovers only $11\%$ of the oracle gain: after antenna
  training the worst-fit decade is the \emph{bulk} ($\mathrm{MSE}\sim10^{-2}$, vs deep-IR
  $8\times10^{-4}$), and only $\sigma$'s calibrated \emph{magnitude} points there. Deep-IR-following
  heuristics starve the bulk and can net-hurt: a $\rho{=}0.29$ synthetic $\sigma$ scores $-9\%$. This
  is why a pure-rank framing of $\sigma$ quality is misleading.}
  \label{fig:q2_rankmag}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Coverage vs.\ emphasis, and the general default sampling.} The decisive
structural finding: \textbf{$\sigma$-reweighting controls emphasis, not coverage: it cannot beat
the coverage the training pool already has}. The antenna pool holds only $4$\% ($16$k) bulk events,
so even the \emph{oracle} cannot fit the bulk ($8.6\!\times\!10^{-3}$) as well as the sampling-study
mixture ($2.2\!\times\!10^{-3}$), which \emph{generates} bulk events (uniform/\textsc{rambo} fills
$[10^{-1},10^0)$ at $63$\%, antenna at $4$\%; the two are complementary, $0$\% uniform coverage in
the two deepest decades). The mixture ``wins overall'' by \emph{coverage}, which no reweighting of a
starved pool can replicate. And the optimum is \emph{dynamic}: the worst region migrates IR$\to$bulk
\emph{during} training, so no static hand-designed density (antenna, flat-in-log-$y_{\min}$,
mixture) stays optimal: adaptive $\sigma$ tracks the moving target, static densities cannot.
\emph{The general default} (subsuming the process-specific proxies): sample \textbf{flat in
$\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$}, equal training density per amplitude decade. This is the universal,
structure-agnostic coverage criterion, because \emph{every} divergence type (soft/collinear IR,
$s$-channel resonance, threshold) is an \emph{amplitude extreme}, and $|\mathcal{M}|^2$ is the one
coordinate always available (computed to label every event); it matches the $\log$-MSE
equal-footing loss. \emph{How:} you cannot draw a target $|\mathcal{M}|^2$, so shape the density by
adaptive importance sampling (VEGAS/foam) \emph{guided by} $|\mathcal{M}|^2$ toward
flat-in-$\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$, \emph{not} the physical measure $|\mathcal{M}|^2\mathrm{d}\Phi$ a
cross-section generator targets (that is the \textsc{rambo} starvation trap). The three layers, then:
\textbf{coverage} via flat-$\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$ importance sampling; \textbf{emphasis} via
$\sigma$-reweighting $\pi\!\propto\!\sigma^\alpha$ (no physical binning, process-agnostic); and
\textbf{steering} (validated on \code{uugg}, below), $\sigma$-informed \emph{on-the-fly
generation}, the only layer that lets $\sigma$ overcome the coverage ceiling, by generating where
$\sigma$ is high \emph{and} data is sparse. The code and figure names for the steering layer carry an
older \code{l2\_} prefix.

\paragraph{Coverage validated on the $ee\to u\bar u$ $Z$ resonance, by generation rather than resampling.}
The $s$-channel $Z$ pole ($|\mathcal{M}|^2$ peaks $\sim$$200\times$ at $\sqrt{s}\!\approx\!91$; flat
\textsc{rambo} in $\sqrt{s}$ puts only $0.4$\% of events in $[88,95]$, and the production foundation
duly fits the peak $\sim$$400\times$ worse than the bulk, MSE $1.9\mathrm{e}{-}5$ vs $4.8\mathrm{e}{-}8$)
is the second, structurally-distinct singularity. \emph{Key negative result first:}
\textbf{resampling the fixed $10^6$ \textsc{rambo} pool to flat-$\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$ does not fix the
resonance}: the pool holds only $\sim$$3300$ real pole events, so flat resampling merely duplicates
them ($155$k unique of $400$k), no new pole physics. This is the coverage ceiling made concrete: \emph{you cannot resample your way past what the pool contains.} The fix is to
\textbf{generate} fresh pole events, exactly as the uug antenna sampler generates deep-IR points.
\code{gen\_uu\_sampling.py} draws $\sqrt{s}$ from a pole-covering importance proposal, builds $2\to2$
momenta, labels the exact tree $|\mathcal{M}|^2$ with the compiled \code{ee\_uu} standalone
($14.5$k\,ev/s, scale verified vs production), and thins $3\mathrm{M}$ candidates to $400$k
flat-in-$\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$ \emph{without replacement} (all unique). The shaper reads \emph{only}
$|\mathcal{M}|^2$; $\sqrt{s}$ is eval-only. Generation lifts pole coverage $0.4\%\!\to\!32\%$ with genuinely
new events, and beats resampling $2.6\times$ on the log-flat metric (Fig.~\ref{fig:eeuu_flatlogm_resonance}). \emph{Bug found + fixed (would have inverted the result):} the standalone expects
the final-state quarks in the \emph{opposite} slot order from how production stores $[e^-,e^+,u,\bar u]$;
a direct driver call mirror-flips the $Z$ forward-backward asymmetry, so the model trains perfectly on
its own set (internal val $10^{-5}$) yet \emph{anti-correlates} on production test ($\rho_{\rm bulk}\!=\!-0.99$).
Relabeling production AS-IS gives $\rho{=}0.94$ (mirrored); swapping the final two recovers the stored
$|\mathcal{M}|^2$ exactly. Fix: swap for the driver, store unswapped ($\rho{=}+0.994$ post-fix).

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{eeuu_flatlogm_resonance.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{Coverage on the $ee\to u\bar u$ $Z$ resonance: generation, not resampling.} Held-out MSE
  binned in $\sqrt s$ for three training densities scored on the same test set: raw \textsc{rambo}, a
  flat-$\log|\mathcal M|^2$ set obtained by \emph{resampling} the fixed $10^6$ \textsc{rambo} pool, and
  one obtained by \emph{generating} fresh events. Raw \textsc{rambo} is the best arm in the bulk and the
  worst at the pole: it puts only $0.4\%$ of events in $\sqrt s\!\in\![88,95]$, and the foundation
  fits the peak $\sim\!400\times$ worse than the bulk, so both flat arms trade bulk accuracy for pole
  accuracy. Resampling cannot go far: the pool holds only $\sim\!3300$ genuine pole events, so flat
  resampling merely duplicates them ($155$k unique of $400$k). Generating lifts pole coverage
  $0.4\%\!\to\!32\%$ with genuinely new events, and is $2.6\times$ better than resampling on the log-flat
  metric; against raw it is $9.4\times$ better log-flat and $2.1\times$ at the $Z$ peak. This is the
  coverage ceiling made concrete: you cannot resample past what the pool contains.
  \emph{(Rebuilt from the corrected re-eval \code{eeuu\_reson\_clean\_summary.json}; the earlier version
  of this figure was drawn from the pre-fix evaluation, whose cross-tag state leak corrupted every tag
  after the first and gave $5.8\times/1.5\times$ here.)}}
  \label{fig:eeuu_flatlogm_resonance}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The generated fraction sweep: a clean interior optimum, and pure flat over-corrects.}
$f$ = fraction flat-$\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$ (rest uniform-$\sqrt{s}$), generated fresh, same base22
ckpt/HPs/$N{=}400$k, common \textsc{rambo} test binned by $\sqrt{s}$, each arm de-standardized to true
$\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$ (per-dataset standardization already handles the scale, so the earlier
``$\sigma$-scale confound'' worry was spurious). Under the equal-per-$\sqrt{s}$-decade (log-flat) metric:
$f{=}0$ (raw) $1.0\mathrm{e}{-}3$; $f{=}0.25$ $3.5\mathrm{e}{-}5$; $\mathbf{f{=}0.5}$ $\mathbf{1.5\mathrm{e}{-}5}$
(optimum); $f{=}0.75$ $7.9\mathrm{e}{-}5$; $f{=}1$ (pure flat) $1.1\mathrm{e}{-}4$. Event-weighted
(bulk-heavy) overall bottoms at $f{=}0.25$. So the interior optimum is $f\!\approx\!0.25$--$0.5$, beating
raw \textsc{rambo} $\sim$$30$--$70\times$ and \textbf{pure} flat-$\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$ $\sim$$4$--$7\times$:
\emph{flat-$\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$ is the coverage criterion you mix in at a modest fraction, not a good
pure base}: pure flat starves the bulk (mirror of \textsc{rambo} starving the pole), same shape as the
uug antenna interior optimum. Generation still beats resampling $\sim$$8\times$ ($3.5\mathrm{e}{-}5$ vs
$2.8\mathrm{e}{-}4$). Tooling: \code{gen\_uu\_sampling.py} (\code{uniform}/\code{flatlogm}/\code{mixture}),
\code{finetune\_genmix.sh}, \code{plot\_genmix\_sweep.py} (Fig.~\ref{fig:eeuu_genmix_fraction_sweep}).

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{eeuu_genmix_fraction_sweep.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{The generated fraction sweep on the $Z$ resonance: a clean interior optimum.}
  $f$ is the fraction of freshly \emph{generated} flat-$\log|\mathcal M|^2$ events (rest
  uniform-$\sqrt s$); same base22/HPs/$N{=}400$k throughout.
  \emph{Left: the interior optimum.} Three metrics vs $f$. The objective is the \emph{log-flat}
  one: the mean of the per-$\sqrt s$-bin MSE, so every bin counts once
  however many events it holds; the plain event mean instead follows the bulk, which holds almost all
  the events, and so barely sees the pole. Log-flat gives $1.0\mathrm{e}{-}3$, $3.5\mathrm{e}{-}5$,
  $\mathbf{1.5\mathrm{e}{-}5}$, $7.9\mathrm{e}{-}5$, $1.1\mathrm{e}{-}4$ at $f=0,0.25,0.5,0.75,1$,
  a minimum at $f{=}0.5$, beating raw \textsc{rambo} $\sim\!30$--$70\times$ and \emph{pure} flat
  $\sim\!4$--$7\times$. The resonance-region curve ($\sqrt s<100$) tracks it; the event-weighted
  ``overall'' curve, being bulk-heavy, bottoms earlier at $f{=}0.25$, which is exactly why the
  objective has to be the equal-per-bin one.
  \emph{Right: the mechanism, the bulk$\leftrightarrow$pole trade-off.} The same runs resolved per
  $\sqrt s$ bin: $f{=}0$ is far the best in the bulk and far the worst at the pole, $f{=}1$ is the
  mirror image, and the interior mixtures are the flattest across both. So
  flat-$\log|\mathcal M|^2$ is a criterion to \emph{mix in}, not a good base: pure flat starves the
  bulk exactly as \textsc{rambo} starves the pole. Same shape as the $u\bar u g$ antenna sweep
  (Fig.~\ref{fig:deep_sampling_fraction_sweep}).}
  \label{fig:eeuu_genmix_fraction_sweep}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Eval bug (invalidated an earlier read): in-process state leak across tags.} The multi-tag
resonance eval (\code{eval\_eeuu\_resonance.py}) corrupted \emph{every tag after the first}: the same
\code{ft\_mix025} scored logflat $3.5\mathrm{e}{-}5$ as tag~1 but $2.1\mathrm{e}{-}4$ as tag~2, reproducibly
(a module/$\mu$P\ global survives across \code{AmplitudeExperiment} instances; a per-tag \code{run\_dir} does
\emph{not} fix it $\Rightarrow$ in-process, not file-based). Fix: self-dispatch one tag per subprocess,
merge summaries. The corrected re-eval is what the numbers above report; the pre-fix ``$f{=}0.5$ noise
bump'' was this corruption, not seed noise.

\paragraph{Emphasis: $\sigma$-reweighting the coverage base does not robustly help (3 seeds).} On the $f{=}0.25$
base: grow a $\sigma$ row on the $\mu$ model, two-stage fit (frozen $\mu$, $\beta{=}0$; $\mu$ recovers
bit-identically), extract $\sigma$ over a fresh $1.5\mathrm{M}$ mix025 pool (global $\rho(\sigma,|r|){=}0.66$),
subsample $400$k \emph{without replacement} by $\pi\!\propto\!\sigma^\alpha$ (no ceiling, since the pool is fresh).
$\sigma$'s budget reallocation matches the oracle almost exactly (pole $20\%$ vs $18\%$). But over
\textbf{3 seeds} $\times$\{base,$\sigma$,oracle\} (\code{finetune\_l1\_seeds.sh}): logflat base
$4.6\mathrm{e}{-}5\!\pm\!3.4\mathrm{e}{-}5$, $\sigma$ $1.8\mathrm{e}{-}4\!\pm\!2.3\mathrm{e}{-}4$, oracle
$3.1\mathrm{e}{-}5\!\pm\!3.3\mathrm{e}{-}6$; $\sigma$/base per-seed $=1.46\times,0.04\times,1.12\times$ (one
seed $25\times$ \emph{worse}). \textbf{The seed-0 $1.5\times$ ``win'' was inside the $\sim$$4\times$
run-to-run variance.} Even the oracle buys little on the mean and does not beat base's best draws. Conclusion:
\textbf{coverage is the dominant lever; emphasis is second-order once coverage is good}, and the current $\sigma$ head is too weak a within-region signal to realize the oracle.
$\sigma$-emphasis should earn its keep only where coverage is \emph{capped}: a starved,
un-regenerable pool.

\paragraph{Emphasis confirmed where coverage is capped: $\sigma\!\approx\!$oracle on a starved base (3-seed).}
Repeating the emphasis pipeline on a \textbf{starved} $1.5\mathrm{M}$ \textsc{rambo} base (pole $0.44\%$;
\code{sigma\_fit\_raw.sh}, $\rho(\sigma,|r|){=}0.52$; $\sigma$ lifts pole selection $0.4\%\!\to\!1.6\%$,
the $4\times$ ceiling, $\approx$oracle's $1.5\%$) \emph{flips the verdict}. Over 3 seeds
$\times$\{base,$\sigma$,oracle\}, logflat: base $1.4\mathrm{e}{-}4\!\pm\!1.3\mathrm{e}{-}4$, $\sigma$
$4.1\mathrm{e}{-}5\!\pm\!2.8\mathrm{e}{-}5$, oracle $4.4\mathrm{e}{-}5\!\pm\!2.4\mathrm{e}{-}5$; $\sigma$/base
per-seed $=1.10\times,1.89\times,12.65\times$ (\emph{helps every seed}, mean $5.2\times$ logflat / $5.7\times$
resonance), and \textbf{$\sigma$ recovers $103$--$143\%$ of the oracle}: the real head is as good as the
perfect error signal here. Contrast the covered $f{=}0.25$ base, where $\sigma$/base was
$1.46\times,0.04\times,1.12\times$ (null). \textbf{This scopes emphasis precisely: $\sigma$-reweighting is the
tool for when you cannot regenerate coverage (a starved, fixed pool); on a base you can generate
well, emphasis adds nothing.} Mechanism = the coverage ceiling: on \textsc{rambo}, uniform barely touches
the pole ($0.4\%$), so concentrating the \emph{limited} pole events fixes the resonance; on mix025 coverage
is already balanced, leaving $\sigma$ nothing to reallocate.

\paragraph{Takeaway.} \emph{Generate a modest flat-$\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$ admixture
($f\!\approx\!0.25$--$0.5$) for coverage; add $\sigma$-reweighting only when the pool is starved and
un-regenerable.}

\paragraph{Steering validated on $ee\to u\bar u gg$: $\sigma$-informed on-the-fly generation beats
uniform generation, coordinate-free.} Steering is the layer where $\sigma$ finally overcomes the
coverage ceiling: instead of \emph{reweighting} a fixed pool, the model's live uncertainty steers what
gets \emph{generated} next. Demonstrated on $ee\to u\bar u gg$, deliberately the process the
earlier $ee\to u\bar u$ attempt flagged as the right test, since its overlapping soft \emph{and}
collinear singularities admit \emph{no} single hand-tuned density (no one-parameter $f$ can be
near-optimal, unlike the $2\!\to\!2$ resonance). \emph{The loop} (\code{l2\_online\_uugg.py}): one
long-lived process, model $+$ optimizer $+$ a single cosine built once over the full horizon
(round~0 \code{init\_data} also freezes the amp/mom preprocessing stats); rounds are boundaries
\emph{inside} the one \code{train()} loop, reached by monkeypatching \code{exp.\_cycle} (no core
edit: the runtime imports the core modules from the main repo, so a worktree edit to \code{train()}
would be dead code). At each boundary: propose fresh momenta from a process-agnostic base, score by
the live $\sigma$, keep $n$, label survivors with the exact \code{ee\_uugg} standalone, preprocess
with the frozen stats, and append to a growing pool. The base proposal
(\code{gen\_ir\_democratic.py}) is genuinely process-agnostic: an IR-democratic recursive $1\!\to\!2$
phase-space split with log-uniform intermediate masses (SARGE/HAAG core, reaches the soft/collinear
corners of \emph{any} massless final state) mixed $50/50$ with flat \textsc{rambo} for the bulk: no
\code{uugg} antenna, no $y_{\min}$/$\sqrt s$ coordinate anywhere. \emph{Clean isolation of the
sampling effect:} both arms train the identical HETEROSC net with a \emph{detached} $\sigma$ backbone
at $\beta{=}1$, so $\mu$ gets a pure-MSE gradient and trains \emph{exactly} as MSE in both arms while
$\sigma$ is a read-only calibration head that is always live to score proposals (no per-round
$\sigma$-fit subprocess, no \code{grow\_sigma\_head} at each round). The arms differ in one thing: the
keep rule: \textbf{base} keeps a \emph{uniform} subset of the proposal (the ``best hand-coded
sampler'' control), \textbf{sigma} keeps $\propto\!\sigma(x)^\gamma$ without replacement. Matched
totals both arms ($4000$ steps, $N{=}300$k over $R{=}10$ rounds, warm-started from \code{base22} grown
$1$ch$\to$$2$ch), fresh data every round both arms, so the \emph{only} difference between base and
sigma is \emph{where} the increment is drawn. Held-out eval on a fixed disjoint deep-IR set ($100$k,
$y_{\min}$ down to $10^{-8}$, seed $777$), scored on raw $\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$ per $y_{\min}$ decade
(preprocessing-independent, so base/sigma are strictly like-for-like); the eval reloads the run's
\code{model\_run0\_best.pt} and can run in-process at the run tail (\code{--heldout\_eval}), no separate
job. Tooling: \code{l2\_online\_uugg.py}, \code{gen\_ir\_democratic.py}, \code{grow\_sigma\_head.py},
\code{make\_heldout\_uugg.py}, \code{eval\_heldout\_uugg.py}; plots \code{plot\_l2\_uugg\{,\_gamma,
\_abs\}.py}.

\paragraph{The process-agnostic base generator: ``democratic'' phase space.} The one component that
makes the whole method coordinate-free is the base proposal (\code{gen\_ir\_democratic.py}); it must
reach the singular regions of \emph{any} massless process with \emph{no} process-specific antenna or
channel list; otherwise the physics has been smuggled back in. It uses a single generic fact: the
IR singularities of a massless amplitude all live at \emph{small invariants}, soft ($E_i\!\to\!0$)
and collinear ($s_{ij}=(p_i+p_j)^2\!\to\!0$), and both are reached ``democratically'' (equal weight
per decade of invariant) by a recursive $1\!\to\!2$ split of the total invariant mass with the
intermediate masses drawn \textbf{flat in log}. This is the process-agnostic \emph{core} of the
SARGE/HAAG antenna generators (Draggiotis--van~Hameren--Kleiss), stripped of their process-specific
antenna/channel weights. \emph{Construction} (CM frame, $N$ final legs of mass $m_i$, total energy
$\sqrt s$): start a ``blob'' $Q=(\sqrt s,\mathbf 0)$ of invariant mass $M_N=\sqrt s$; for $k=N,\dots,2$
split the current blob (mass $M_k$) into an emitted particle $p_k$ (mass $m_k$) plus a remaining blob
(mass $M_{k-1}$), drawing $M_{k-1}^2$ \emph{log-uniform} in $[(m_{\rm rest}+M_{\rm floor})^2,\,
(M_k-m_k)^2]$, where $m_{\rm rest}=\sum_{j>k} m_j$ is the least mass the leftover cluster can carry.
A \emph{small} $M_{k-1}$ is exactly a soft/collinear leftover cluster, so log-uniform sampling visits
those corners across all scales at once. Two-body kinematics fix the back-to-back momenta in the
blob's rest frame at an isotropic direction; a boost by the blob's lab momentum puts them in the lab;
the final blob \emph{is} $p_1$. Momentum conservation and on-shellness are \textbf{exact by
construction} (no reject-for-validity). $M_{\rm floor}=\sqrt s\,\sqrt{y_{\rm lo}}$ sets the deepest
invariant reached, the coordinate-free analogue of the antenna's $y_{\rm lo}$ cut. \emph{One
implementation subtlety:} the \emph{last} split ($k{=}2$) does \emph{not} draw a free mass: the
remaining blob already \emph{is} the final particle, so its mass is pinned to $m_1$ (drawing it freely
would leave the last leg off-shell); for $N{=}2$ this is the only split and gives a back-to-back
massless pair. \emph{Why no Jacobian appears anywhere:} we deliberately never compute this proposal's
density or weight, because training regresses $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ \emph{pointwise}, only \emph{where}
the events sit matters, not how often the generator would have put them there (the same argument that
lets $\sigma$-reweighting skip an importance correction). So \emph{any} construction that yields valid
on-shell, momentum-conserving events covering the soft/collinear corners is admissible; this one,
unlike a bespoke per-process antenna, is process-agnostic: the \emph{same} \code{democratic\_draw}
generates $u\bar u$, $u\bar u g$, and $u\bar u gg$ with no per-process code, verified (\code{--validate},
\code{ir\_report}) to reach $y_{\min}\!<\!10^{-6}$ and small soft fraction $x_{\min}$ while staying
momentum-conserving and massless. It is mixed $50/50$ with flat \textsc{rambo}, which the democratic
draw under-covers: \textsc{rambo} fills the non-singular $O(1)$ bulk, the democratic half fills the
IR corners.

\emph{Result (3 seeds each of base and $\gamma\!\in\!\{1,2,3,5,10\}$).} $\sigma$-driven generation
beats uniform, and the gain \textbf{concentrates in the deep IR and grows monotonically with the
concentration exponent $\gamma$}. Log-flat-per-decade MSE, $\sigma$/base (mean over the $7$ $y_{\min}$
decades): $92\%,81\%,78\%,76\%,74\%$ at $\gamma{=}1,2,3,5,10$, a real but \emph{saturating} overall
gain. The deepest decade ($y_{\min}\!<\!10^{-6}$) tells the sharper story: $\sigma$/base
$=90\%,70\%,66\%,62\%,\mathbf{55\%}$: the deep-IR error is nearly \emph{halved} at $\gamma{=}10$.
\emph{Correction from the extended sweep:} an earlier reading of this series as ``still falling'' at
$\gamma{=}10$ was too optimistic. Pushing to $\gamma=20,30$ (single seed, so compared within the seed-0
series) shows the deepest decade \textbf{saturating}: ratio $0.529\to0.525\to0.509$ over
$\gamma=10\to20\to30$, i.e.\ $\sim\!4\%$ of further gain for a trebling of $\gamma$, while the bulk
penalty keeps climbing, $1.30\to1.55\to1.63$, and the overall metric degrades monotonically past its
$\gamma\!\approx\!3$ optimum until it is $\sim\!12\%$ \emph{worse} than uniform at $\gamma{=}30$.
So the useful range really does end near $\gamma\!\approx\!3$--$10$: beyond it the trade-off buys almost
nothing deep and pays steadily in the bulk (Fig.~\ref{fig:l2_gamma_response_extended}). The overall metric saturates because the reallocation has a real cost: the shallowest
(bulk) decade $y_{\min}\!>\!0.1$ \emph{rises} $107\%\!\to\!128\%$ over $\gamma{=}1\!\to\!10$, an
accelerating penalty that eats the deep-IR gain in the log-flat average. So $\gamma\!\approx\!3$--$5$ is
the sweet spot for the overall metric; larger $\gamma$ buys still-lower deep-IR error by paying more in
the bulk, exactly the water-filling trade-off above, now traversed by a single coordinate-free
knob. \textbf{This closes the steering layer: on a process where no one-parameter hand density fits, the model's own
$\sigma$, raised to a power, generates a strictly better training set than uniform, with no physics
coordinate, no reference density, no loss correction, isolated from coverage- and loss-function
confounds over $3$ seeds.} (This supersedes the confounded $ee\to u\bar u$ steering numbers from the earlier
session, which are retracted.) See Figs.~\ref{fig:l2_uugg_perdecade}--\ref{fig:l2_uugg_perdecade_abs}.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{l2_uugg_perdecade.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{Steering on $e^+e^-\to u\bar u gg$: $\sigma$-driven generation vs uniform ($\gamma{=}1$,
  $3$ seeds).} Held-out deep-IR MSE per $y_{\min}$ decade. Both arms train the \emph{identical}
  HETEROSC net with a detached $\sigma$ backbone at $\beta{=}1$ (so $\mu$ trains exactly as MSE in
  both) on matched totals with fresh data every round: the \emph{only} difference is the keep rule,
  uniform vs $\propto\sigma^\gamma$. The $\sigma$ arm wins, and the gain is concentrated at small
  $y_{\min}$: the model's own uncertainty, with no physics coordinate and no reference density, builds
  a better training set than the best hand-coded sampler.}
  \label{fig:l2_uugg_perdecade}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{l2_uugg_gamma.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{The concentration exponent $\gamma$ traverses a water-filling trade-off.} Per-decade
  $\sigma$/base ratio and the deep-vs-bulk split against $\gamma\in\{1,2,3,5,10\}$. The deepest decade
  ($y_{\min}\!<\!10^{-6}$) falls to $\sim\!\mathbf{55}\%$ of base ($90,70,66,62,55\%$) while the
  shallowest bulk decade \emph{rises} $107\!\to\!128\%$. So the log-flat average is best around
  $\gamma\!\approx\!3$--$5$ and larger $\gamma$ buys deep-IR accuracy by paying in the bulk: one
  coordinate-free scalar knob sliding along the equimarginal allocation trade-off. Extending the sweep to
  $\gamma=20,30$ shows the deep gain \emph{saturating} rather than continuing
  (Fig.~\ref{fig:l2_gamma_response_extended}).}
  \label{fig:l2_uugg_gamma}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{l2_uugg_perdecade_abs.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{Absolute per-decade MSE for all $\gamma$.} The same runs as
  Fig.~\ref{fig:l2_uugg_gamma} on an absolute scale rather than as a ratio to base, so the size of each
  decade's error is visible alongside the relative gain: the deep decades carry MSE orders of
  magnitude above the bulk, which is why a log-flat-per-decade metric
  (Fig.~\ref{fig:q2_logflat}) is the honest way to score the trade-off.}
  \label{fig:l2_uugg_perdecade_abs}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Transfer of the coordinate-free loop to harder divergences ($u\bar u g$ multi-scale,
$u\bar u ggg$ more legs).} Because the steering loop carries \emph{no} process coordinate, it should transfer
with \emph{no} per-process tuning; we stress it on two structurally-different divergences by changing
only the process registry (\code{--process}: PDG/multiplicity, the compiled standalone labeller, and
the round-0 frozen-stats set): same base22, same democratic generator, same finetune HPs. Held-out
sets are drawn from the same democratic base (uug enriched near $M_Z$ so the resonance region is
well-measured), scored per $y_{\min}$ decade and, for uug, per $\sqrt s$ region.
\emph{(i) $u\bar u g$, a genuine multi-scale process:} the $s$-channel $\gamma^\ast/Z$ propagator
gives a resonance at $\sqrt s\!\approx\!M_Z$ (the window bottom) \emph{and} the gluon gives soft/collinear
IR, two competing extremes at once. The open question was whether a \emph{single} $\sigma^\gamma$ knob
can budget across both, or must go multi-modal. It budgets, and it does so by finding the genuinely-hard
extreme: the entire difficulty lives in the \textbf{resonance}, not the IR continuum, which \code{base22}
already fits to $\mathrm{MSE}\!\approx\!9\times10^{-4}$. $\sigma$-driven generation cuts the Z-peak error
$8.3\times10^{-2}\!\to\!3.3\times10^{-2}$ ($2.5\times$) and the deep-IR$\,\cap\,$Z-peak corner
$9.0\times10^{-2}\!\to\!2.2\times10^{-2}$ ($4\times$) at $\gamma{=}3$, while leaving the already-easy
continuum \emph{untouched} ($8.6\times10^{-4}$, unchanged), with overall MSE $2.2\times10^{-2}\to
1.4\times10^{-2}\to9.3\times10^{-3}$ at $\gamma{=}0,1,3$. So one scalar $\sigma$ automatically identifies
the resonance as the sparse region and reallocates there; it need \emph{not} be multi-modal.
\emph{(ii) $u\bar u ggg$, higher multiplicity:} here $\sigma$-driven generation gives essentially
\emph{nothing} (overall $5.68\!\to\!5.67\!\to\!5.65\times10^{-2}$ at $\gamma{=}0,1,3$), because with three
gluons the divergence is \emph{diffuse}: every $y_{\min}$ decade is comparably hard
($\sim\!3\times10^{-2}$--$1.5\times10^{-1}$), with no thin sparse region to target; reallocating within a
uniformly-hard set cannot help (Figs.~\ref{fig:l2_uug_multiscale}, \ref{fig:l2_uuggg_perdecade}).
The obvious objection, that $u\bar u ggg$ was simply not concentrated \emph{hard enough}, since
$u\bar u gg$ needed $\gamma\!\approx\!3$--$10$, was tested directly and \textbf{fails}: pushing to
$\gamma=10,20$ does not unlock a gain, it \emph{reverses} the small one. The deep-IR ratio turns over,
$0.989,0.976,1.035,1.079$ at $\gamma=1,3,10,20$, so by $\gamma{=}20$ the $\sigma$ arm is $8\%$
\emph{worse than uniform} (overall $5.65\!\to\!6.07\!\to\!6.48\times10^{-2}$). Harder concentration on a
diffuse divergence just starves the rest of an already-uniformly-hard set. That contrast, saturation
for $u\bar u gg$, and reversal for $u\bar u ggg$, is the sharpest evidence for the principle below
(Fig.~\ref{fig:l2_gamma_response_extended}).

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{l2_gamma_response_extended.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{Pushing the concentration exponent: saturation vs reversal.} $\sigma$-arm/base MSE
  ratio against $\gamma$ (seed 0), by $y_{\min}$ band. \emph{Left, $u\bar u gg$ (concentrated):} the
  deepest decade improves steeply to $\gamma\!\approx\!10$ then \textbf{saturates} ($0.53\to0.51$ over
  $\gamma=10\to30$) while the bulk penalty keeps growing ($1.30\to1.63$): the water-filling trade-off
  running out of anything left to buy. \emph{Right, $u\bar u ggg$ (diffuse):} the curve is nearly flat
  and \textbf{turns over} above $\gamma\!\approx\!3$, crossing the uniform baseline so that harder
  concentration is actively worse. Concentration of the error, not depth of the divergence, is what
  decides whether $\sigma$-steering can pay.}
  \label{fig:l2_gamma_response_extended}
\end{figure}
