\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{l2_uug_multiscale.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{$e^+e^-\to u\bar u g$: the multi-scale test (Z-resonance \emph{and} IR).} Left:
  held-out MSE per $y_{\min}$ decade for base (uniform) vs $\sigma$-driven generation at
  $\gamma{=}1,3$. Right: the same error split by $\sqrt s$ region. The difficulty lives almost
  entirely in the \emph{Z-peak}, not the IR continuum (which \code{base22} already fits to
  $\sim\!9\times10^{-4}$); one scalar $\sigma^\gamma$ finds the resonance on its own ($2.5\times$
  at the peak, $4\times$ in the deep-IR$\,\cap\,$peak corner), while leaving the easy continuum
  untouched. The steering density need \emph{not} be multi-modal to serve two competing extremes.}
  \label{fig:l2_uug_multiscale}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{l2_uuggg_perdecade.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{$e^+e^-\to u\bar u ggg$: more legs, a \emph{diffuse} divergence.} Held-out MSE per
  $y_{\min}$ decade, base vs $\sigma$-driven at $\gamma{=}1,3$: the three curves lie on top of each
  other. With three gluons every decade is comparably hard, so there is no thin sparse region for
  $\sigma$ to reallocate \emph{into}: the contrast with Fig.~\ref{fig:l2_uug_multiscale} is the
  content of the concentration principle below.}
  \label{fig:l2_uuggg_perdecade}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{A massive final state ($e^+e^-\to b\bar b g$), and a genuine out-of-sample test of the
$\sigma$-contrast diagnostic.} The loop had only been stressed on massless finals. The obvious
candidate, the $t\bar t$ threshold, is ruled out, and that is itself a measured result rather than a
deferral: at tree level $ee\to t\bar t$ has \textbf{no threshold divergence at all}. $|\mathcal M|^2$
spans only $8.7\times$ in total and is \emph{flattest} at threshold ($\mathrm{std}\log|\mathcal M|^2
= 0.022$ for $\beta<0.05$ against $0.665$ in the bulk), because the $2m_t$ cusp is a phase-space and
cross-section feature, not an amplitude one; running it would have produced a guaranteed null that
says nothing about the method. $b\bar b g$ is the replacement, and the direct
massive analogue of $u\bar u g$ (same $Z$ resonance, same soft gluon), but the \emph{collinear}
limit is regulated by $m_b$ (the dead cone), which softens one of the two singular directions; the
dead-cone scale $m_b^2/s\!\approx\!2\times10^{-5}$--$3\times10^{-3}$ sits inside the $y_{\min}$ window,
so the mass reshapes the divergence rather than relabelling it ($|\mathcal M|^2$ spans $7\times10^{10}$).
The generator needed \emph{no} change: \code{democratic\_draw} already yields exact on-shell massive
momenta (verified to $10^{-13}$); only a registry row, the mass-correct collinear variable
$y_{ij}=2p_i\!\cdot\!p_j/s$ (identical to $s_{ij}/s$ for massless legs, so nothing above moves), and a
standalone slot-order check were required. That convention is \emph{per-build}, not global
(\code{ee\_ttbar} takes production order bit-identically, \code{ee\_uu} needs its final pair
swapped), so every new standalone must be checked; here it was settled by the sign
of $A_{\rm FB}$ at the $Z$ pole against the already-validated $u\bar u g$, a data-free substitute where
no production data exists (both negative under the same construction, with $|A_{\rm FB}(b)|>|A_{\rm
FB}(u)|$ as the SM requires; the swapped order anti-correlates). \emph{What makes this more than one
more transfer:} the $\sigma$-contrast diagnostic (previous paragraphs) was used to \textbf{predict the
result before the $\sigma$ arm ran}. From the base arm alone: a $Z$-peak contrast of $2.51$ (between
$u\bar u g$'s $4.48$ and $u\bar u gg$'s $1.83$) $\Rightarrow$ a \emph{moderate} $Z$-peak gain, milder than
massless $u\bar u g$; a deep-IR contrast of only $1.61$ $\Rightarrow$ a \emph{weak} IR gain, the dead cone
having flattened the collinear enhancement. Both held: at $\gamma{=}3$ the $\sigma$ arm cuts the $Z$-peak
MSE to $\mathbf{0.56\times}$ (predicted ${\sim}0.50$) and the shoulder to $0.50\times$, while the pure
deep-IR ($y_{\min}<10^{-5}$) moves only to $0.85\times$ (predicted ${\sim}0.71$) and the continuum to
$0.82\times$, with overall $2.15\!\to\!1.29\times10^{-2}$ ($0.60\times$), log-flat $0.80\times$. So the gain
is \textbf{$Z$-driven, exactly where the contrast pointed}, and the mass's suppression of the IR direction
is visible as the weaker deep-IR gain: the diagnostic called both the \emph{magnitude} and the
\emph{location} on a process it was never tuned on (Fig.~\ref{fig:l2_bbbarg}). \emph{Caveat: single seed;}
a $3$-seed repeat would firm the numbers, but the sign and the region-ordering are the claim.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{l2_bbbarg.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{$e^+e^-\to b\bar b g$ (massive): $\sigma$-steering pays where the $\sigma$-contrast
  predicted.} \emph{(a)} Held-out MSE per $y_{\min}$ decade (mass-correct $y_{\min}=\min_{ij}2p_i\!\cdot\!
  p_j/s$), base vs $\sigma$-driven $\gamma{=}3$. \emph{(b)} The same error by $\sqrt s$ region, with the
  \emph{pre-recorded} predictions (navy) beside the measured ratios (crimson). The gain concentrates at
  the $Z$-peak ($0.56\times$) and shoulder ($0.50\times$), not the IR continuum ($0.82\times$): the
  $m_b$ dead cone softens the collinear direction, so, as the low deep-IR $\sigma$-contrast predicted,
  there is less to gain there. The contrast, computed from the base arm with no A/B, called both the
  size and the location of the effect on a process the diagnostic was never fit on.}
  \label{fig:l2_bbbarg}
\end{figure}

\begin{principle}[$\sigma$-driven generation helps iff hardness is \emph{concentrated}]
The single-knob $\sigma^\gamma$ keep-rule wins exactly when the error is concentrated in an identifiable,
data-sparse sub-region the model can point to: the deep soft$\cap$collinear corner of $u\bar u gg$, the
$Z$ resonance of $u\bar u g$ (which it finds automatically, without a multi-modal density, leaving the
already-learned IR continuum alone). It gives $\sim\!$nothing when the difficulty is diffuse across the
whole divergence, as at higher multiplicity ($u\bar u ggg$). Concentration, not divergence depth per se, is
what $\sigma$-steering can exploit. It also survives the move to a \emph{massive} final state ($b\bar b g$),
where it correctly localises to the $Z$ resonance and the $m_b$ dead cone's suppression of the collinear
direction shows up as a weaker IR gain, both predicted in advance by the $\sigma$-contrast. The $\gamma$ sweep separates the two regimes cleanly: on a concentrated
divergence the gain \emph{saturates} with harder concentration (there is a finite amount of misplaced
budget to recover), whereas on a diffuse one it \emph{reverses} and drops below uniform, so a flat or
turning $\gamma$-response is the diagnostic that no targetable sparse region exists, and the right move is
more capacity or more data everywhere, not smarter allocation. Concentration also has a directly
measurable order parameter that does \emph{not} require running the A/B: the \textbf{$\sigma$-contrast}
of the baseline model, $\langle\sigma\rangle_{\rm singular}/\langle\sigma\rangle_{\rm bulk}$, which
orders these three processes exactly as their measured gains do (next paragraph,
Fig.~\ref{fig:l2_sigma_vs_divergence}).
\end{principle}

\paragraph{$\sigma$ resolved against the divergence coordinate, and a \emph{pre-flight} test for
the concentration principle.} Everything above scores $\sigma$ only through its downstream effect (the
A/B on held-out MSE). Resolving $\sigma$ directly against the variable that \emph{defines} each
divergence both confirms the mechanism and, unexpectedly, makes the concentration principle
\emph{predictive}. The stored held-out evaluations already carry what is needed: \code{score\_and\_save}
saves $\sigma$ row-aligned with $y_{\min}$ and $\sqrt s$, so this costs no GPU
(\code{plot\_sigma\_vs\_divergence.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:l2_sigma_vs_divergence}). Multiplying the stored
(preprocessed-unit) $\sigma$ by the run's amplitude standardization scale puts $\sigma$ and the actual
RMSE in the same $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ units, so the two curves are directly comparable and their gap
\emph{is} the calibration. \emph{(i) $\sigma$ does rise inside the divergence, and it is well calibrated
there.} On $u\bar u g$, $\sigma$ climbs $4.5\times$ from the continuum into the $Z$ pole and plateaus
across it, tracking a $10\times$ rise in the true error; per region $\sigma/\mathrm{RMSE}=0.77$ (peak),
$1.01$ (shoulder), $1.69$ (continuum), accurate where it matters and merely \emph{conservative} in the
easy bulk. Across the $u\bar ugg$ $y_{\min}$ decades the same ratio stays in $0.85$--$1.24$ except the
very deepest bin ($0.53$, under-covering where the error is largest). \emph{(ii) The sharpness of that
rise predicts the gain.} Define the \textbf{$\sigma$-contrast} $\langle\sigma\rangle_{\rm
singular}/\langle\sigma\rangle_{\rm bulk}$, measured on the \emph{base} (uniform-keep) arm alone. It
orders the three processes exactly as the measured steering gain does: $u\bar u g$ (Z peak) contrast
$4.48\Rightarrow$ $\sigma$/base MSE $0.40$; $u\bar u gg$ (deep IR) $1.83\Rightarrow0.53$; $u\bar u ggg$
(deep IR) $1.29\Rightarrow0.97$ (nothing). Because the contrast needs only one forward pass of the
baseline model (no second arm, no A/B), it converts ``is this divergence concentrated?'' from a
post-hoc reading of the outcome into a \textbf{cheap test you can run before committing the compute},
and gives the concentration principle a directly measurable order parameter.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \pnl{l2_sigma_vs_divergence_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{l2_sigma_vs_divergence_b.pdf} \\[1ex]
  \pnl{l2_sigma_vs_divergence_c.pdf}\hfill\pnl{l2_sigma_vs_divergence_d.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{The learned $\sigma$ rises inside the divergence, and how sharply it rises
  predicts whether $\sigma$-steering pays.} \emph{(a)--(c)} Mean $\sigma$ and actual RMSE against the
  coordinate defining each divergence, both in $\log|\mathcal M|^2$ units (so the vertical gap is the
  calibration), all on the base/uniform arm. $u\bar u g$: $\sigma$ climbs $4.5\times$ into the $Z$ pole
  and plateaus across it, following a $10\times$ rise in the error. $u\bar u gg$: a $1.8\times$ rise into
  the deep IR. $u\bar u ggg$: nearly flat ($1.3\times$): there is no thin region for $\sigma$ to point
  at. \emph{(d)} The pre-flight diagnostic those three feed: the $\sigma$-contrast
  $\langle\sigma\rangle_{\rm singular}/\langle\sigma\rangle_{\rm bulk}$, measured on the base arm
  alone, against the $\sigma$-arm/base MSE ratio actually measured in that region. They order
  monotonically, so the contrast decides the question with one forward pass and no A/B.}
  \label{fig:l2_sigma_vs_divergence}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{A genuinely Bayesian $\sigma$ (full-network Bayes-by-backprop) does \emph{not} pay for
itself.} The steering $\sigma$ above is a \emph{point} heteroscedastic head, a learned (aleatoric)
spread. The arguably-right signal for deciding what to \emph{generate} is \emph{epistemic}: high where
the training set is sparse, which is exactly what steering wants to fill. We therefore built a genuinely
Bayesian alternative, \textbf{full-network Bayes-by-backprop} (\code{bbb.py}), a mean-field Gaussian
posterior $q(w)=\mathcal N(\mu_w,\sigma_w^2)$ over \emph{every} weight and bias (not MC-dropout, not a
Laplace/last-layer approximation, not an ensemble). \emph{Implementation:} rather than rebuild the
architecture, \code{variationalize()} wraps an already-built, $\mu$P-finalised, \code{base22}-warm-started
net: each existing \code{weight}/\code{bias} is kept \emph{verbatim} as the posterior mean (so $\mu$P
init, the MuAdam per-layer scaling, and the warm start are untouched: the net \emph{starts} as the
deterministic pretrained model and only learns the spread) and a sibling \code{\_rho} parameterises
$\sigma=\mathrm{softplus}(\rho)$, initialised to $\sigma_{\rm rel}$ times that layer's $\mu$P weight RMS
so the injected noise keeps the $\mu$P activation scaling across width; the per-layer Gaussian prior uses
the same scale, and $\rho$ inherits its weight's \code{infshape} (MuAdam asserts one on every parameter).
Training maximises the ELBO ($\mathrm{loss}\mathrel{+}=\beta_{\rm KL}\mathrm{KL}/N$, with $\vlnr$ left as
\emph{pure} MSE so checkpoint selection and the comparison metric stay like-for-like). The model
is \emph{evaluated through its posterior}, never collapsed to a mean-weights forward: the point estimate is
the predictive mean $\mathbb E_q[f]\!\approx\!\tfrac1K\sum_k f(x,w_k)$ (which is \emph{not} $f(x,\mu)$ for a
nonlinear net), and we report the predictive $\sigma$, calibration $\langle z^2\rangle$, Gaussian NLL, and the
$\sigma$-vs-$|\mathrm{err}|$ rank correlation; checkpoint \emph{ranking} scores the same predictive quantity.
Proposals are kept $\propto\sigma_{\rm epi}^\gamma$ with $\sigma_{\rm epi}$ the $K$-sample predictive spread.

\emph{Fair best-vs-best (12-trial single-fidelity DyHPO sweep over $\beta_{\rm KL}\!\in\![10^{-5},10^{-1}]$,
$\sigma_{\rm rel}\!\in\![5\!\times\!10^{-3},0.15]$, $\gamma\!\in\![1,5]$; objective = held-out deep-IR MSE).}
The tuned Bayesian network reaches $2.87\times10^{-2}$, versus $3.01\times10^{-2}$ for uniform generation
($0.95\times$, a real but modest win) and $2.65\times10^{-2}$ for the het-head $\sigma^{\gamma=3}$ baseline
($1.08\times$: \textbf{it loses}). The sweep's \emph{structure} is the informative part:
$\sigma_{\rm rel}$ alone decides the outcome (Spearman $\rho=+0.965$ with the objective, monotone: wider
posterior is uniformly worse; $\sigma_{\rm rel}\!\approx\!0.1$ costs a factor $\sim\!3$), while
$\beta_{\rm KL}$ ($\rho=-0.05$) and even the keep exponent $\gamma$ ($\rho=-0.07$) are \emph{noise} by
comparison, and the optimum sits \textbf{on the lower bound} of the $\sigma_{\rm rel}$ range, i.e.\ the
optimiser is driving the weight posterior toward the \emph{deterministic limit} to buy back point accuracy.
That is the mechanism: Bayes-by-backprop trains under sampled weights, which degrades the mean fit, and the
only way to recover accuracy is to shrink the posterior, which simultaneously removes the epistemic
signal that was the whole point. Calibration is respectable ($\langle z^2\rangle\!\approx\!1.26$, mildly
under-covering as expected for an epistemic-only $\sigma$), but $\sigma_{\rm epi}$ \emph{ranks} the error at
only $\rho\!\approx\!0.17$--$0.20$, barely above the het-head's $0.11$--$0.16$, so it never earns back
the accuracy it costs, on top of $K$ forwards per scoring round ($\sim\!3\times$ slower runs).
\emph{Caveat:} one seed per trial, and the gaps are $5$--$8\%$, comparable to seed scatter, so the safe
claim is ``no evidence the Bayesian network beats the het head'', and ``it beats uniform'' is marginal
(Figs.~\ref{fig:l2_uugg_bbb_vs_het}, \ref{fig:l2_bbb_sweep}). Tooling \code{bbb.py},
\code{sweep/l2\_bbb\_trial.py}, \code{sweep/l2\_bbb\_sweep\_config.yaml} (the sweep drives the steering loop via
\code{generate\_sweep}'s new \code{trial\_script} hook).

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{l2_uugg_bbb_vs_het.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{Epistemic (Bayesian network) vs the het-head $\sigma$ on $e^+e^-\to u\bar u gg$.} Held-out MSE per
  $y_{\min}$ decade. The full-network Bayes-by-backprop arm sits \emph{above} both the het-head
  $\sigma^\gamma$ and the uniform baseline across essentially every decade: training under sampled
  weights costs point accuracy, and the epistemic $\sigma$ does not rank the error well enough
  ($\rho\!\approx\!0.2$ vs $0.15$) to earn it back.}
  \label{fig:l2_uugg_bbb_vs_het}
\end{figure}

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \widerow{\pnl{l2_bbb_sweep_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{l2_bbb_sweep_b.pdf}}
  \caption{\textbf{The fair Bayesian-network HP sweep ($12$ single-fidelity DyHPO trials).} Held-out deep-IR MSE vs
  the initial posterior width $\sigma_{\rm rel}$ (left) and the ELBO KL weight $\beta_{\rm KL}$ (right),
  coloured by the keep exponent $\gamma$; dashed/dotted lines are the het-head and uniform baselines.
  $\sigma_{\rm rel}$ alone sets the outcome (Spearman $+0.965$) and its optimum lands \emph{on the search
  lower bound} (the optimiser drives the weight posterior toward the deterministic limit to buy back
  accuracy), while $\beta_{\rm KL}$ ($-0.05$) and $\gamma$ ($-0.07$) are noise. The best tuned Bayesian network
  (circled) still sits above the het-head line: the accuracy$\leftrightarrow$uncertainty tension is
  structural, not a tuning artefact.}
  \label{fig:l2_bbb_sweep}
\end{figure}

\begin{principle}[For steering generation, what matters is how well $\sigma$ \emph{ranks} error]
A genuinely Bayesian weight posterior is not automatically the better generation signal. What the keep-rule
consumes is a \emph{ranking} of where the model is wrong, and full-network Bayes-by-backprop buys only a
marginally better ranking ($\rho\!\approx\!0.2$ vs $0.15$) at the price of a systematically worse point
fit, and its own HP search runs the posterior width to the deterministic bound to recover that accuracy. The cheap
detached heteroscedastic head remains the better trade on this harness.
\end{principle}

\paragraph{Is $\sigma^\gamma$ the right keep-rule family, or does the optimum need curvature? A
\emph{nested} polynomial test.} The deployed keep rule is $p\!\propto\!\sigma^\gamma$, ``$\gamma$ as a
temperature'', one scalar. We generalise it to a polynomial in $u=\log\sigma-\mathrm{median}$,
$\log w = c_1 u + c_2 u^2 + c_3 u^3$, so the power law is the \emph{nested} member $c_1{=}\gamma,\,
c_2{=}c_3{=}0$ (verified identical to $10^{-16}$): a polynomial that cannot beat $c_2{=}c_3{=}0$ has
genuinely found nothing. The motivation is measured, not aesthetic: $\mathbb E[\mathrm{err}^2\,|\,\sigma]$
on held-out sets is \emph{not} a power law in $\sigma$: fitting $\log\mathbb E$ against $\log\sigma$,
$R^2$ rises $0.89\!\to\!0.99$ ($u\bar u gg$) and $0.68\!\to\!0.93$ ($u\bar u ggg$) from degree $1$ to $2$,
with \emph{positive} curvature, so positive $c_2$ should concentrate extra budget in the high-$\sigma$
tail while staying mild at moderate $\sigma$, exactly what a single large $\gamma$ cannot do (it
over-concentrates everywhere, the measured bulk penalty). A $12$-trial single-fidelity DyHPO sweep over
$(c_1,c_2,c_3)$ on $u\bar u gg$ (objective the log-flat MSE, \emph{not} deep-only, which would just reward
starving the bulk) confirms the \emph{direction}: the best two configs both sit at $c_2\!\approx\!+1.3$--$1.4$
and every negative-$c_2$ config is mid-pack or worse (Fig.~\ref{fig:l2_poly_keep}a). \textbf{But the gain
does not survive seeds.} The single-seed best ($c_2{=}1.30$, log-flat $3.25\times10^{-2}$) looked $3\%$
under the best power law, until one notes that the \emph{$3$-seed} $\sigma^{10}$ arm already averages
$3.25\times10^{-2}$: the ``win'' was against one unlucky power-law seed. Running the polynomial optimum at
$3$ seeds gives $3.22\times10^{-2}\!\pm\!1.2\times10^{-3}$ vs $\sigma^{10}$'s $3.25\times10^{-2}\!\pm\!
7\times10^{-4}$, a $1.0\%$ edge well inside the pooled seed scatter, i.e.\ a \textbf{wash}
(Fig.~\ref{fig:l2_poly_keep}b). So the curvature the error profile shows is real and points the right way,
but on the flat $\gamma$-basin it buys nothing a well-tuned exponent does not already have. \emph{The
temperature picture holds: $\sigma^\gamma$ stays the right practical family: one knob, no measurable
loss.} Tooling: the polynomial keep rule and \code{--objective logflat} in \code{l2\_online\_uugg.py},
\code{sweep/l2\_poly\_sweep\_config.yaml}, \code{plot\_poly\_keep.py}.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \widerow{\pnl{l2_poly_keep_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{l2_poly_keep_b.pdf}}
  \caption{\textbf{Polynomial keep rule vs the $\sigma^\gamma$ power law.} \emph{(a)} The $12$-trial DyHPO
  sweep: log-flat MSE vs the quadratic coefficient $c_2$, coloured by $c_1{=}\gamma$. The power law is the
  nested $c_2{=}0$ line (red dashed); positive $c_2$ is the helpful direction (best two configs at
  $c_2\!\approx\!+1.3$--$1.4$, negative-$c_2$ mid-pack), confirming the $\mathbb E[\mathrm{err}^2|\sigma]$
  curvature. \emph{(b)} The honest $3$-seed comparison: the polynomial optimum ($3.22\times10^{-2}$) and the
  best power-law arm $\sigma^{10}$ ($3.25\times10^{-2}$) overlap heavily seed-to-seed: the $1\%$ edge is
  inside the scatter. Curvature is the right direction but does not robustly beat a well-tuned exponent.}
  \label{fig:l2_poly_keep}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{Uncapped data: the uncertainty tail floors, and more data does not move it.}
Every A/B above is at a fixed data budget. The complementary question (\emph{how much} data a region
actually needs before more is waste) the loop can now answer directly, because the same $\sigma$ it
uses to steer is also a saturation gauge. At each round it records the $\sigma$ distribution over the
\emph{fresh} proposal batch (drawn from the same fixed base every round, so it is a like-for-like
measure of the model's remaining uncertainty over the whole space, unlike a pool statistic, which
drifts as the keep rule reshapes the pool); the $p99$ (the hard tail, i.e.\ the singular region the
bulk has long since left) is the stopping statistic, since a mean would call saturation far too early
(the bulk saturates first). Running $u\bar u gg$ at $N\!\in\!\{75,150,600\}$k (\code{--sat\_log},
\code{plot\_saturation.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:l2_saturation}): the $\sigma$-$p99$ falls steeply and then
\textbf{floors at a level essentially independent of the data budget}: base arm $0.0448$, $0.0468$,
$0.0477$ and $\sigma$ arm $0.0420$, $0.0418$, $0.0427$ at $N=75$k, $150$k, $600$k. \emph{$8\times$ more
generated data does not lower the floor}; for the base arm it is marginally \emph{worse} ($+6.4\%$),
against ${\sim}2\%$ run-to-run reproducibility on a repeat of the whole sweep. (The $\sigma$ arm also
floors ${\sim}8\%$ \emph{below} base at every budget, an independent sign that $\sigma$-steering
removes more of the hard tail than uniform generation.)

\emph{What this does and does not establish: the compute confound.} Every run here uses the
\textbf{same $4000$ steps} at batch $16384$, i.e.\ ${\sim}65$M example-presentations regardless of pool
size: ${\sim}870$ passes over a $75$k pool but only ${\sim}109$ over a $600$k one. Data volume and
per-example optimisation are therefore \emph{confounded}, and the slight \emph{rise} of the base floor
with $N$ is exactly what that confound predicts. So the defensible claim is ``\textbf{at a fixed
optimizer budget, $8\times$ more unique data buys no reduction in residual uncertainty}'', i.e.\ the
binding constraint in this configuration is capacity/optimisation rather than coverage, and \emph{not}
that ${\sim}75$k events is all this region could ever absorb. Separating the two needs a fixed-\emph{epoch}
design (steps scaled with data) or a capacity sweep; that is left open (Sec.~\ref{sec:div-open}). A second,
related caveat: within a run $\sigma$ falls both because the pool grows \emph{and} because the model
trains longer, and the probe cannot separate those: it is the \emph{cross-budget} comparison at
end-of-training that carries the weight, not the within-run decay.

The per-round fractional fall in $p99$ crossing (and staying below) a $2\%$ tolerance is nonetheless a
machine-checkable stopping rule (the \code{--stop\_on\_saturation} latch freezes the pool there and
lets training continue), so the loop can add data until the tail stops responding instead of running to
a fixed cap. And it makes the ``add more capacity, not more data'' half of the concentration principle
actionable: a floored $\sigma$-tail that no longer falls as data is added is the signal to stop
generating and grow the model or the horizon.

\begin{figure}[H]
  \centering
  \includegraphics{l2_saturation.pdf}
  \caption{\textbf{At a fixed optimizer budget the uncertainty tail floors regardless of data volume.}
  \emph{(a)} $\sigma$-$p99$ over the fresh proposal batch vs pool size, for three data budgets on
  $u\bar u gg$. The curves do not collapse on pool (a larger budget has taken fewer optimizer steps by
  the time it reaches a given pool, so its live $\sigma$ sits higher there); what matters is the
  end-of-training floor, which is flat in the budget: base $0.0448/0.0468/0.0477$, $\sigma$
  $0.0420/0.0418/0.0427$ at $N{=}75/150/600$k. $8\times$ more data does not lower it, and for the base
  arm marginally raises it. \emph{Caveat:} all runs share the same $4000$ steps, so a larger pool gets
  proportionally less optimisation per event: the honest reading is ``more data buys nothing \emph{at
  fixed compute}'', not that $75$k saturates the region absolutely. \emph{(b)} The per-round fractional
  fall in $p99$; crossing below the $2\%$ tolerance band is the stopping rule the
  \code{--stop\_on\_saturation} latch uses.}
  \label{fig:l2_saturation}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Hand-off: open items}\label{sec:div-open}
Genuinely-unfinished / future work for this line (the completed findings are the subsection above):
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Disentangle data from compute in the saturation result.} The $\sigma$-tail floor is flat
    in the data budget, but every run held the step count fixed at $4000$, so more data meant less
    optimisation per event and the two axes are confounded (see the saturation paragraph). Two clean
    follow-ups: (i) a \emph{fixed-epoch} sweep (scale \code{total\_steps} with $N$ so every event gets the
    same number of passes): if the floor then falls with $N$, the constraint was compute, not data;
    (ii) a \emph{capacity} axis at fixed data (vary \code{num\_heads}): if the floor falls with width,
    that confirms ``grow the model'' as the right response to a floored tail, which is currently an
    inference rather than a measurement. Cheap: the runs are ${\sim}13$--$45$ min each.
  \item \textbf{The $\mu$P-LLoCa $\sigma$-head subset is now on \code{jeanzay}} (cherry-picked, not a
    full \code{heterosc} merge): the $2$-channel (mean, softplus $\sigma$) HETEROSC output head with
    \code{detach\_sigma\_backbone}/\code{sigma\_after\_pool}, used by the steering loop above. Cosmetic
    left-over from the wider \code{heterosc} branch, if ever merged: the HETEROSC validation
    ``combined'' display still prints $0$ via the geometric mean, which is harmless since training and
    checkpoint selection use the arithmetic mean.
  \item \textbf{Seed-replicate the massive final state, and find a process carrying all three
    limits at once.} The $b\bar b g$ result above (Fig.~\ref{fig:l2_bbbarg}) is single-seed; a
    $3$-seed repeat would firm the magnitudes, though the sign and the region-ordering are what the
    claim rests on. Still missing is a single process carrying soft, collinear, and resonant
    structure simultaneously, which is the sharpest available test of whether one $\sigma^\gamma$
    knob can budget across three competing extremes rather than two.
  \item \textbf{The \emph{cheap} epistemic approximations, and a seed-replicated confirmation}
    (full-network Bayes-by-backprop lost; see the ``ranking'' principle above).
    Two loose ends. (i) The Bayesian network was the most expensive way to get an epistemic $\sigma$; the cheap
    approximations were never tried: \textbf{MC-dropout} and a \textbf{Laplace/last-layer} posterior are
    $\approx$free (no $2\times$ parameters, no training under weight noise, so they should \emph{not} pay
    the Bayesian network's point-accuracy penalty), and a \textbf{deep ensemble} ($k\times$ training) is the quality ceiling.
    Since the diagnosis was ``the Bayesian network's accuracy loss, not its ranking, is what sinks it'', an epistemic
    $\sigma$ that costs \emph{no} accuracy is the obvious next probe: does it keep the
    $\rho\!\approx\!0.2$ ranking without the penalty? (ii) The Bayesian-network verdict rests on single-seed trials with
    $5$--$8\%$ gaps, comparable to seed scatter; $3$ seeds at the swept optimum would firm ``loses to the
    het head'' from suggestive to settled. Also untested: the claimed \emph{annealing} property, that epistemic
    $\sigma$ should \emph{shrink} as coverage improves, giving a natural stopping signal the point head lacks.
\end{itemize}

% ==========================================================================
\section{Hand-off: priors and representations (planned, not started)}\label{sec:prior-open}
Planned next directions, collected while the Jean Zay results were unreachable (CC-IN2P3
migration, September 2026). They come from two external lines of work read against what this
project has already tested: LLM-conditioned event generation (arXiv:2606.23791, Bahl, Plehn,
Schiller, Sivagnanalingam) and the synthetic-prior tabular foundation models TabICL/TabICLv2
(arXiv:2502.05564, arXiv:2602.11139, Qu, Holzm\"uller, Varoquaux, Le Morvan). Already taken
from the first and therefore \emph{not} listed again: the graph-transformer diagram encoder
(Sec.~\ref{sec:levers}, dropped in-distribution at $448$ datasets), the descriptive
particle encoding (Principle~\ref{prin:zeroshot}), continuous mass conditioning, and the
fine-tune data-efficiency protocol (Sec.~\ref{sec:transfer}).

The organising question is \textbf{which kind of prior helps amplitude regression}: none,
physics (our pretraining), language (a pretrained LLM), or a synthetic function class (a
PFN-style prior). Every arm below is evaluated the same way, on one held-out process, as a
curve of $\vlnr$ against fine-tune events, with the seed bands of Sec.~\ref{sec:transfer}.
\begin{enumerate}
  \item \textbf{Pretrained LLM backbone, three arms.} (a) Qwen2.5-0.5B partially fine-tuned
    as in arXiv:2606.23791 (frozen copy of the weights for the text tokens, trainable copy for
    the momentum tokens and the event token, KV cache of the frozen prefix; their CFM head
    replaced by our regression head on standardised $\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$); (b) the \emph{same}
    architecture from random initialisation, the control that isolates the language prior from
    the size of the transformer; (c) $\mu$P-LLoCa physics-pretrained, i.e.\ the existing
    transfer arm. Practicalities: no bf16 on V100 (fp32 or fp16 with loss scaling; the trainable
    0.5B copy with Adam state is ${\sim}8$\,GB in fp32), $\mu$P does not apply so $\eta$ goes
    through DyHPO, Lorentz equivariance is given up on purpose, and the final LayerNorm of the
    LLM is reset to unity as they do. This is the expensive item and deserves its own worktree.
  \item \textbf{Frozen LLM as a process-text encoder for LLoCa (cheap first step).} Embed the
    \code{in-interm-out} string (\code{e+ e- > [z, a] > u u~ g}) once per process with a frozen
    Qwen, cache the vector, and feed it through the \code{d\_diag} slot in place of the diagram
    encoder's $E(\text{process})$. Zero cost in the training loop; tests the paper's headline
    modality (their text $\approx$ edge-list on hold-outs) without touching the backbone.
  \item \textbf{Re-judge the diagram channel on the question it was built for.} The paper's
    diagram gain is not in-distribution (${\sim}0.01$ AUC, matching our ``static conditioning
    buys nothing'') but hold-out zero-shot and ${\sim}500\times$ fine-tune data efficiency.
    Our drop decision (Sec.~\ref{sec:bigrun}) used the converged in-distribution bulk only. One
    A/B: zero-shot and fine-tune-size curves with \code{use\_diagrams} on/off on a held-out
    process. The code exists.
  \item \textbf{Structured hold-out taxonomy.} Our zero-shot evidence is two processes. With the
    $448$-process catalog, hold out by the symmetry probed (their Table~1): flavour and isospin
    degeneracy, $e/\mu$ universality, Higgs self-coupling, quartic gauge topologies, $\tau$
    Yukawa. Gives a map of which structure transfers instead of one number. Recipe design is
    CPU-only and can be done now; evaluation needs a pretrained model. Sharpest sub-case: their
    embeddings transferred PDF-driven shape but not the matrix-element-driven threshold; for us
    the matrix element \emph{is} the target, so novel-vertex hold-outs are the decisive test.
  \item \textbf{TabICLv2 as a zero-training baseline.} An amplitude dataset is a table
    ($4n$ momentum components, property vectors, \code{amp\_orders} $\to$ $\log|\mathcal{M}|^2$;
    ${\le}100$ features, $10^4$--$10^6$ rows, inside its $10^6\times500$ envelope). Inference
    weights are public, prediction is one forward pass with the training table in context, no
    gradient step. Expected to lose badly on precision (a generic SCM prior knows nothing of
    poles or Lorentz structure, and outputs 999 quantiles tuned for RMSE-level noise) but it is
    the natural competitor in the few-hundred-event regime where the transfer curves live.
    Also a curious zero-shot test if several processes are concatenated into one table with
    the property vectors as features.
  \item \textbf{Synthetic-amplitude prior (the novel one).} The PFN idea transferred to
    physics: pretrain $\mu$P-LLoCa on unlimited \emph{fake matrix elements}, random rational
    functions of Mandelstam invariants with random pole positions and widths, random polynomial
    numerators in the spin-sum style, random masses and multiplicities, Lorentz-invariant by
    construction and with the right singularity structure but no real process behind them; then
    fine-tune on real processes. Tests whether what transfers from the $25$/$448$-process
    pretraining is physics or merely ``smooth function of invariants with poles'', and removes
    MadGraph from the pretraining loop entirely. The generator is a CPU script and can be
    written and validated before any GPU budget is allocated.
  \item \textbf{Smaller items.} Their long-pretraining schedule (grow the per-process pool
    mid-run, resample per epoch, cosine tail) captured a resonance the short schedule never did
    and bears on the data-versus-compute item of Sec.~\ref{sec:div-open}. Their frozen-plus-
    trainable-copy fine-tune is a PEFT variant absent from the \code{fine\_tune} block (LoRA,
    EWC, freezing, layer decay). Separate per-diagram prefix tokens instead of the pooled
    readout ranked last: they found pooled marginally better.
\end{enumerate}
Suggested order given the compute situation: 2, 3, 4 need only the small recipes we can
generate here plus a modest pretraining; 5 needs no training at all; 6 starts as a CPU
generator; 1 waits for a compute plan.

\section{Hand-off: generating the catalog}\label{sec:catalog-open}
\code{catalog\_v2} (Sec.~\ref{sec:catalog}) is generated (train, scan, hold-outs); the first
pretraining wave (Sec.~\ref{sec:catv2-census}) leaves these decisions before the next one:
\begin{itemize}
  \item \textbf{Re-measure with the generation feature at the full horizon.} The
    $1000$-step A/B (Sec.~\ref{sec:catv2-census}) resolves $ud\to ud$ and makes $ds\to ds$
    at one loop representable but not held; whether $8601$ steps hold both is open. At
    $1000$ steps the three fixes together are measured (Tables~\ref{tab:catv2_short} and
    \ref{tab:catv2_short_smix}, the latter on the shaped measure every run uses from now on); what
    only the full horizon can tell is whether the signed one-loop $2\to3$ class learns with
    more steps, and where $\tau$ lands the trade between the bulk and the best-learned tail. Three
    trials at the full horizon are $\sim9$ GPU-hours. It is a fresh sweep, not an extension: wave 0's DyHPO state holds
    observations under the old target and without the $\tau$ dimension.
  \item \textbf{Hold-outs.} $ds\to ds$ and $cs\to cs$ test nothing as ``relabels of $us\to
    us$''; the one-loop entries of hold-out trees must leave the train recipe (or the trees
    must enter it), whichever the order-axis test wants.
  \item \textbf{Which aggregation for the wave.} The needle was a missing input
    (Table~\ref{tab:catv2_offshell}; the wave needs the diagram sidecars present, masks
    $478/478$ in the log); what the aggregation decides is the starvation and the
    $2\to4$ class, and the wave should not run with the geometric mean unchanged. The arithmetic mean works at twelve at a factor $2$--$4$ on the
    best-learned processes; at $478$ the $2\to4$ plateaus at $10^{-2}$ will take most of the
    gradient once the needles are down, which is what the floor
    $\frac1P\sum_p\log(\mathrm{MSE}_p+\tau)$ (\code{training.loss\_aggregation\_tau}) is
    for, but at the bulk's scale it does nothing (Table~\ref{tab:agg_ab}) and its useful
    range, $\tau\in[10^{-3},1]$, is the threshold between ``done'' and ``still counts''.
    That is a hyperparameter, and the wave is a DyHPO sweep already, so $\tau$ is in its
    search space (\code{sweep/sweep\_config\_ccin2p3\_catalog\_v2.yaml}, log-uniform over
    that range; $\tau\to\infty$ is the arithmetic mean) rather than chosen by hand or swept
    at twelve processes, where there is no $2\to4$ bulk to protect. The reference-weighted
    aggregation is out: corrected, it is worse than the arithmetic mean at $1000$ steps at both
    learning rates (worst of the three at $5.8\times10^{-3}$ and with the off-shellness input,
    between the two means at $9.7\times10^{-3}$) and loses again at the quick working point
    (Table~\ref{tab:catv2_excess} and the quick-working-point paragraph). The aggregation choice and \code{training.lr} are swept
    together (their optimum is joint), and the wave needs the diagram sidecars present
    (masks $478/478$). Training-side knobs shape the gradient and must not move the
    metric: the validation aggregate is fixed separately
    (\code{training.val\_aggregation}, geometric mean) so \code{val\_loss\_no\_reg} stays
    comparable across arms, with the census (median and $90$th percentile by class) as the other read-out.
    The first two arms of Table~\ref{tab:agg_ab} predate this split, so their checkpoints
    were selected on their own training aggregate; the per-process numbers stand.
  \item \textbf{Open after the off-shellness arms.} (a) The range of the target drives the
    spread of final losses in every arm (Fig.~\ref{fig:loss_vs_range}); a fix at the level
    of the target's scale is the lead to try before more aggregation work. (b) The $W$ arm is
    read (the paragraph above); the sweep configs still carry \code{[23,6,25]}, and the
    run-to-run spread at $1000$ steps, needed to call its $13\%$ a cost, is unmeasured. (c) The reference weighting is
    settled at this working point (it loses to the arithmetic mean once its validation is
    recorded correctly; the quick-working-point paragraph), and the geometric and arithmetic
    means tie on the combined number there while differing in shape. The propagator subtraction on the
    target is in and adopted: the massive $s$-channel poles for every process and the
    massless $t$-channel exchange for the $2\to2$ processes (three seeds each, outside the
    band where it matters); the sign head is in, for a modest gain on the signed class.
    Open: whether to keep the $2\to2$-only $t$-channel rule, which was adopted on two of its three
    seeds (the third blew up; at its best checkpoint the arm is $0.091\pm0.12$ against $0.022$);
    the divergence at peak learning rate with the $t$-channel factor (two of nine
    runs; the arithmetic mean's exposure, to be settled with lr and aggregation swept
    jointly at this working point, and the geometric mean re-checked with the new targets);
    the signed class is still the worst by five, now a magnitude problem. The aggregation
    question of the joint-training gap is answered (Table~\ref{tab:steps_agg}): the aggregation
    sets the $2\to2$ and positive one-loop slopes and not the $2\to4$ one, so the
    exponent-deficit loss weight is not pursued. Open: on the full pools with every horizon
    tuned (Fig.~\ref{fig:steps_tuned}) the resonant $2\to2$ pools scale with $\alpha=0.39$
    (arithmetic) and $0.21$ (geometric) against $1.34$--$1.69$ alone, with validation equal to
    train, so the stall is optimisation or representation, not data; what the residual profile
    of these pools looks like at $4000$ steps is the first look. The solo references' HPO is
    thin (six trials, the same three start-up configurations in every sweep); their exponents
    carry that noise. The $\tau$ floor diverged once in three at $4000$ steps. Sharing across multiplicities costs $20$--$45\%$ at
    $1000$ steps on top; the structural options for the $2\to2$ classes (capacity or routing by
    class, a per-class fine-tune) are next now that the aggregation question is settled.
    Every 1000-step arm since the overhead fix is three seeds on the $5$k pools at the
    sweep's best HPs; \code{analysis/catalog\_v2/seed\_arms.py} is the read-out.
    Three $2\to4$ pools with neutrino pairs ($ee\to u\bar u\nu\bar\nu$, $ee\to d\bar d\nu\bar\nu$,
    $ee\to\nu\bar\nu\nu\bar\nu$) have numerically unstable outputs early in training
    (Sec.~\ref{sec:throughput}); worth a look as a struggler pattern. (d) The arithmetic mean diverged once on the
    small pools (non-finite gradient); one seed, undiagnosed. (e) Any comparison between arms finer than about $25\%$ needs
    repeats: the best arithmetic-mean trial rerun at its own HPs and seed ended at $0.068$
    against the trial's $0.054$ (GPU nondeterminism at $500$ steps), so three repeats per
    arm before the next A/B.
  \item The chunk cost model puts a one-loop $2\to3$ at $48$ LO-$2\to2$ units; the measured
    ratio is $100$--$200$, so those pools come out as single $5\times10^4$-event chunks and
    make the prebuild tail. The chunk policy is part of the recipe identity, so a recalibration
    regenerates every pool; do it together with the next recipe change, not alone.
  \item \textbf{The diagram encoder, once the pretraining is understood.} It is off
    (\code{model.use\_diagrams: false}): on the $448$-process big run removing it lowers the
    geometric mean by a third and the median by $4\%$ but raises the $90$th percentile from
    $0.085$ to $0.10$, one seed each (Table~\ref{tab:bigrun_arms}), and every catalog run so far
    trained without it. Once the joint-training gap of the catalog pretraining is explained,
    try it again on the catalog, three seeds, read on the same three statistics.
  \item \textbf{Seed-averaged references for the other classes.} The $2\to2$ references were
    rerun with three more seeds at their best HPs (Fig.~\ref{fig:solo16k_seeds}); the other seven
    references are still best-of-trials single runs, biased toward steep exponents and uncertain by
    $0.1$--$0.4$. The same three seeds per cell ($\sim20$ GPU-hours) turn the multiplicity comparison
    of Fig.~\ref{fig:alpha_mult_16k} into one with error bars.
  \item \textbf{The $ee\to\gamma\gamma$ floor.} Under the catalog setup $ee\to\gamma\gamma$ levels
    off at $1.5\times10^{-5}$ on either pool, where the from-scratch set reached $2.2\times10^{-7}$
    (Fig.~\ref{fig:solo_old_new}). One setup change at a time switched off on that process alone
    (the $t$-channel factors, the sign head, the added inputs, $4$ heads) says which one sets it.
\end{itemize}

\section{catalog\_v2, first pretraining: the census and what it exposes}\label{sec:catv2-census}
% ==========================================================================

The first DyHPO wave on \code{catalog\_v2} (three trials of the bigrun setup, Sec.~\ref{sec:bigrun},
with the two ablations adopted there, no diagram encoder and no MLP embedder
(Tab.~\ref{tab:bigrun_arms}), $8601$ steps at $\mathrm{bs}=16384$, lr $1.9$--$9.7\times10^{-3}$)
trains cleanly ($0.9$\,s per step, $2.9$--$3.2$\,h per trial with evaluation, $9$\,GB of VRAM;
objectives $3.1\times10^{-3}$, $8.9\times10^{-3}$, $9.8\times10^{-3}$ for lr $9.7$, $2.4$,
$1.9\times10^{-3}$) and is a diagnostic
of the catalog more than of the hyperparameters: the best trial (lr $9.7\times10^{-3}$, no EMA)
ends at a geometric-mean $\vlnr=3.1\times10^{-3}$ with a median per-process loss of
$5\times10^{-3}$ and a $90$th percentile of $0.12$ (Table~\ref{tab:catv2_census}), and the
worst-fitted processes are the same across the three trials ($38$ of the worst $48$ in all
three), so the tail is structural.

\begin{table}[H]
\centering
\caption{catalog\_v2 census (best wave-0 trial): median and $90$th percentile of the
per-process $\vlnr$ by catalog layer.}
\label{tab:catv2_census}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule
layer & processes & median / $90\%$ \\
\midrule
all & $478$ & $0.0052$ / $0.12$ \\
$2\to2$ trees & $35$ & $0.0006$ / $0.077$ \\
$2\to3$ trees & $82$ & $0.0019$ / $0.047$ \\
$2\to4$ trees & $185$ & $0.013$ / $0.081$ \\
one-loop QCD & $95$ & $0.0094$ / $0.33$ \\
loop-induced & $3$ & $0.0014$ / $0.0014$ \\
$\alpha_s$ scan & $46$ & $0.00085$ / $0.0038$ \\
$m_t$ scan & $12$ & $0.0007$ / $0.0018$ \\
$M_Z$ scan & $20$ & $0.078$ / $0.19$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{table}

The pools themselves are sound: $200$ stored events of three one-loop pools, including the
worst, recompute with MadLoop to machine precision, and the audit of Sec.~\ref{sec:catalog}
holds. Three mechanisms account for the failures, each isolated against the data.

\paragraph{The needle is smaller, not gone.} The $s$-channel $2\to2$ pools that lost the $Z$
resonance in the $448$ run (Prin.~\ref{prin:needle}) now train on the mixture and improve by
$30\times$ ($ee\,d\bar d$ $1.6\to0.05$, $ee\,b\bar b$ $1.6\to0.04$) but stop at
$0.04$--$0.09$; $ee\,\mu\mu$ sits at $0.09$, and the pure-$Z$ $ee\,\nu_\mu\bar\nu_\mu$ at
$0.56$, rising from $0.3$ over training. The $M_Z$ family is the same needle shifted
(median $0.08$, $90$th percentile $0.19$ over its $20$ pools). Shaping the training measure moved these from
``nothing learned'' to ``the bulk learned, the peak not''; the remaining error is the
resonance shape on the flat validation measure. Its cause is not the data but the loss
aggregation: the family learns alone, and it learns in the joint run under the arithmetic mean
(Sec.~\ref{sec:catalog}, Table~\ref{tab:agg_ab}).

\paragraph{Signed one-loop targets and the signed log.} $50$ of the $95$ one-loop pools of the
training recipe have a finite part that changes sign somewhere in the window, so they train
under the signed log; the $27$ with more than $5\%$ negative events ($2\to3$ with a gluon or
photon: $u\bar d\to e\nu g$ is $81\%$ negative, $u\bar u\to\mu\mu g$ $90\%$) fail as a class
(median $0.09$, losses up to $3.7$, i.e.\ worse than the constant). The cause is the transform: the signed
log $\mathrm{sgn}(x)\log(1+|x|)$ is linear below $|x|=1$, and these targets have a median
$|x|\sim10^{-5}$ over $28$ decades, so after standardization the target has a kurtosis of
$1000$--$3000$ with single events at $70$--$90\sigma$. Dividing by a per-dataset scale before
the log ($\mathrm{sgn}(x)\log(1+|x|/s)$, $s$ the median $|x|$ of the train pool, or a
hundredth of it) brings the kurtosis to $3$--$7$ and the largest event to $4$--$6\sigma$;
the other $68$ one-loop pools, with at most $5\%$ negative events ($45$ of them positive
throughout and on the plain log), have a median of $4\times10^{-3}$. This never showed before
because every earlier one-loop pool was positive. The scale is
now in: \code{data.signedlog\_quantile} (default $0.01$) makes $s$ the $1\%$ quantile of
$|x|$ on the train pool, carried in the transform string as \code{signedlog:<s>} so the
frozen statistics and the inverse read the same value; the per-process transforms are saved
and reloaded with the statistics, which they were not before (a frozen-stats reload of a
mixed catalog used one transform for every pool). Measured on the $51$ sign-changing pools
of the train cache, the recipe's $50$ plus one hold-out window
(\code{analysis/catalog\_v2/signed\_pools.py}): the standardized target's
kurtosis goes from a median of $188$ ($28$ pools above $100$, the worst $9700$) to $4.6$,
and the largest event from a median $31\sigma$ (up to $148\sigma$) to $4.8\sigma$ (up to
$12\sigma$). Four pools stay above $10$; two of them, $u\bar d\to Wg$ and $u\bar u\to Zg$ at
$36$ and $39$, have $1\%$ negative events against a narrow positive bulk, a bimodal target
no scalar transform flattens. Positive pools keep the plain log, so no earlier number
moves; what the scale does for the learning is for the re-measure wave to show. Two
comparability consequences: the $50$ signed pools' losses are on a new target scale, so
against wave 0 they compare only in rank, not by value; and the raw-space inverse,
which had clamped every amplitude at $30$ since the first commit, now clamps the exponent
instead, so raw-space metrics of old and new runs differ for large amplitudes (the
log-space losses every comparison uses are untouched).

\paragraph{The encoding does not tell $d$ from $s$ or $u$ from $c$.} $ds\to ds$ at one loop
ends at $\vlnr=1.58$, rising from $1.0$ during training (anti-learned), while $us\to us$ at
one loop is among the best ($6\times10^{-4}$) and its stripped target is the same function as
$ds\to ds$'s (correlation $1.000$ on the same momenta). Evaluating the $dd\to dd$ one-loop
target on the $ds\to ds$ validation momenta, standardized with $dd\to dd$'s train statistics,
gives an MSE of $1.580$: the model returns $dd\to dd$ for $ds\to ds$, because in the
property encoding $d$ and $s$ differ only in \code{log10\_mass\_gev} and the model ignores it,
so $dd\to dd$ ($t$- and $u$-channel) and $ds\to ds$ ($t$-channel only) are the same input with
two targets. Under the equivalence $u\equiv c$, $d\equiv s$, $e\equiv\mu\equiv\tau$ the $400$
base train entries fall on $368$ distinct inputs; the $27$ collisions split into harmless
same-function twins ($\mu\leftrightarrow\tau$ finals) and genuinely different functions that
differ by generation structure: the same-generation pairs with $W$ exchange against the
cross-generation ones without ($ud\to ud$ $0.10$ vs $us\to us$ $6\times10^{-4}$; $ud\to ud$,
$us\to us$, $cs\to cs$ at one loop $0.33/6\times10^{-4}/0.33$; $dd\to dd$ vs $ds\to ds$ at
one loop), plus their $+g$, $+\gamma$, $+gg$, $+\gamma\gamma$ towers. The v1 dedup rule treated
these relabels as the same function, which is true only for $s$-channel pair production. The
same rule makes several hold-outs untestable as designed: $ds\to ds$ and $cs\to cs$ (``relabels
of $us\to us$'') are seen as $dd\to dd$ and $ud\to ud$, whose functions differ; and the
one-loop layer, built from every coloured tree, put the loops of six hold-out trees into
training ($ds\to ds$, $cs\to cs$, $dd\to dd$, $ee\to s\bar sg$, $u\bar u\to\mu\mu g$,
$u\bar u\to\gamma\gamma$), the reverse of the order-axis hold-out.

The remaining waves of the sweep were cancelled after wave 0: the objective is dominated by
these three effects, none of which a hyperparameter fixes, and the catalog has to change
before the sweep means anything.

\paragraph{Fix: the table says what the generator says, and carries the generation.} Two
changes to the encoding (Def.~\ref{def:enc}). The property table's masses are now the
generator's, taken from the same constants the pipeline locks, with a test pinning them
(\code{tests/test\_particle\_encoding.py}); this also removes a silent inconsistency in the
scan machinery, which rebuilt final-state masses from the table. And a ninth column, the
fermion generation, one-hot encoded like spin and color (\code{data.generation\_onehot}),
gives $d$ versus $s$ and $u$ versus $c$ an input difference the data can support: under the
new table a $d$ and an $s$ row differ in exactly the generation columns and nothing else.
The projection from the property vector is the only layer whose input width changes, so
$\mu$P shapes and the transformer are untouched; earlier checkpoints cannot warm-start,
which costs nothing since the catalog run restarts. The catalog's colliding entries become
distinguishable inputs, so the dedup rule can stay; whether they should also be pruned, and
the hold-out fixes, remain open (Sec.~\ref{sec:catalog-open}).

\paragraph{Test of the fix at the catalog's scale.} Two runs of the wave-0 setup on the full
recipe, $1000$ steps, lr $9.7\times10^{-3}$: the generation one-hot against the encoding the
census ran with, reproduced by dropping the column (\code{data.generation\_feature: false};
the table's masses are already the generator's, so $d$ and $s$ rows are then identical). A
first attempt on a twelve-process recipe of the colliding families alone was uninformative
and is recorded here so it is not repeated: its ``off'' arm kept the generation as a scalar
column (only the one-hot form was switched), so both arms carried the flavour and both
learned every process to $10^{-4}$; and the per-process levers cannot be the cue at any
scale, since the off-shellness masks are built for $0$ of the processes here (no diagram
sidecars on this cluster) and the coupling and internal-mass scalars are equal for twins.
At the catalog's scale the baseline reproduces the census to three digits, $ds\to ds$ at one
loop at $1.57$, never below $1.0$ during training, and $ud\to ud$ on a plateau at $0.097$
from step $300$ on while $us\to us$ reaches $2\times10^{-4}$; with the one-hot, $ud\to ud$
learns to $2\times10^{-3}$ and $ds\to ds$ at one loop reaches $7\times10^{-3}$ at step
$550$, i.e.\ the function is representable and found, but at this learning rate and horizon
it does not hold it: it drifts back to $0.7$ over the last $400$ steps while $dd\to dd$ at one
loop settles at $4\times10^{-3}$, so the two twins still compete late in training. The
overall median moves the right way ($0.068$ against $0.076$). Whether the full $8601$-step horizon (and its lower final learning rate)
holds both twins is what the re-measure wave has to show. One more thing the baseline run
exposed: its evaluation aborted on the inverse of the signed log
(\code{AssertionError: signedlog failed}), the overflow of $\exp|y|$ on a prediction far
from the sign-changing pools' range, the same defect as the training-side one; the
per-dataset scale fixes both ends.

\paragraph{The signed-log scale and the aggregation at the catalog's scale.} The same
$1000$-step setup, generation one-hot throughout, run with the three fixes
(Table~\ref{tab:catv2_short}; median and $90$th percentile by class from the in-training
validation lines, \code{analysis/catalog\_v2/census.py}). The scale re-bases the signed pools'
target, so their losses compare across arms only in rank. Three readings. The aggregation
moves everything: under the arithmetic mean the median over all processes goes from $0.059$
to $0.038$ and the $90$th percentile from $0.32$ to $0.13$, every class but tree $2\to2$
improves, the $s$-channel family from $0.080$ to $0.036$, and the price is again the
best-learned tail, tree $2\to2$ at a median of $2.7\times10^{-4}$ under the geometric mean
against $3.0\times10^{-3}$; the twelve-process result (Sec.~\ref{sec:catalog},
Table~\ref{tab:agg_ab}) transfers to $478$ processes unchanged, and $\tau$ in the wave's
search space is the right place to set the trade. The signed-log scale does what it was
built for and no more: the standardized target is sane, the signed $2\to2$ pools learn
($cs\to cs$ at one loop $0.26\to0.025$, $u\bar u\to\gamma g$ $0.95\to0.23$, and to
$4\times10^{-3}$ and $1.5\times10^{-2}$ with the scale at the median), but the class as a whole stays far behind (median $0.31$ at the $1\%$ quantile, $0.25$ at the
median, against $0.024$ for the positive one-loop pools), and the worst pools under every scale
are the
one-loop $2\to3$ with a gluon or photon, all of which change sign, so at $1000$ steps their
failure reads as the difficulty of a one-loop function with a soft or collinear emission,
not as the transform; only the full horizon can separate the two. The scale stays at the
$1\%$ quantile (the median arm is not better overall, median $0.062$ against $0.059$, one seed). The
$M_Z$-shifted family stays hard under every arm (median $0.085$--$0.18$).

\paragraph{The same on the shaped measure.} Table~\ref{tab:catv2_short} is on the
uniform-$\sqrt s$ validation pools the model never trained on (Sec.~\ref{sec:catalog}, the
sampling policy). Rerun with validation and test drawn from the shaped measure
(Table~\ref{tab:catv2_short_smix}), train, validation and test losses agree per process
($ee\to\mu\mu$ under the geometric mean: $0.46$, $0.41$, $0.41$; before: $0.45$ against $0.12$),
and the census reads harder, as it must once the pole and the tails carry their training
weight in the metric: median $0.095$ against $0.059$, $90$th percentile $0.42$ against $0.32$,
and the $s$-channel family at a median of $0.24$ under the geometric mean, which is the needle
as it is. The histogram of a
resonant process shows why: the prediction never exceeds $\log A\approx-1.5$, where it
piles up, so the network emits one capped value for the whole pole region. The aggregation result survives the change of measure unchanged: the arithmetic mean lowers
the median of every class but tree $2\to2$ (all processes $0.045$ against $0.095$; signed
one-loop $0.11$ against $0.31$; positive one-loop $0.021$ against $0.059$) and costs the
best-learned tail (tree $2\to2$ at $4.1\times10^{-3}$ against $7.5\times10^{-4}$). Every catalog\_v2 number from here on is on this measure.

\paragraph{What fails: two mechanisms, told apart by the two arms.} Joining each
process's loss with its pool's dynamic range, the span of $\ln|\mathcal M|^2$ over the
train pool (Fig.~\ref{fig:loss_vs_range}; \code{analysis/catalog\_v2/loss\_vs\_range.py}),
sorts the catalog. The loss grows with the range under both aggregations and in every
multiplicity (the correlation of the log-loss with the range is $+0.4$ to $+0.6$ within the
$2\to2$, $2\to3$ and $2\to4$ processes alone): below a range of $10$ ($142$ processes) the median
is $0.015$ under the geometric mean and $0.014$ under the arithmetic one, between $10$ and $20$
($213$) $0.081$ and $0.037$, above $20$ ($123$) $0.21$ and $0.10$. The low-range processes with a
high loss under both are the resonances, $ee\to\mu\mu$ with a range of $9.5$ but a
$2.5$\,GeV pole in a $975$\,GeV window, and the $2\to4$ neutrino finals with internal $W$
and $Z$ poles. What the range measures is a pole or an infrared singularity in the window:
the mixture places $35\%$ of the training points there, the loss is dominated by that part,
and the model at this horizon does not resolve it (the prediction of a resonant process
never leaves a narrow band, Sec.~\ref{sec:catalog}). The second mechanism shows only in the
difference between the arms: starvation. Twelve processes end more than ten times worse
under the geometric mean than under the arithmetic one (none a hundred times), seven of them
with a range below $10$: $u\bar u\to t\bar t$ at one loop, with a range of $1$, sits at $0.96$
under the geometric mean and at $0.013$ under the arithmetic one; $ds\to ds$, $u\bar d\to Wg$,
$u\bar u\to b\bar b$ and $u\bar u\to Zg$ at one loop and the loop-induced $u\bar u\to HH$ are
the same. A process that does not fall in the first steps keeps a weight of order one while
the converged bulk reaches $10^4$, and it never trains, which is the run-to-run flakiness of
Prin.~\ref{prin:gradcomp} seen at the process level. So ``nothing works'' is two things: the
aggregation starves a dozen processes outright, and the sharp structure of the high-range
half of the catalog is not resolved at $1000$ steps in the joint run, although the $s$-channel
family resolves its pole alone.

\paragraph{Weighting by a reference, and width.} Two more $1000$-step arms
(Table~\ref{tab:catv2_excess}). The first replaces the current-loss weighting by a
reference: each process is scored as $m_p/L_{\rm ref}(n_p)$, with $L_{\rm ref}(n)$ the loss a
process of multiplicity $n$ reaches \emph{alone} on the compute this run gives every process
($1000$ steps at $\mathrm{bs}/P=34$ events per step; same multiplicity, same scaling, so one
number per multiplicity: $1.7\times10^{-3}$, $1.0\times10^{-2}$, $1.8\times10^{-2}$ from three
solo runs of two minutes each), the weights $1/L_{\rm ref}$ normalised to mean one
(\code{training.loss\_aggregation: excess}, \code{excess\_reference}; unnormalised, the loss
sits $250\times$ higher, the gradient is clipped on every step, median pre-clip norm $131$
against $0.65$, and the outputs blow up in the first fifty steps). The gradient weight of a
process is then set by its multiplicity, $2.9:0.48:0.27$, not by its own loss, so there is
neither the geometric mean's veto nor the arithmetic mean's domination by the largest raw
MSE.

\textbf{A bookkeeping error, and its correction.} Until commit \code{5a6a7b7} the
multi-process validation scored each process by running its batches through
\code{\_batch\_loss}, i.e.\ through the \emph{training} aggregation. For the mean and the
geometric mean of a single-process batch that is the process's mean loss; under the
reference weighting it is $m_p w_p$, the loss times the process's gradient weight, and the
combined value is $\mathrm{GM}_p(m_p w_p)=\mathrm{GM}(m)\,\mathrm{GM}(w)$ with
$\mathrm{GM}(w)=0.60$ at these weights. Every per-process number, census count, curve and
figure of the reference-weighted arms recorded before that commit carried the weight:
$2\to2$ losses $2.9\times$ too high, $2\to4$ losses $3.7\times$ too low. The weights are
known exactly from each run's references and multiplicities, so
\code{analysis/catalog\_v2/unweight\_excess.py} divides them back out of the logs
(\code{runs/\_unweighted/}); every reference-weighted number in this section is the corrected
one, and the earlier reading of these arms, that the weighting gave the best census through
a $2\to4$ gain at a $2\to2$ cost, was the weight and nothing else. The validation loop now
takes the plain per-event mean, and the single-pass validation
(Sec.~\ref{sec:throughput}) never went through the aggregator.

Corrected, the weighting does what its weights say. It puts $2.9\times$ the gradient on the
$2\to2$ class and a quarter on the $2\to4$ class, and the census follows, against the
arithmetic mean: tree $2\to2$ from a median of $4.0\times10^{-3}$ to $2.0\times10^{-3}$
($3.6$ to $3.0\times10^{-3}$ with the off-shellness input), while tree $2\to4$ goes from $0.062$
to $0.11$ ($0.051$ to $0.11$ with the input) and tree $2\to3$ from $0.027$ to $0.059$; over all
processes its median, $0.076$, sits between the arithmetic mean's $0.045$ and the geometric
mean's $0.095$ (Table~\ref{tab:catv2_excess}). Cutting a class's gradient share starves it;
concentrating the gradient on the $2\to2$ functions, already the best-learned, buys them a
factor of two at twice the loss on the classes that make up most of the catalog. The rerun at $5.8\times10^{-3}$ says the same (median $0.11$ against $0.054$ for the arithmetic mean and $0.098$ for the
geometric one), so it is the weighting, not the rate. The second arm
doubles the width at the same hyperparameters (sixteen heads, $\mu$P, arithmetic mean):
median $0.043$ against $0.045$, every class within noise, at twice the step time. The short
runs are not capacity-limited.

\paragraph{The pole is detected, not resolved, and the input meant to resolve it was never
there.} The amplitude histograms of the resonant $2\to2$ pools tell the same story in every
arm: the bulk is reproduced, and the whole pole plateau, $\log A$ from $-2$ to $+2$ and
$15\%$ of the events, is predicted as one narrow spike ($-1.6$ for $ee\to\mu\mu$, $-0.5$ for
$ee\to u\bar u$, $0$ for $ee\to b\bar b$) with an empty gap to the bulk. The network knows
an event sits on the resonance and emits one value for all of them; the pole's coordinate,
$(s-M_Z^2)/(M_Z\Gamma_Z)$, a $2.5$\,GeV feature in a $975$\,GeV window, $0.008$ of the
standardized input scale, is not resolved from the momenta at this horizon. That coordinate
is what the off-shellness lever supplies as an input, the flagship feature of the big run
(Table~\ref{tab:levers}, a $50\times$ gain on the resonant sets), and it was on in every
catalog\_v2 configuration; but its per-event masks are built from the diagram sidecars
(\code{data/diagrams/<process>.diagrams.json}, \code{tools/dump\_diagrams.py}), and none
existed on this cluster: every run's log says \code{offshell\_per\_event: built propagator
masks for 0/478 processes}. So every catalog\_v2 number above, wave 0 included, was taken
without the one input designed for the needle, and the ``needle as a multi-task effect''
reading stands only with that caveat: the family resolves its pole alone without the
input, and fails in the joint run without it.

\paragraph{With the input, the needle is gone.} The sidecars generated for the whole
registry (\code{tools/dump\_diagrams.py --all}, $424$ processes, three minutes of CPU) and
the three aggregation arms rerun with the masks built for $478/478$ processes
(Table~\ref{tab:catv2_offshell}): $ee\to\mu\mu$ goes from $0.41$ to $0.012$ under the
geometric mean and from $0.26$ to $0.012$ under the arithmetic one, the $s$-channel family
from a median of $0.24$ to $0.012$ and from $0.14$ to $0.0062$, the $M_Z$ family from $0.23$ to
$0.032$ and from $0.15$ to $0.020$, and the resonant $2\to2$ class under the arithmetic mean from
$0.14$ to $0.017$. What the family learned
alone in the probe it learned from thirty times the exposure; in the joint run it needed its
coordinate, and the coordinate was not there. The multi-task reading of the needle is
superseded: the needle was a missing input. What the input does not touch is as clear:
the starved processes under the geometric mean ($u\bar u\to t\bar t$ at one loop still at
$0.92$), the signed one-loop class (median $0.44$ under the geometric mean, $0.09$--$0.21$ under
the others) and most of the $2\to3$ and $2\to4$ sharp structure; those remain the
aggregation and horizon questions. The reference weighting with the input (corrected)
is at a median of $0.058$ against the arithmetic mean's $0.030$: its $s$-channel and $M_Z$
families come down as under the mean, but the $2\to3$ and $2\to4$ classes, at a quarter of the
gradient, are worse than under either other aggregation (tree $2\to4$ median $0.11$ against
$0.051$ and $0.079$).

\paragraph{The $W$ in the propagator list.} The $W$ (PDG $24$) was not in
\code{internal\_mass\_pdgs}, so the $W$-channel processes had no pole coordinate. The
arithmetic arm rerun with \code{[23,6,25,24]} (\code{runs/catalog\_short\_offshW2\_mean},
masks $478/478$; a first attempt ran with the $Z$ alone because \code{sbatch --export}
truncated the comma list) does for the $s$-channel $W$ what the $Z$ coordinate did for the
$Z$ pole: $u\bar d\to e\nu$ from $0.052$ to $0.011$, its one-loop twin from $0.035$ to
$0.011$, $u\bar d\to e\nu g$ and its $\alpha_s$ variants from $0.13$--$0.16$ to $0.03$--$0.04$.
Everything else moves little and mostly up: over the $478$ processes the geometric mean of
the ratio to the $Z$-only arm is $1.13$ ($337$ worse, $137$ better), over the $117$ processes with a $W$ in them $1.04$. One seed, $1000$ steps;
the run-to-run spread at this horizon is not measured, so the $13\%$ is not established as
a cost, while the factor of five on the resonant $W$ processes is well outside it. The
coordinate belongs in the list for the same reason the $Z$'s does.

\paragraph{Distance from the reference instead of a threshold.} A threshold on the loss
hides how far a process is from what it can do, so the better reading is each
process's distance from what its multiplicity reaches alone, $e_p=m_p/L_{\rm ref}(n_p)$ (\code{analysis/catalog\_v2/excess\_ratio.py},
Fig.~\ref{fig:excess_ratio}). The reference is the batch-$1024$ solo set of the steps curve
below: for each multiplicity the geometric mean over its two tree reference processes
($ee\to\gamma\gamma$ and $u\bar u\to u\bar u$; $ee\to u\bar ug$ and $u\bar d\to W^+ZZ$;
$ee\to u\bar ugg$ and $u\bar d\to W^+Z\gamma\gamma$), each at its sweep's best validation, at
$33$ steps, the grid point matched to the $1000$-step joint runs in events seen
($33\,800$ against the $38\,200$ a tree pool of the full-pool catalog gets; \code{solo\_b1k.tree\_reference}):
$L_{\rm ref}=0.045$, $0.12$ and $0.33$ for $2\to2$, $2\to3$, $2\to4$. Against it most processes
in the joint run are below what their multiplicity reaches alone on the same number of events:
$78\%$ under the geometric mean, $94\%$ under the arithmetic one, $85\%$ under the reference
weighting, with medians $0.26$, $0.18$ and $0.35$. The joint run takes $1000$ optimizer steps
where the solo run takes $33$ on the same events, and that is what the ratio below one measures.
By class: tree $2\to2$ at $0.014$, $0.08$ and $0.067$ times its reference, $2\to4$ at $0.24$,
$0.15$ and $0.33$, resonant $2\to2$ at $0.52$, $0.37$ and $0.44$, and the signed one-loop class
the one above it, at $4.4$, $0.83$ and $1.7$. Within the $2\to2$ processes the same two groups
stand out as before, since the reference is one number per multiplicity: the $W$-channel
processes ($u\bar d\to e\nu$, $u\bar d\to W\gamma$, $u\bar d\to Wg$ and their one-loop versions,
median $6.9$ under the geometric mean, $0.73$ under the arithmetic one), because the $W$ (PDG
$24$) is not in the propagator list the masks are built for, so these have no coordinate for
their pole; and the $M_Z$-scan variants (median $0.70$ and $0.43$, against $0.055$ and $0.088$
for the other scan families), with the mask verified to place the pole at the scanned mass and
the pools verified to cover it ($13$--$16\%$ of events within $5$\,GeV of their own pole), so
what remains is that a shifted pool is a twin of its nominal pool with the same momenta and a
different pole, told apart by the coordinate and the internal-mass scalar. The $W$ is being
added to the propagator list. Under the reference weighting the furthest processes are the
signed one-loop and the $2\to3$ pools ($u\bar u\to b\bar bH$ at $4.1$, $ee\to e\nu W$ at
$3.0$), the classes it starves. The reference is two processes per multiplicity, one number
for resonant and non-resonant pools alike, so the ratios by class carry the prefactor
differences within a multiplicity.
And across every arm the dominant driver of the spread of final losses is the range of the
target (Fig.~\ref{fig:loss_vs_range}), resonance being one way to have a large range; a fix
at the level of the target's scale, rather than of the aggregation, is the open lead.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\widerow{\pnl{../analysis/catalog_v2/excess_ratio_a.pdf}\hfill\pnl{../analysis/catalog_v2/excess_ratio_b.pdf}} \\[1ex]
\pnl{../analysis/catalog_v2/excess_ratio_c.pdf}
\caption{catalog\_v2 at $1000$ steps with the off-shellness input: each process's loss
divided by the solo loss of its multiplicity on the same number of events (batch-$1024$ references
at $33$ steps; dashed), against the range of
$\ln|\mathcal M|^2$ in its train pool, under the geometric mean (a), the arithmetic mean (b) and
the reference weighting (c, corrected).}
\label{fig:excess_ratio}
\end{figure}

\paragraph{The short runs are step-limited, not data-limited.} The same three arms with the
train pools cut to $5000$ events per process, so that $1000$ steps are seven passes over
the pool instead of a third of one: the geometric mean ends where it ends with the full
pools (median $0.056$ against $0.053$, $90$th percentile $0.44$ against $0.38$), the
reference weighting better (median $0.030$ against $0.058$, corrected), and in both the train and validation
losses are equal per process (ratio $1.0$ at the median, $1.1$ at the $90$th percentile),
so nothing has been fitted to the point of overfitting: at this compute the per-step
optimisation sets every number and the data is not reached, and $5000$ events are still far
from a saturating pool. The arithmetic arm on the small pools diverged (a non-finite
gradient early, pre-clip norms to $10^{11}$, every process at loss $1$ by the end; at its best
checkpoint, validation $8$ of $20$, the median is $0.12$ and the $90$th percentile $0.33$), where the
geometric arm on the same data ran normally; one seed, recorded as the arithmetic mean's
exposure to a single extreme standardized target, not diagnosed further.

\paragraph{The quick working point: the two aggregations swept.} To iterate faster the
working point moved to $500$ steps on the $5$k-event pools with the input on, and the
evaluation overhead was removed (Sec.~\ref{sec:throughput}; a trial is $8$ minutes of wall
time). Two DyHPO sweeps of $12$ trials in three waves, identical except for the training
aggregation (\code{sweeps/catalog\_q\_mean}, \code{sweeps/catalog\_q\_excess}), over lr in
$[1.4\times10^{-3},1.2\times10^{-2}]$, $\lambda$, warm-up, $\eta_{\min}$, EMA, and for the
weighted arm $\beta\in[0,2]$; the objective, checkpoint selection and this comparison are the
validation geometric mean in both. The references for the weighted arm are the best trials
of one small DyHPO per solo process at $\mathrm{bs}/P=34$ and $500$ steps
(\code{sweeps/ref\_q\_*}; lr over two decades, $\lambda$, warm-up): $4.3\times10^{-3}$,
$1.1\times10^{-2}$, $1.1\times10^{-2}$ for $2\to2$, $2\to3$, $2\to4$, within $15\%$ of the
fixed-lr solos, on a broad lr plateau. The arithmetic mean's best trial reaches $0.054$
(lr $9.7\times10^{-3}$, $\lambda=3.8\times10^{-8}$, warm-up $0.17$, no EMA; the five best
trials all at lr $8$--$11\times10^{-3}$), the weighted arm's $0.068$ (lr $1.1\times10^{-2}$,
$\beta=0.05$); every weighted trial with $\beta>1$ is at $0.17$ or worse. With the
validation recorded correctly the weighting loses to the arithmetic mean at this working
point too, and the emphasis $\beta$ only makes it worse. The geometric mean swept the
same way (\code{sweeps/catalog\_q\_geo}) reaches $0.053$ (lr $1.2\times10^{-2}$, the top of
the window, EMA on), a tie with the arithmetic mean on the combined number. The tie is
inside the run-to-run spread: the arithmetic mean's best trial rerun at its own HPs and
seed ended at $0.068$ against $0.054$, a $25\%$ spread from GPU nondeterminism at this
horizon, so no single-run difference of that size means anything. Where the two differ is
the shape of the census (\code{analysis/catalog\_v2/arms\_compare.py},
Fig.~\ref{fig:arms_compare_q}): the geometric mean has $27$ processes below $10^{-3}$ and
$50$ stuck above $0.5$, the starvation of Prin.~\ref{prin:gradcomp} in its bimodal form,
where the arithmetic mean has none below $10^{-3}$ and one above $0.5$. Read as the
distance from the tuned solo loss of each multiplicity (Table~\ref{tab:catv2_q_classes}; no
pass/fail line, which says nothing about how far a process is from what it can do): the
tree $2\to2$ class is the one place where joint training beats solo, and only under the geometric
mean ($0.39\times$ solo at the median, its converged tail); the resonant $2\to2$ pools sit
at $9$--$11\times$ solo under every arm, the $2\to4$ class at $8$--$11\times$, the signed
one-loop class at $19\times$ under the arithmetic mean and $82\times$ under the geometric
one. The weighting is worst on the $2\to3$ class ($6.2\times$ against $3.4$). The
arithmetic mean has the lowest $90$th percentile in every class but the two $2\to2$ ones.
