\subsection{Proposed, not run: one-loop synthetic amplitudes}\label{sec:syn-loops}
Any one-loop amplitude decomposes into a few universal scalar integrals with process-dependent coefficients,
\begin{equation*}
  \mathcal M^{(1)}=\sum d_i\,D_0^{(i)}+\sum c_i\,C_0^{(i)}+\sum b_i\,B_0^{(i)}+\sum a_i\,A_0^{(i)}+\mathcal R ,
\end{equation*}
the boxes $D_0$, triangles $C_0$, bubbles $B_0$ and tadpoles $A_0$, plus a rational term $\mathcal R$; all the loop
structure (logarithms, double logarithms, thresholds at $s=(m_1+m_2)^2$ with their $\sqrt{1-4m^2/s}$ behaviour,
imaginary parts above them) sits in the integrals, which are known functions. A synthetic one-loop amplitude keeps that
split: coefficients drawn like the tree numerators (random polynomials, dimension fixed by the same count), integrals
evaluated exactly. To keep it cheap and tied to the tree, the integrals are placed on the tree's own channels: a bubble
on each line's $s_S$ with that line's mass, triangles and boxes on adjacent pairs and triples of channels. Tadpoles and
bubbles have short closed forms, and massless or one-mass triangles and boxes do too; general masses would go through
a one-loop library on CPU (not timed here; such libraries are built for per-event use). The target follows the real one-loop pools: the
$\alpha_s$-stripped tree--loop interference $2\,\mathrm{Re}(\mathcal M^{(0)*}\mathcal M^{(1)})$
(Def.~\ref{def:coup}), so it is signed like them. The cost stays far below MadLoop's, which needs a generation, a
compile and a certification per process.

\subsection{What it costs to generate}\label{sec:syn-cost}
Per core, through \code{build\_mixture\_dataset} with the mixture policy (\code{tools/bench\_synthetic.py},
$2\times10^4$ stored events per process, a CC-IN2P3 login node, Intel Xeon E5-2640 v3):
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lcc}
\hline
 & synthetic, stored events/s & real (Sec.~\ref{sec:catalog}, \code{htc} nodes), stored events/s \\
\hline
$2\to2$ & $77$--$99\times10^3$ (median $86\times10^3$) & LO trees $2$--$15\times10^3$ \\
$2\to3$ & $44$--$59\times10^3$ (median $48\times10^3$) & one-loop $2\to2$, massive $2\to3$: $150$--$800$ \\
$2\to4$ & $27$--$50\times10^3$ (median $39\times10^3$) & gluonic one-loop $2\to3$: $50$--$100$ \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The two columns were measured on different machines and the real one spans multiplicities, so the ratio is indicative:
$\sim5$--$40\times$ faster than real trees, $10^2$--$10^3\times$ faster than one-loop processes. The labeller is only
$24$--$42\%$ of the synthetic time; the rest is the shared phase-space sampler, so the synthetic rate is near the
sampler's own ceiling. On top of the rate, a real process needs a MadGraph output and a backend compile before its
first event ($432$ compiles for the $478$-entry catalog; their time was never recorded separately), and a one-loop one
its certification; a synthetic process needs neither.

What this would mean for larger synthetic catalogs (the hand-off's larger catalogs are real processes first, the user's
call of 2026-10-08): $10^3$ processes at $1.2\times10^5$ events each are $1.2\times10^8$ events, at $\sim5\times10^4$ per core-second about
$0.7$ core-hours, minutes on one node. The real catalog took about six CPU-hours plus the compiles for $478$ entries,
also small next to a $64$k-step pretraining (about $7$ GPU-hours). So at these sizes generation speed is not what
decides: neither side's generation is the bottleneck. What the synthetic prior buys is the \emph{number of distinct
processes}: the real catalog has the $478$ processes MadGraph was run on (and fewer once equal processes are merged),
so $10^3$ real processes would mean defining, generating and certifying several hundred new ones, while $10^3$ (or
$10^5$) synthetic ones are a range of names. Whether that is worth anything depends on transfer growing with the
synthetic catalog, which is what the larger-catalog item measures.

\par\smallskip\noindent \emph{Proposed, not run: multiplicities MadGraph cannot reach.} The synthetic prior is not tied to the real catalog's
processes or sizes. A real tree's cost grows with its diagram count, faster than exponentially in the number of gluons ($gg\to n\,g$
has $4$, $25$, $220$, $2485$ diagrams for $n=2$ to $5$), and multi-gluon $2\to6$ and beyond is where we expect standalone MadGraph
outputs, compiles and evaluations to become impractical (not measured here). The synthetic cost per event is about $K\times|\Pi|$ evaluations of
Eq.~\eqref{eq:syn}, independent of how many diagrams a real process would have; the permutation average is its only
factorial term, and a cheaper symmetrisation is proposed with Eq.~\eqref{eq:syn}. Pretraining on synthetic processes
far above the real catalog's $2\to4$, and fine-tuning on the highest real multiplicities we have, would test whether
transfer reaches where real data is scarcest.

\section{Training throughput}\label{sec:throughput}
% ==========================================================================

The runs below are on V100~32GB (Jean-Zay \code{gpu\_p2}, account \code{itg@v100}); A100 was then
unavailable to the account (since 2026-10-02 the \code{itg@a100} allocation runs this project, with TF32 A/B'd:
Sec.~\ref{sec:tf32-a100}), which matters below. A per-step optimization campaign attacked two
distinct regimes, each fast path proven numerically equal to the original by CPU equivalence
guards (\code{tests/test\_amp.py}, Section~0).

\begin{principle}[The step was data-bound before it was compute-bound]\label{prin:dataloader}
At \code{num\_workers=0}, profiling showed dataloading was $69\%$ ($260$\,ms) of a $380$\,ms
step: $8192$ serial \code{\_\_getitem\_\_} calls plus a collate concatenation on the main
thread with the GPU idle. Moving to \code{num\_workers=2} with pinned memory and a
\code{non\_blocking} host$\to$device copy overlaps the load under compute and takes the step
from $0.356$ to $0.128$\,s/iter ($2.78\times$), landing on the $\sim0.119$\,s compute floor.
This is now the default (\code{num\_workers: 2}); $nw{=}4$ is no better, $nw{=}6$ oversubscribes
the 8 CPUs and hangs.
\end{principle}

The compute floor was then lowered on two fronts. \emph{Vectorizing the on-GPU hot path} (
per-event mean pooling, the block-diagonal attention mask (built once per forward, not per
block), the per-process loss (a single segment-mean, not a Python loop over processes), the
$L_2/L_1$ regularizer (one fused \code{\_foreach}), and broadcasting per-particle local frames
across heads instead of materializing a per-head copy) gives a combined $\sim3.1\times$ at
$nw{=}0$ (dominated by the pooling rewrite, $3.08\times$ alone). A \emph{fused Adam}
(\code{fused\_optimizer}, default on) adds $1.34\times$; \emph{deferring host$\leftrightarrow$device
syncs} (collapsing $\sim4$ syncs/step into one fused \code{stack(\dots).tolist()}, with the NaN
guard moved onto the already-synced grad-norm) adds a further $\sim2\%$, small precisely
because, after Principle~\ref{prin:dataloader}, the step is no longer GPU-bound. Each vectorized
path is toggleable (\code{LLOCA\_*} env vars) with the original retained for A/B, and each is
\code{allclose}-verified against it.

\paragraph{The evaluation overhead at many processes.} A $1000$-step run on the
$478$-pool catalog with $5$k-event pools took $30$ minutes of wall time for $7.5$ minutes of
steps. The rest was the evaluation: every validation iterated $478$ per-process loaders whose
batch is capped at half a process's pool ($1000$ events), so a pass was $956$ forward calls
with a sync each ($32$\,s; twenty of them, $11$ minutes), and the post-training pass did the
same over three splits ($2900$ calls, $95$\,s) before $478$ per-process histogram pages
($113$\,s). Three changes (commits 43c18bf, 162393c): the multi-process validation and the
evaluation make one pass per split over the combined loader and accumulate the per-process
means by \code{index\_add\_} on the process ids, as the training loss does; the dataset
gained a vectorized batch fetch (\code{\_\_getitems\_\_}, one gather from the flat arrays per
batch instead of $16384$ item calls and a $16384$-slice \code{cat}); and
\code{plotting.per\_process\_pages} caps the histogram pages. A split's collection went from
$33$\,s to $8.5$\,s (the fetch was the cost, not the GPU: with \code{workers=0} the
per-item collate of a $16384$-event batch ran in the main process at $0.5$\,s a batch); the
training step is unchanged at $0.43$\,s, its two workers already hid the fetch. The two
validation paths were run on the same model (\code{LLOCA\_VAL=check}, both fetch modes): $475$
of $478$ per-process losses agree to $2\times10^{-4}$ or better; the three that do not
($ee\to u\bar u\nu\bar\nu$, $ee\to d\bar d\nu\bar\nu$, $ee\to\nu\bar\nu\nu\bar\nu$) sit at losses of
$30$--$850$ twenty steps into training and move by factors of three between two validations
of the same model on either path, so their outputs are numerically unstable at that stage
and rounding differences between a mixed and a single-process batch are amplified (the
same three, and only they, deviate with the combined batches cut to the loop's $1000$
events, so it is the composition of the batch, not its size, acting on near-divergent
outputs); not a property of the validation code, and worth a look as a struggler pattern
of its own. The
per-item fetch and the per-process loop stay behind \code{LLOCA\_FETCH=item} and
\code{LLOCA\_VAL=loop}. A $500$-step trial on the $5$k pools now takes about $6$ minutes of
wall time.

\begin{remark}[Carried but inert, and deliberately declined]
\code{allow\_tf32} is carried (default on) but is a \textbf{no-op on V100}; it would give
$\sim2\times$ matmul on A100 at $\sim10^{-3}$ precision, so it must be loss-A/B'd on A100 before
it is trusted (done for the transfer study on 2026-10-02: TF32 on and off agree within the run-to-run spread,
Sec.~\ref{sec:tf32-a100}). Two optimizations were declined on purpose:
converting the $L_2$ penalty to decoupled \code{weight\_decay} (it is folded into $\vlnr$,
checkpoint selection, and the HPO objective, and $\lambda$ is a tuned sweep HP, so converting it
would silently break continuity with all existing sweep results), and \code{torch.compile} (the
compute is dominated by the torch-geometric framesnet and xformers attention, neither of which
compiles, so it buys heavy graph breaks for no gain; and it would prefix \code{state\_dict} keys,
breaking warm-start/fine-tune reloads). A gradient-checkpointing knob
(\code{checkpoint\_blocks}) trades $\sim$one extra forward for a large activation-memory saving
when memory-bound.
\end{remark}

\paragraph{TF32 on A100 (2026-10-02).}\label{sec:tf32-a100} Jean Zay's \code{itg@v100} was over-consumed (fair share
$0.08$, jobs waiting $\sim6.5$\,h with hundreds of V100s free), so its jobs moved to \code{itg@a100} (\code{gpu\_p5}, A100
80GB) with TF32 on. Two fixed-HP cells at $D=10^3$ (\code{tp3\_calib}, $8$k steps): $ee\to d\bar d$ $7.2\times10^{-5}$ on
V100, $5.7\times10^{-5}$ on A100 with TF32 off, $5.1\times10^{-5}$ with it on; the one-loop $ee\to b\bar b$
$1.6\times10^{-3}$, $4.18\times10^{-3}$, $4.18\times10^{-3}$ (test $6.4$, $6.5$, $8.8\times10^{-3}$). TF32 changes nothing
beyond the run-to-run spread, which is larger between the two GPUs. A step is $\sim20\%$ faster than on V100 (these runs
are data-bound). Transfer cells from then on mix V100 and A100 trials. One run per setting.

\paragraph{The $\Delta$ plots (D42, D64; 2026-10-14).} For the paper draft (Butter, Plehn): arXiv:2603.22407's
$\Delta=(A_\theta-A)/A$ as $|\Delta|$ histograms on log bins per process, for every ladder rung, the scaling figures'
arms and the star arms, from the $8$k grid at $D=10^4$ (\code{analysis/transfer/delta\_audit.json}). As first
collected, $179$ runs: $107$ on Jean Zay, $37$ on CC, $32$ on lxplus (copied to CC, \code{runs\_from\_lxplus/<sweep>/}),
and three pruned trials rerun on CC from their configs (\code{tp3\_finfte\_uubar\_Zggg\_d8} trial $96$,
\code{tp3\_r1fte\_udbar\_enu\_d8} $197$, \code{tp3\_r1fte\_ee\_dd\_ew\_nlo\_d8} $98$; their parent
\code{runs/tp3\_ladder\_r1/trial\_0047} md5-identical to lxplus's); later extended to the $251$ models of the evaluation-precision paragraph below. \code{tools/eval\_delta.py} rebuilds a run with its own
frozen \code{data\_stats.json} and tokenizer as a resume reads them, the fine-tune head kept, and writes its test
predictions (\code{--tf32 on|off}); \code{analysis/transfer/collect\_delta.py} collects the test $\Delta$ and the rebuilt
validation loss, and \code{delta\_plots.py} draws the figures and flags a rebuild more than $5\%$ off its recorded loss.
All but one of those off (\code{tp3\_r9fte\_ee\_WW\_d8\_002} trial $32$, unexplained) were traced to the evaluation precision and to the checkpoint's EMA weights (the two paragraphs below). Ruled
out before that: the Lorentz-frame seed (four digits over seeds $1$, $2$, $3$, $42$), the tokenizer, pools, sidecars and
recipes (older than the runs), the checkpoint step.

\paragraph{TF32 at evaluation (2026-10-15).}\label{sec:tf32-eval} The training-side A/B above does not cover evaluating a
model at a precision other than the one it was trained at, and that is not harmless. Each of the $107$ Jean Zay runs in
the $\Delta$ plots was rebuilt on A100 twice (\code{tools/eval\_delta.py --tf32 on|off}) and its rebuilt validation loss
compared with the recorded one (\code{analysis/transfer/tf32\_eval.py}, Fig.~\ref{fig:tf32_eval}; the best
checkpoint's EMA weights, sec:ema-ckpt): $59$ match within $0.02$\,dex either way, $28$ only with TF32 on, $20$ only with
it off, none with neither. At the wrong precision the error
is up to $133\times$ larger (\code{tp3\_r3fte\_uubar\_gg\_d8/trial\_0031}, trained on V100, rebuilt with TF32 on), and a
TF32-trained model rebuilt in fp32 is up to $4\times$ better (\code{tp3\_r6fte\_ud\_ud\_d8/trial\_0194}). TF32's $10$-bit
mantissa is a relative error of $\sim10^{-3}$ per matmul, against $|\Delta|\sim10^{-5}$ to $10^{-4}$ in these models,
so a model's precision is part of what it learned. The $\Delta$ plots draw each model at the precision that reproduces
its recorded loss. On H100 the same holds (\code{FA-ccin2p3-c0a4a22d}, TF32 on, the five CC runs that V100 did not reproduce):
\code{tp3\_finfte\_ud\_ud\_d8/trial\_0175} rebuilds at $1.00\times$ its recorded loss (V100: $0.29\times$),
\code{tp3\_finfte\_uubar\_Zg\_d8/trial\_0031} at $1.00\times$ (V100: $1.52\times$), and the lxplus-trained
\code{tp3\_r9fte\_ee\_ddbar\_d8\_002/trial\_0060} matches only in fp32 ($7.3\times$ with TF32). With each model at its
own precision and its EMA weights, $10$ of the $251$ $\Delta$-plot models at $D=10^4$ still rebuilt off their recorded
loss by more than $0.02$\,dex after the V100 and A100 rebuilds. Nine of them were rebuilt on H100 at both precisions
(2026-10-23, \code{FA-ccin2p3-6030a6a3} TF32 on, \code{FA-ccin2p3-c2518adc} off; \code{analysis/transfer/delta\_runs\_off10.txt},
\code{tf32\_h100\_off10.json}): with TF32 on, eight reproduce their recorded loss to $0.4$ to $4\%$ ($0.993$ to
$1.040\times$), among them \code{tp3\_ssudakovewfte\_ee\_ddbar\_d8/trial\_0132}, which is $0.34\times$ in fp32 on H100 as on
V100: it was trained on H100 with TF32, and that settles it. With TF32 off the same eight are $0.34$ to $1.52\times$. The
ninth, the lxplus-trained \code{tp3\_r9fte\_ee\_WW\_d8\_002/trial\_0032}, rebuilds at $1.06\times$ at both precisions
($0.026$\,dex), on no hardware we have tried; it is the one model of the $251$ drawn dotted in the $\Delta$ figures,
which are redrawn with each model at the precision that reproduces its loss (\code{delta\_plots.pick}). Not settled: whether
the study's V100/A100/H100 mix moves its cell values beyond the run-to-run spread (the training-side A/B says not, at
two cells). 

\paragraph{The best checkpoint's \code{"model"} weights are not the EMA weights (2026-10-15).}\label{sec:ema-ckpt}
The counts above are with the EMA weights (redone after this finding; the first count, with the live weights, had $5$
matching at neither precision). \code{base\_experiment.\_save\_model} takes
\code{state\_dict()} inside \code{ema.average\_parameters()} but saves it after the block ends; the state dict shares
storage with the parameters, which the EMA restores to the live weights, so for every run with \code{ema: true} the
checkpoint's \code{"model"} entry is the live weights at the best step, not the EMA average that validation scored (the
\code{"ema"} entry still holds that average). Checked on \code{tp3\_synfte\_ee\_bb\_nlo\_d8\_lad}: \code{"model"} equals
the live weights to $0$, and differs from the EMA shadow by up to $5.8\times10^{-3}$. Rebuilt with the shadow
(\code{tools/rebuild\_run.load\_best\_weights}, commit \code{570c5cc}; \code{FA-ccin2p3-500d70ed}, V100):
\code{tp3\_synfte\_ee\_bb\_nlo\_d8\_lad} $1.000\times$ its recorded loss (was $13\times$) and
\code{tp3\_ssudakovewfte\_ee\_dd\_ew\_nlo\_d8/trial\_0131} $1.000\times$ (was $7.2\times$). The \emph{recorded} values (the
scaling figures, every sweep's ranking) are the EMA weights' and are right. Wrong were: every rebuild of an EMA run (the
$\Delta$ plots, the counts of Fig.~\ref{fig:tf32_eval}, the MSE baseline of the het A/B: \code{trial\_0144} has EMA on),
being redone with the fixed tool (\code{FA-ccin2p3-3eacf1a4}, $122$ CC runs on V100; Jean Zay's $107$ next); the
post-training evaluation of every EMA run, which loads the best live weights and then swaps in the \emph{final} EMA
shadow (\code{experiment.py} \code{evaluate}), so its ``MSE (prepd)'' lines, test metrics and \code{plots\_0} are the
final EMA model's (in the synfte run exactly the step-$8000$ value); and every fine-tune of an EMA parent starts from the
parent's live weights at its best step (\code{base\_experiment.py:1203}), not from the model whose loss the parent
reports. The best checkpoint of a run whose best step sits late in the cosine schedule has live $\approx$ EMA weights,
which is why most rebuilds matched; the four far off had their best step on an unstable stretch.
\code{tp3\_ssudakovewfte\_ee\_ddbar\_d8/trial\_0132} ($0.34\times$) has EMA off; it was trained on H100 with TF32 and
reproduces there (par.~\ref{sec:tf32-eval}). Fixed since (D63, 2026-10-15, commits \code{cd26a58} to \code{cff8039}): with
EMA on, the checkpoint's \code{"model"} is the EMA weights and \code{"model\_live"} the live ones (\code{"model\_is\_ema"}
marks the format); the post-training evaluation scores the best checkpoint's EMA; a fine-tune starts from the parent's
EMA weights, an old checkpoint's through its EMA shadow mapped by parameter name (\code{tests/test\_ema\_checkpoint.py},
real-model check $3/3$). Fine-tunes of EMA parents launched since start from other weights than the transfer study's
earlier ones.

\begin{figure}[H]
\centering
\includegraphics{../analysis/transfer/figs/tf32_eval.pdf}
\caption{Jean Zay's $107$ $\Delta$-plot runs rebuilt on A100 with TF32 matmuls on and off, each divided by the run's
recorded validation MSE$(\log|\mathcal M|^2)$.}
\label{fig:tf32_eval}
\end{figure}

% ==========================================================================
\section{The compute envelope: VRAM and step time}\label{sec:envelope}
% ==========================================================================

Because Jean-Zay bills a fixed GPU-hour budget, each job should run near the hardware ceiling.
Two quantities bound the operating point on the V100: peak VRAM (limiting batch size and width)
and step time (limiting how many updates fit in an allocation). The standard training batch is
therefore the largest that fits, $\mathrm{bs}=16384$ (at $\nh\!\le\!8$ on the 32\,GB V100);
for the many-process joint runs this is a statistical requirement as well as a throughput one:
at $448$ datasets and a per-dataset geometric-mean loss, $\mathrm{bs}=16384$ gives
$\sim\!36$ events/dataset per batch, where $\mathrm{bs}=1024$ would give only $\sim\!2$ and the
per-dataset MSEs (hence their log-mean) would be noise. With batch size $\mathrm{bs}$,
particles per event $n$, and width $\nh$ ($d=16\nh$), peak VRAM is bilinear,
\begin{equation}
  V_{\text{peak}}(\mathrm{bs},n,\nh) \approx a(\nh)\,\mathrm{bs}\,n + b(\nh)\,\mathrm{bs} + C(\nh)
  \quad[\mathrm{MB}],
  \label{eq:vram}
\end{equation}
with the activation coefficient $a\propto\nh$ and the fixed term $C\propto\nh^2$ (parameters
plus the two AdamW moment buffers, $\approx3\times$ params in float32; the EMA shadow adds one
more params-sized copy). Below $\mathrm{bs}=2048$ the widest tested model ($\nh=64$) fits for
all $n\le12$ on 16\,GB; at a practical $\mathrm{bs}=4096$ one fits $\nh=32$ for $n\le8$. Step
time is set by the MLP ($\mathrm{FLOPs}_{\text{MLP}}\propto n\,d^2$ dominates attention's
$n^2 d$ for $n\lesssim12$): it is linear in $n$ once the GPU is saturated
($\mathrm{bs}\gtrsim2048$) and grows as $t\propto\nh^{1.1}$, so throughput scales roughly as
$1/\nh$.

% ==========================================================================
\section{Methodological principles}\label{sec:principles}
% ==========================================================================

The results above impose a concrete protocol on how sweeps, HPO, and comparisons are run.

\begin{principle} For model comparisons through \S\ref{sec:bigrun}, compare on $\vlnr$, the
best \emph{non-regularized} validation loss on
log-amplitudes (Eq.~\ref{eq:vlnr}), never the regularized tracked loss, since $\lambda$ is a
tuned HP. Re-base for preprocessing before comparing two sides (Principle~\ref{prin:perproc}).
The transfer compute-scans and the divergence studies use their own, named metrics
(Def.~\ref{def:metric}); do
not compare their numbers against $\vlnr$.
\end{principle}
\begin{principle} Never re-sweep $\eta$ across width: $\mu$P transfers it. Tune once at the
smallest width and reuse for all larger widths, up to a $5\times$ cut on a width-scaling grid.
\end{principle}
\begin{principle} Centre the $\eta$ search by evaluating the measured 2D surface
$\eta^\ast(t,D)$ at your \emph{own} $(t,D)$: the inverted-$U$ $\eta_c(t)=10^{-2}\min[(t/3000)^{0.5},(t/3000)^{-0.55}]$
(Eq.~\ref{eq:invU}) lifted onto the high-$D$ row of the grid, and sweep only $\pm\tfrac12$
decade, not a flat 4-decade prior and not the $t$-only $\eta_c(t)$. Every real run sits past the
peak ($t\gg\tstar=3$k), so the centre \emph{decreases} with horizon; a large-$D$ foundation run
lives in the (high-$D$, high-$t$) corner the grid does not cover, so its centre is an
extrapolation of the high-$D$ decay ($\approx$2, 1.4, $0.9\times10^{-3}$ at $t=$30, 50, 100k for
per-process $D\!\approx\!70$k) and the sweep both centres and measures it. Do not hold $\eta$ at
the $\tstar$ peak, and do not discard $D$.
\end{principle}
\begin{principle} Narrow the secondary knobs to where optima actually land
(Table~\ref{tab:searchspace}): cap \code{regularization\_lambda} at $10^{-6}$, fix
\code{cosanneal\_eta\_min}$\approx10^{-8}$, keep \code{warmup\_frac}$\in[0.05,0.2]$, and drop the
exotic sampler variants ($\abs\rho\approx0.1$, noise). Together with the $\eta$ window this
shrinks the search volume $10$--$100\times$, so halve the trial budget at equal resolution or
explore far more densely at equal budget.
\end{principle}
\begin{principle} Finetune from a pretrain-tuned config: \code{lr\_scale}$\in[0.1,10]$ centred at
$1$ (best finetune $\eta\approx$ pretrain $\eta$), \code{layer\_decay}$\in[0.75,1.0]$; same
reg/warmup/$\eta_{\min}$ rules.
\end{principle}
\begin{principle} Seed new sweep cells from the law, not a flat prior: $\eta_c(t)$ is smooth in
$t$ and width-invariant, so a good rate at one horizon predicts its neighbours. Do \emph{not}
assume batch/data-size invariance; those axes were never cleanly isolated here.
\end{principle}
\begin{principle} Submit multiple sweeps \emph{interleaved} (round-robin), not all-at-once: DyHPO
trials only inform siblings that \code{observe} before they \code{suggest}, so a simultaneous
launch suggests against a stale surrogate (Def.~\ref{def:dyhpo}). When there are not enough
concurrent jobs for interleaving to serialize anything (a lone sweep, or a moment of abundant
free GPUs), fall back to \textbf{sequential batches}: split the trials into (by default) three
dependency-chained waves, so each wave \code{observe}s before the next \code{suggest}s and the
single-fidelity optimizer always has data to fit (Rem.~\ref{rem:dyhpo}).
\end{principle}
\begin{principle} A/B fairly and cheaply: reuse the existing baseline (the best of its own short
HPO sweep, never a single arbitrary run); first try the feature at the baseline's best HPs and
stop if it already wins; only if it does not, sweep the feature with the same HPO and compare
best-vs-best on $\vlnr$, re-basing for any preprocessing difference first.
\end{principle}
